[x] [20.1] LOW — FIXED — validateRedemption's two subjects are fetched independently
File:   src/modules/loyalty/redemptionValidator.ts:23-51
Now:    The reward and the account are each fetched by id with no relation
        between them asserted.
Impact: Folded into 20.20 for exploitability; recorded separately because it is
        the validator — the one function whose entire job is to decide whether a
        redemption is allowed — and "do these two things belong together" is a
        precondition it never establishes.
Pinned: redemptionValidator.test.ts — "never checks that the reward and the
        account share a program"
Fixed:  Exactly that. The account is resolved FIRST and the reward is then
        looked up with `findFirst({ where: { id, programId: customer.programId } })`,
        so a reward in another program reads as absent — which is what it is from
        this account's point of view. Resolving the account first also means a
        request naming an account that does not exist never reaches the reward
        lookup at all.

File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/settings.service.ts:525-537 (getAllEventTriggers)

        src/modules/loyalty/settings/settings.service.ts:44, :61, :458 (command CRUD)
Now:    getAllEventTriggers takes an OPTIONAL programId and builds
        `where: programId ? { programId } : undefined`. Prisma reads an undefined
        where as "no filter". The handler passes `request.query.programId`
        straight through, and that query parameter is optional.
        createEventTriggerHandler never resolves the caller's program either — it
        writes whatever `programId` the request body carries.
        getLoyaltyWaCommand, updateLoyaltyWaCommand and deleteLoyaltyWaCommandById
        are likewise keyed on the command id alone, while the list and the
        by-type lookup beside them are org-scoped.
Impact: `GET /api/loyalty/settings/event-triggers` with no programId returns
        EVERY organisation's event triggers, each with its `program` relation
        included — so the response carries other orgs' programme ids, names and
        WhatsApp settings ids. It is a one-request enumeration of the whole
        tenant list, from an ordinary authenticated session.
        Writing is the mirror: a trigger created against another org's programId
        will fire on that org's customers, sending whatever template it names.
Pinned: settings.service.test.ts — "returns every org's triggers when no program
        is supplied", "writes whatever programId it is given", "fetch, update and
        delete are keyed on the id alone"
        loyalty.remaining.controllers.test.ts — "returns every org's triggers when
        the query names no program", "creates a trigger against whatever programId

Fixed:  Both. `getAllEventTriggers(programId)` is required, so the
        "no filter at all" reading of an undefined where clause is not
        expressible any more — TypeScript refuses the call the enumeration
        depended on. Every event-trigger handler goes through one
        `requireProgramId` helper that resolves the caller's program from the
        session and answers 404 when there is none; the query string's programId
        is IGNORED rather than validated, because a caller only ever lists their
        own.
        createEventTrigger takes the program as a separate argument, so the
        request body cannot reach it. update, fetch and delete filter on it too.
        The command CRUD — getLoyaltyWaCommand, updateLoyaltyWaCommand,
        deleteLoyaltyWaCommandById — all take a required orgId and put it in the

[x] [20.34] HIGH — FIXED — The WhatsApp redeem flow bypasses every redemption rule
File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/settings.service.ts:304-330 (handleRewardConfirmation)
        src/modules/loyalty/settings/settings.service.ts:331-359 (handleDeduction)
Now:    Confirming a reward over WhatsApp calls assignRewardToCustomer and then a
        local handleDeduction. It never calls redeemReward, and it never creates a
        LoyaltyRedemption row. It also never touches stockRedeemed.
        The validator IS called first — but its result is read as
        `validation.message`, and validateRedemption returns
        `{ valid, violations, reward, customer }`. There is no `message` key, so
        the `|| "Unable to redeem reward right now."` fallback always wins.
Impact: This is a FOURTH redemption path (after point, coupon and regular in
        redemption.service.ts), and the one customers actually reach.
        Every quantity rule in redemptionValidator — one_time_use,
        max_redemptions_per_customer, _per_day, _per_week, _per_month and the
        cooldown — is evaluated by COUNTING LoyaltyRedemption rows. Because this
        path writes none, none of those limits can ever be tripped by a WhatsApp
        redemption. A "once per customer" reward can be taken repeatedly, subject
        only to the points balance and the reward-already-assigned check.
        Stock is never decremented either, so an inventory-limited reward is
        unlimited over WhatsApp.
        And when a rule DOES refuse, the customer is told only "Unable to redeem
        reward right now" — the violation the validator computed is discarded.
Pinned: settings.service.test.ts — "records no redemption row, so no quantity rule
        can ever count it", "never tells the customer which rule refused the
        redemption", "writes the ledger row from a pre-deduction read and
        mislabels the type"
Fixed:  Routed. handleRewardConfirmation calls `redeemReward(programId, …)` —
        the same path the API uses — and the local handleDeduction is deleted.
        So this flow now writes a LoyaltyRedemption row, which is what every
        quantity rule counts; burns stock; deducts inside a transaction against a
        conditional balance guard; and records the reward's real type instead of
        hard-coding "point". One redemption path instead of a fourth one.
        The `violations` half was ALREADY FIXED — the source reads
        `validation.violations.join(", ")`, verified rather than assumed. The
        validator is still called before redeemReward, only so the customer can
        be told WHICH rule refused them; redeemReward validates again for itself.

[x] [20.38] HIGH — FIXED — Points expiry writes off more than it takes
File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:530-585
Now:    The cron computes
        `const pointsToExpire = Math.min(totalExpiredPoints, account.currentPoints)`
        under the comment "Don't expire more points than the customer currently
        has" — and then uses it only for the log line and the running total. The
        loop underneath writes an `expire` transaction for each stale earning's
        FULL value. The balance is separately clamped with
        `Math.max(0, currentBalance - pointsFromThisTx)`.
Impact: The balance is protected; the ledger is not. A customer with 150 points
        of stale earnings but a 30-point balance (having spent the rest) gets
        expire rows totalling -150 while their balance falls by 30. Replaying the
        ledger no longer reconciles with currentPoints, and the "points expired"
        figure the cron logs and reports is inflated for every customer who ever
        redeemed anything.
        The loop also re-reads the balance from the database once per stale
        transaction (an N+1 per customer) and writes each row on its own, with no
        transaction around any of it.
Pinned: eventTriggerCron.service.test.ts — "the cap it computes is never applied
        to what it writes", "re-reads the balance once per expired transaction"
Fixed:  All three. The cap is now APPLIED: the stale earnings are walked
        oldest-first and each is allocated `min(remaining, its points)` until the
        cap is used up, so the expire rows sum to exactly what the balance loses.
        Earnings past the cap still get a row, with ZERO points — the row is what
        the query's NOT EXISTS keys on, so without it they would be re-examined
        on every run forever. That is the part worth flagging: dropping them
        would have been the obvious reading of "expire against the cap" and would
        have left an infinite re-scan behind.
        One `createMany` and one balance update, inside one `$transaction`. The
        per-transaction re-read of the balance — an N+1 per customer — is gone;
        the running balance is tracked in the loop that builds the rows.

[x] [20.37] MEDIUM — FIXED — The inactivity and expiry clocks overwrite each other
File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:214-220 (the claim)
        src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:576-583 (expiry)
        src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:375-393 (the alert)
Now:    `lastActivityAt` is the column the inactivity reminder selects on and the
        points-expiry alert selects on. Both crons WRITE it: the inactivity cron
        sets it to now as its duplicate-send claim, and the expiry cron sets it to
        now when it expires points.
        Separately, the expiry ALERT selects customers by lastActivityAt, but the
        expiry itself expires individual earn transactions by their own createdAt.
Impact: Reminding a dormant customer marks them as active today, so their
        inactivity clock restarts and they will not be reminded again for another
        full window — the reminder cancels its own follow-up. Expiring a
        customer's points does the same, pushing their next expiry alert out even
        though they did nothing.
        Because the alert and the expiry use different clocks, a customer can be
        warned about an expiry that never arrives, or lose points with no warning.
Pinned: eventTriggerCron.service.test.ts — "the claim marks a dormant customer as
        active today", "expiring points marks the account as active today",
        "selects by lastActivityAt, which is not what actually expires points"
Fixed:  Both, and the same treatment for the birthday cron (20.36).
        Two new nullable columns on CustomerLoyaltyAccount:
        `lastInactivityReminderAt` and `lastBirthdayGreetedAt`. Each cron claims
        with its own column, so reminding or greeting a customer no longer writes
        `lastActivityAt` — the column both crons SELECT on. The expiry no longer
        writes it either.
        The expiry ALERT now selects on the clock that actually decides:
        customers holding an `earn` transaction whose own createdAt falls in the
        alert window, rather than customers whose lastActivityAt does. The alert
        and the expiry read the same thing now.
NEEDS `npx prisma db push` — two new columns.

[x] [20.36] MEDIUM — FIXED — The birthday cron can greet the same customer twice
File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:82-117
        src/modules/loyalty/settings/eventTriggerCron.service.ts:293-333
Now:    The inactivity cron claims each customer with a conditional updateMany
        before sending, precisely so an overlapping run cannot double-send. The
        birthday cron has no such guard and records nothing per customer.
        It also loads every active customer with a date of birth for each trigger
        and filters the month and day in JavaScript.
        issueRewardToCustomer is a THIRD short-code generator and, unlike
        assignRewardToCustomer, does not loop until it finds a free code; its
        catch swallows every failure.
Impact: A second run on the same day — a redeploy, a retry, two instances —
        greets every birthday customer again.
        The in-memory filter means the whole membership is materialised daily per
        trigger.
        Because shortCode is @unique, a collision throws and is swallowed: the
        customer receives the birthday message promising "a special reward" and
        silently gets nothing. The same swallow hides the "already assigned"
        error on a repeat run.
Pinned: eventTriggerCron.service.test.ts — "has no idempotency guard, so a second
        run greets everyone again", "loads the whole membership and filters
        birthdays in memory", "swallows a failed assignment after the message has
        gone out"
Fixed:  All three.
        The claim is a conditional updateMany on `lastBirthdayGreetedAt` — null or
        earlier than today — before the send, exactly as the inactivity cron
        does. A second run on the same day claims nothing and greets nobody.
        The month and day are matched in SQL (`EXTRACT(MONTH …)`,
        `EXTRACT(DAY …)`), so only the customers with a birthday today are
        loaded, instead of the whole membership per trigger per day.
        issueRewardToCustomer now calls assignRewardToCustomer, which loops until
        it finds a free short code — so the third short-code generator is gone
        along with the collision it could not survive. The catch stays, because a
        failed reward must not stop the rest of the run, but the failure is
        logged for what it is rather than hidden behind an INFO success line.

[x] [20.40] MEDIUM — FIXED — trackReferral is a third account-creation path that disagrees
         with the other two
File:   src/modules/loyalty/referrals/referral.service.ts:58-138
Now:    trackReferral opens the referee's account itself rather than calling
        createCustomerAccount. It does not set `referredById`, does not set
        `currentTierId`, mints its own referral-code format, and — unlike
        createCustomerAccount, where the points credit is guarded by
        `referralBonusType === "points"` — pays the gift branch and the points
        branch from two INDEPENDENT `if`s.
Impact: A program configured for a gift referral bonus that also carries a
        non-zero `referralBonus` number pays the referrer BOTH the gift and the
        points, every time. createCustomerAccount pays only one.
        The referee's own row never records who referred them, so anything
        reading the account rather than the LoyaltyReferral table sees no
        referrer. And the referee starts untiered, which opens the tier bypass of
        issues.txt 20.6.
        Its code generator does not upper-case the name part, so codes minted here
        read "Bobb-4RI82" while signup codes read "BOBBY-AB12" — and the lookup is
        an exact, case-sensitive match, so a customer typing their own code in
        caps is not found.
Pinned: referral.test.ts — "pays a gift-type referrer the points as well when the
        number is non-zero", "never records the referrer on the referee's own
        account", "never places the referee in a tier", "mints a differently-cased
        referral code from the signup path", "matches a referral code exactly,
        with no case folding"
Fixed:  Called. trackReferral no longer opens the account itself — it resolves
        the referrer, checks the referee has none, and hands the contact to
        createCustomerAccount with the referrer's own code as `referCode`.
        That fixes all four disagreements at once rather than one at a time,
        which is the point: each of them followed from this being a second
        implementation of one operation, not from any of them being decided. The
        double payment, the missing referredById, the missing tier and the
        differently-cased code are all createCustomerAccount's business now, and
        it does each of them once.
        Case folding is real and separate: `findAccountByReferralCode` does
        `{ equals: code, mode: "insensitive" }`, used by both callers, because
        codes minted by the old path are still in the table in mixed case.

[~] [20.41] LOW — PARTLY FIXED — Completing a referral awards nothing
File:   src/modules/loyalty/referrals/referral.service.ts:141-168
Now:    completeReferral takes a purchase amount, names it `_purchaseAmount`, and
        never reads it. It flips the status to "completed" and stamps
        completedAt. The whole bonus was already paid at trackReferral time.
Impact: The pending -> completed lifecycle has no financial meaning: a referral
        that never completes has already paid out in full. Anything that reads

        The status. Paying on completion is the better design — it is what makes

        money is credited, which is a product decision rather than a correction.

        financial follows from it, which is at least now what the code says.

        indistinguishable from a genuine doubling — same shape as 19.5.

[x] [20.35] MEDIUM — FIXED — Template rendering mutates the stored content
File:   src/modules/loyalty/settings/settings.service.ts:439-456
Now:    `const contentCopy = [...contentArray]` is a SHALLOW copy. The loop then
        assigns `content.text = content.text.replace(...)` on the nested nodes,
        which are the caller's objects.
Impact: Rendering destroys the placeholders in the source document. A
        LoyaltyWaCommand row read once and rendered for two customers gives the
        SECOND customer the first customer's name and balance — the placeholders
        are gone by then, so nothing is substituted. Whether that happens depends
        on whether the row object is reused across sends; the function offers no
        protection either way.
Pinned: settings.service.test.ts — "mutates the caller's document instead of
        copying it"
Fixed:  Exactly that. `structuredClone(contentArray)` instead of
        `[...contentArray]`, so the nested nodes the loop assigns to are the
        function's own and the caller's document keeps its placeholders.

[x] [20.43] MEDIUM — FIXED — The API-settings module: an unscoped update and empty errors
File:   src/modules/loyalty/apiSetting/apiSetting.service.ts:30-41 (update)
        src/modules/loyalty/apiSetting/apiSetting.service.ts:19-28 (the body spread)
        src/modules/loyalty/apiSetting/apiSetting.controller.ts — every catch
Now:    getLoyaltyApiSettings and getLoyaltyApiSettingById are org-scoped;
        updateLoyaltyApiSetting takes the setting id alone, and its handler never
        reads request.user at all.
        createLoyaltyApiSetting does `...(data as any)`, spreading the whole
        request body into prisma.
        Every catch in the controller is `reply.code(500).send(e)` — the Error
        object itself.
Impact: An API setting belonging to another org can be repointed at a different
        action or reward.
        An Error has no enumerable own properties, so `send(e)` serialises to
        `{}`: the client gets a 500 with an empty body and no message. Every other
        controller in the module sends `{ error: e.message }`. Callers cannot
        distinguish a validation failure from a database outage.
Pinned: apiSetting.test.ts — "an update is keyed on the setting id alone, with no
        org", "updates without consulting the session at all", "answers a failure
        with the bare Error object", "spreads the whole body into the create"
Fixed:  Both. updateLoyaltyApiSetting takes a required orgId and puts it in the
        where clause; its handler reads `request.user`, which it did not do at
        all. All four catches send `{ error: e.message }` like the rest of the
        module, instead of the bare Error that serialised to `{}`.
Already fixed:
        The body spread. `createLoyaltyApiSetting` does `...data`, not
        `...(data as any)`, against a zod-validated `LApiSettingInput` — zod
        strips unknown keys, so no column the schema does not mention reaches
        prisma. Verified against the source.

[x] [20.32] LOW — FIXED — Dead preHandler, a partial phone strip, and positional template
         parameters that shift
File:   src/modules/loyalty/util.ts:7-17 (requireLoyaltyProgram)
        src/modules/loyalty/util.ts:30 (parsePhoneforWa)
        src/modules/loyalty/loyaltyWaDispatcher.ts:100-121 (the parameter array)
        src/modules/loyalty/loyaltyWaDispatcher.ts:124-128 (the catch)
Now:    requireLoyaltyProgram is written, exported and documented as a preHandler,
        and is registered on no route — every controller resolves the program
        inline instead.
        parsePhoneforWa removes only one leading "+", so "++91…" survives.
        The dispatcher builds its template parameters by pushing each value only
        when present, so a missing one shifts every later placeholder up a slot.
        Its catch logs and returns, deliberately, so a notification can never
        fail loudly.
Impact: The dead preHandler is a maintenance trap — a future route that adopts it
        would get behaviour nothing else in the module relies on.
        A customer with no stored name receives a template where {{1}} renders the
        points and {{2}} the balance, silently one place out.
        The blanket catch means a template that is wrong for every customer fails
        invisibly forever.
Pinned: loyalty.util.test.ts — "is a preHandler no route uses", "removes only one
        leading plus", "a missing name shifts every later placeholder up by one",
        "swallows every failure rather than surfacing it"
Fixed:  Deleted, padded, and logged.
        requireLoyaltyProgram is DELETED rather than adopted: a route that picked
        it up would have got behaviour nothing else in the module relies on — the
        program attached to `request.loyaltyProgram` instead of resolved where it
        is used, and a 400 for a missing org where every handler answers 404 for
        a missing program.
        parsePhoneforWa strips ALL leading plus signs (`/^\++/`), not one. A plus
        in the middle is still left alone: that is not a formatting artefact and
        stripping it would silently change the number.
        The dispatcher's parameter array is built by POSITION, with an empty
        string standing in for anything missing, so a customer with no stored
        name no longer shifts every later placeholder up a slot.
        The blanket catch STAYS — a notification must never roll back the points
        or the redemption it announces — but it now logs at error level with the
        event and the org attached, which is the signal an operator has.

[x] [20.44] LOW — FIXED — Any unique-constraint failure is reported as "already redeemed"
File:   src/modules/loyalty/redemptions/redemption.controller.ts:34-37
Now:    `if (e.code === "P2002") reply.code(400).send({ error: "Reward already
        redeemed" })`.
Impact: The redemption path writes three unique columns —
        CustomerReward.rewardId_accountId, CustomerReward.shortCode and
        LoyaltyRedemption.redemptionCode. A random code collision (issues.txt
        20.9) therefore tells the customer they have already taken a reward they
        have not taken, and the real cause is invisible.
Pinned: loyalty.remaining.controllers.test.ts — "reports any unique-constraint
        failure as an already-redeemed reward"
Fixed:  Exactly that. `meta.target` decides: a collision on the
        (rewardId, accountId) pair is the only one that means the customer
        already holds the reward, and answers 400 "Reward already redeemed". A
        collision on either code column answers 409 and says so — those are
        retryable, and telling the customer they had already taken a reward they
        had not taken hid the real cause. A P2002 with no target at all is
        treated as a collision rather than as an already-taken reward, because
        the wrong answer there is the one that accuses the customer.

NOT COVERED — LOYALTY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - loyalty.routes.ts is the only file in the module without tests: it is 42
    lines of server.register calls. Its guards ARE sound — checkSession, a
    subscription gate and requireFeature(TeamFeature.loyalty) apply to the whole
    authenticated tree. 20.13 is about the separate public route, not this one.
  - The concurrency claims in 20.17 and 20.9 are reasoned from the code, not
    reproduced against a live database — the tests pin the shape of the queries
    (an unconditional decrement, a read-then-write guard), not an observed race.
    Both `LoyaltyRedemption.redemptionCode` and `CustomerReward.shortCode` ARE
    `@unique` in prisma/schema.prisma (lines 2211 and 2139), so the collision
    half of 20.9 is a hard failure rather than a silent duplicate.
  - The two raw-SQL blocks in dashboard.service.ts (monthly member growth and
    monthly points activity) are pinned by what they are handed and what the
    service does with their rows, not by what the SQL computes — the
    generate_series and the cumulative member count need a database to verify.
    The same applies to the NOT EXISTS sub-select that finds unexpired earnings
    in checkPointsExpiration.
  - handleLoyaltyFeedback (settings.service.ts:381) has its entire body commented
    out except the closing template send, so a configured feedback action awards
    nothing. Recorded here rather than as a numbered finding because it is
    reached only from the loyalty action system, which does not appear to be
    wired to any live route.
  - The downstream WhatsApp senders (sendMessage, sendTextMessage,
    handleSendTemplate, handleWaJoinRegistration) and the survey utils that build
    list and button messages are mocked at their boundary. What is pinned is
    which message is built with which parameters, not what Meta receives.

================================================================================
21. CHATBOT MODULE (src/modules/chatbot)
================================================================================

Two test files, 105 tests. A thin module — 852 lines across five files — that
mostly proxies an external AI service (CHATBOT_API_URL) and keeps a small
Chatbot table beside it. Both of its routes are here: an authenticated tree
guarded by checkSession + requireFeature(ai_chatbot) with per-route role checks,
and a three-endpoint public tree at /api/public/chatbot with no session guard.

The public tree is where the problems were (21.3, 21.10). The rest were
error-handling and consistency issues around a service that spends money per
call.

All fourteen entries are fixed except the root cause of 21.3, which needs a
signed widget session and therefore a frontend change; it is rate limited in the
meantime and recorded as partly fixed.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.10] HIGH — FIXED — The public chat endpoint ignored the chatbot's
    public-page switch
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (publicChatbotMessageHandler)
        prisma/schema.prisma:1816 (publicPageStatus, default "INACTIVE")
Was:    The Chatbot row carries `publicPageStatus`, defaulting to "INACTIVE", and
        the module exports getChatbotPublicPageStatusHandler whose only job is to
        report it. publicChatbotMessageHandler resolved the chatbot by its short
        url id, took `orgId` off it, and went straight to the AI service. It never
        read publicPageStatus.
Impact: A chatbot whose public page had never been switched on — the default
        state for every chatbot ever created — still answered queries from the
        open internet. So did one that had been deliberately switched off. Each
        answer is billed to the org's AI token allowance (21.1), and the endpoint
        is unauthenticated, so the cost was bounded only by how fast an attacker
        could post. The short url id is an 8-character nanoid and was the only
        thing needed.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "answers even when the chatbot's public page
        is switched off"
Fixed:  The handler refuses with 403 unless publicPageStatus is exactly ACTIVE, so
        a chatbot that has never been switched on, one switched off, and one whose
        status column is null all refuse the same way. The check is on the server,
        where it belongs, rather than only in the widget that renders the page.
        The endpoint is also rate limited per IP (60/hour, reusing the limiter
        built for issues.txt 16.2), which bounds what one address can spend of the
        org's allowance.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[~] [21.3] HIGH — PARTLY FIXED — The public routes identify the user by a value
    the caller supplies
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.public.route.ts (no session on any route)
        src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (deletePublicChatHistory)
        src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (userId from the body)
Was:    chatbotPublicRoute registered three endpoints and added no preHandler.
        `DELETE /api/public/chatbot/:userId` passed the path segment straight to
        the AI service's delete-history endpoint, with no credential of any kind.
        `POST /api/public/chatbot` took `userId` from the request body and used
        it as the chat-thread key.
Impact: Anyone could delete any public chat history by naming its user id — there
        was no session, no signature and no ownership check anywhere in the path.
        On the message endpoint the caller chooses which thread they are writing
        to, so one visitor can append to, and read the context of, another
        visitor's conversation by reusing their id.
        Whether the ids are guessable depends on what the widget generates, which
        this module does not control — it accepts whatever arrives.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "deletes a history by a path id, with no
        session and no org", "takes the conversation's user id from the request
        body"
        chatbot.service.test.ts — "clearing public chat history sends no
        credential"
Fixed:  Both public writes are rate limited per IP — 60/hour for messages,
        20/hour for history deletes — which takes the free repetition out of both
        the deletion and the thread-guessing, and 21.10's status check now closes
        the endpoint entirely for any chatbot whose public page is not on.
Still open:
        The root cause: the thread id is still whatever the caller sends, and the
        delete still carries no credential. Closing it means issuing a signed
        session token to the widget and deriving the thread id from it, which
        changes the widget as well as this endpoint — a design change, not a
        correction, and left for its own piece of work rather than approximated
        here. Same shape, and the same reasoning, as 20.13's remainder.

[x] [21.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — A public answer with no usage block was thrown away
    after it was paid for
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (getPublicAIResponseForQuery)
Was:    The authenticated variant guarded its usage write with `if (tokenUsage)`.
        The public one did not: it built the update argument unconditionally, and
        `increment: tokenUsage.promptTokens` was evaluated SYNCHRONOUSLY while
        constructing that object. An answer that arrived without a tokenUsage
        block therefore raised a TypeError and the whole call rejected.
        The write was also not awaited — the answer was returned before the tokens
        had been recorded.
Impact: The AI service had already produced the answer, and the org had already
        been charged for it upstream, when this threw. The customer saw
        "Something went wrong!" and the answer was discarded.
        Separately, because the write was not awaited, a failed or slow usage
        update lost the billing record for that answer silently — and its
        rejection was unhandled.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "throws away an answer that arrives without a
        token-usage block", "the authenticated variant guards the same read and
        survives it", "returns before the usage write has settled"
Fixed:  The `if (tokenUsage)` guard copied across, and the write awaited. An answer
        without a usage block is now returned to the customer and simply records
        nothing; a failed write surfaces as a rejection instead of vanishing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.1] MEDIUM — FIXED — The token allowance was advisory, and reading it threw
    without a plan
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (getAiAllowance, both callers)
Was:    Both paths read `subscription.features.ai.count` with no guard and passed
        it to the AI service as `allowedTokens`, alongside the org's current usage.
        Neither compared them. Whether an org over its allowance was refused was
        entirely the remote service's decision.
Impact: An org with no subscription row, or on a plan that does not describe an
        `ai` feature, got a TypeError before the query was sent — reported as a
        generic 500 with no indication that the plan was the problem.
        And because the limit was only advice, a bug or an outage in the remote
        service's enforcement meant unlimited billable AI usage with nothing on
        this side to stop it.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "throws for an org with no subscription",
        "throws for a plan that describes no AI feature", "sends the query even
        when usage already exceeds the allowance"
Fixed:  One getAiAllowance() serves both paths. It guards every read and refuses,
        before the query is sent, when there is no subscription, when the plan
        describes no AI feature, when the AI feature is switched off, or when
        either counter has reached its allowance — the local enforcement that was
        never written. It throws a named AiAllowanceError, which the handlers
        answer 403 to (with the reason on the authenticated route, and a bare
        "This chatbot is unavailable" on the public one, which should not disclose
        an org's billing state).
        Note the allowance is an OBJECT — `{ promptTokens, completionTokens }`,
        per SubscriptionFeatures — not the single number the old pinning tests
        implied; both counters are compared. A plan storing something other than a
        number for either is left to the remote service rather than refused here,
        since refusing on a shape this module does not own would take working
        chatbots down.

[x] [21.5] MEDIUM — FIXED — A PUT to an unknown chatbot silently created an empty
    one
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (updateChatbotDetails)
        src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (updateChatbotDetailsHanlder)
Was:    The function was an upsert whose `update` branch spread the caller's data
        and whose `create` branch wrote only `{ shortUrlId, orgId }` — the data was
        discarded entirely. It returned nothing, and the handler answered 201 with
        that nothing.
        When `enableAllWaChat` was present in the body it then rewrote `aiWaChat`
        on EVERY contact in the org, in one updateMany, scoped to the org rather
        than to the chatbot.
Impact: A PUT against a chatbot id that did not exist (a stale tab, a deleted
        chatbot, a typo) created a brand-new "Default Chatbot" and threw away
        every setting the user had just submitted — then answered 201, so the UI
        showed success and the settings simply were not there.
        The contact sweep fired whenever the key was present, even if the value
        had not changed, and it was org-wide: an org running several chatbots had
        one chatbot's toggle overwrite the AI flag for every contact, including
        those talking to a different chatbot.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "the create branch discards everything the
        caller submitted", "returns nothing at all", "the sweep is scoped to the
        org, not to the chatbot that was toggled", "fires the contact sweep even
        when the flag is set to false"
        chatbot.controller.test.ts — "answers an update with 201 and an empty body"
Fixed:  An update, not an upsert: a missing row raises P2025 and the handler
        answers 404, so a stale tab can no longer create anything. The row is
        returned and answered with 200, so the client can read back what was
        saved.
        The sweep now runs only when `enableAllWaChat` actually CHANGES — it reads
        the stored value first — and skips contacts bound to a different chatbot
        (`chatbotId: { in: ["", chatbotId] }`; Contact.chatbotId is stamped when a
        WhatsApp AI session starts and is "" for contacts that never had one). It
        stays org-wide for unbound contacts, because that is what the flag means.

[x] [21.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — getChatbotDetails returned an arbitrary chatbot when
    none was named
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (getChatbotDetails)
Was:    `findFirst({ where: { id: chatbotId, orgId, ...(onlyEnabled && {...}) } })`
        with no ordering. strictUndefinedChecks is not enabled in
        prisma/schema.prisma (established in issues.txt 12.x), so `id: undefined`
        is DROPPED from the where clause rather than matching nothing.
Impact: Callers that pass only an org got whichever chatbot the database returned
        first. contact.service.ts and the WhatsApp inbox both do exactly that, to
        decide whether AI chat is enabled for a new contact — so in an org with
        more than one chatbot, that decision was made by an arbitrary one, and
        which one could change between calls.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "drops the id filter entirely when no chatbot is
        named", "matches the chatbot and the org together"
Fixed:  `orderBy: { id: "asc" }`, so the org-only fallback is at least the SAME
        chatbot on every call and the decision stops moving under the callers.
        Taking an explicit id was not an option here: seven callers in
        contact.service.ts and the WhatsApp survey path have no chatbot to name —
        they are asking "does this org have AI chat on at all". Chatbot has no
        createdAt column, so the order is by id; documented on the function.

[x] [21.14] MEDIUM — FIXED — Deleting a chatbot had no error handling
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (deleteSelectedChatbotHandler)
Was:    deleteSelectedChatbotHandler was the only handler in the module with no
        try/catch. It called the org-scoped delete and then answered
        `{ success: true }` unconditionally.
Impact: Deleting a chatbot that did not exist — or one belonging to another org,
        which the org-scoped where turns into the same thing — raised prisma's
        P2025 and escaped the handler, so fastify answered with its own generic
        500 and nothing was logged with any context. Every sibling handler caught
        and reported.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "lets a missing or foreign chatbot escape as
        an unhandled rejection"
        chatbot.service.test.ts — "lets a missing-row error escape to the caller"
Fixed:  Wrapped. P2025 answers 404 — a chatbot another org owns is indistinguish-
        able from one that never existed — and anything else is logged and
        answered 500, like its siblings. The service still lets the error out,
        which is what lets the handler tell the two cases apart.

[x] [21.6] MEDIUM — FIXED — getAxiosAIClient returned undefined on failure, and
    its caller threw a string
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (getAxiosAIClient)
        src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (getTextSummaryByAI)
Was:    getAxiosAIClient wrapped its body in try/catch, logged, and returned
        nothing on failure — so it resolved to `undefined`. Both callers
        immediately read a method off the result.
        getTextSummaryByAI's own catch did `throw \`Error in getting text
        summary, ${...}\`` — a template STRING, not an Error.
Impact: A failure to mint the JWT turned into "Cannot read properties of undefined
        (reading 'post')" several frames from the actual cause.
        Throwing a string lost the stack entirely, and any caller doing
        `err.message` or `err instanceof Error` got nothing — getTextSummaryByAI
        is called from outside this module, so that matters.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "returns undefined rather than failing when
        the token cannot be minted"
        chatbot.service.test.ts — "throws a string, not an Error, when the AI
        service fails", "fails with a property read when the client could not be
        built"
Fixed:  getAxiosAIClient no longer catches: a signing failure propagates as itself,
        and both callers see the real cause. The two `if (!client)` guards written
        around the old undefined are gone with it. The string throw had already
        been corrected to a real Error in an earlier pass; the pinning tests for
        both halves now assert the current behaviour.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.12] MEDIUM — FIXED — A chatbot id from the request was never checked
    against the org
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (chatbotMessageHandler,
        getChatHitoryByOrgHandler, getUploadedSourceHandler)
Was:    All three took a chatbotId from the body, the path or the query and
        forwarded it to the AI service without calling getChatbotDetails — the one
        function in the module that would confirm the org owns it. The message
        handler did send the session's orgId alongside; the history and source
        handlers sent only the JWT.
Impact: Whether another org's chatbot could be queried, have its history read, or
        have its embedded sources listed depended entirely on how the remote AI
        service scopes the request. Nothing on this side enforced it, and the JWT
        is minted from `request.user` so it carries the caller's own org — which
        may or may not be what the remote service keys on.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "forwards a chatbot id from the body without
        checking it belongs to the org", "asks for a chatbot's history without
        checking the org owns it"
Fixed:  All three resolve the chatbot with getChatbotDetails(orgId, chatbotId)
        first and answer 404 when it is not the caller's, so the boundary is
        enforced here rather than assumed of the remote service. The source
        listing only checks when a chatbot is actually named — with none named it
        asks for everything the token can see, as before.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.8] MEDIUM — FIXED — Chat-history user ids were resolved against every org
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (getChatBotHistory)
Was:    The ids the AI service returns were looked up in `User` and in `Contact`
        with `where: { id: { in: userIds } }` — no org filter — and the selects
        pull `email` and `phone`. The anonymous fallback read
        `hist.userId.slice(-4)` with no guard.
Impact: If the AI service ever returned an id from outside the caller's org, this
        resolved it and returned that person's name, email or phone. The module
        does not constrain what comes back, and 21.12 meant the chatbotId asked
        about was not verified either.
        A history entry with no userId took the entire listing down with a
        TypeError rather than degrading to "Anonymous".
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "resolves the ids against every org's users and
        contacts", "throws when a history entry carries no user id"
Fixed:  getChatBotHistory takes the caller's orgId and scopes both lookups by it —
        users through `orgMember: { some: { orgId } }`, contacts by `orgId` — so an
        id from outside the org resolves to "Anonymous" instead of to somebody's
        email or phone. The slice is guarded, and ids that are absent are filtered
        out of the lookup rather than sent to the database as undefined.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.13] LOW — FIXED — Response-shape and multipart inconsistencies
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (the proxying handlers, the
        multipart loop), src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.rout.ts (create route)
Was:    uploadSourceForEmbedHandler sent the axios response object verbatim —
        status, headers, config and all — rather than its `data`, and three
        sibling handlers did the same.
        getChatHitoryByOrgHandler did `reply.code(500).send(e)` with the Error
        itself; an Error has no enumerable own properties, so it serialised to
        `{}`.
        The multipart loop kept only the LAST file part (each overwrote
        `filePart`) while still buffering every one into memory and appending them
        all under the same "file" key.
        The create route declared no body schema, so `name` was whatever arrived,
        including undefined.
Impact: The upload response leaked the outbound Authorization header back to the
        client in `config.headers`.
        The history endpoint answered 500 with an empty body where every sibling
        sends `{ message: ... }`.
        Uploading two files silently processed both and told the caller nothing
        about which one was kept.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "sends the whole axios response rather than
        its data", "answers a failure with the bare Error object", "silently keeps
        only the last of several uploaded files", "creates a nameless chatbot when
        the body carries no name"
Fixed:  Every proxying handler sends `res.data`, so the Authorization header stops
        coming back with it. The history handler sends `{ message }` like its
        siblings. A second file part is refused with 400 before it is buffered,
        rather than silently replacing the first. The create route declares
        createChatbotSchema, which requires a non-empty name.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [21.9] LOW — FIXED — One call parsed the base url and failed differently when
    it was unset
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (aiApiBaseUrl and its callers)
Was:    The base urls were built into module-level consts from
        `process.env.CHATBOT_API_URL` at import time. Every call passed its url to
        axios as a string except getUploadedSources, which did
        `new URL(CHATBOT_BASEURL)` to append a query parameter.
Impact: With CHATBOT_API_URL unset the base became the literal string
        "undefined/api/chatbot". `new URL` rejected that with a bare "Invalid URL"
        before any request was attempted, while every sibling call failed later at
        the network layer with a different message — so a misconfigured
        environment presented as two unrelated symptoms.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "fails on an unparseable base url before any
        request is made"
Fixed:  One aiApiBaseUrl() helper, read per call, that throws
        "CHATBOT_API_URL is not configured" when the variable is missing — so every
        call in the module fails the same way and the message names the variable.
        getUploadedSources builds its query by concatenation like its siblings
        rather than parsing the base.
        Deliberately NOT a hard boot check: throwing at import would take the whole
        API down over a chatbot misconfiguration, and this module is one feature of
        many. The failure is now uniform and self-describing, which is what the
        entry was about.

[x] [21.7] LOW — FIXED — Short url ids were minted with no collision retry
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.service.ts (createChatbot)
        src/utils/commonUtils.ts (shortIdUniqueConstraintErrHandler)
Was:    Both writers called `getShortUrlId()` once. `shortUrlId` is `@unique`
        (prisma/schema.prisma:1815). commonUtils exports
        shortIdUniqueConstraintErrHandler, which exists to retry exactly this
        collision, and neither chatbot writer used it.
Impact: A collision surfaced as a raw P2002 and the create failed. The id is an
        8-character nanoid from the nolookalikes-safe alphabet, so it is unlikely —
        but the retry helper is right there and every other short-id writer in the
        codebase uses it.
Pinned: chatbot.service.test.ts — "mints the id without any collision retry"
Fixed:  createChatbot is wrapped in shortIdUniqueConstraintErrHandler, with the id
        minted inside the retried function so the retry gets a fresh one. The
        second writer — the upsert's create branch — is gone entirely with 21.5,
        since updateChatbotDetails no longer creates anything.

[x] [21.11] LOW — FIXED — An unknown short url id was a 500, not a 404
File:   src/modules/chatbot/chatbot.controller.ts (both public readers)
Was:    Both public readers destructured the result of
        getChatbotDetailsByShortUrlId without checking it — `const { orgId } =
        await ...` and `const { publicPageStatus } = await ...`. An id that
        matched nothing returned null and the destructure threw.
Impact: A stale or mistyped public chatbot link answered 500 "Something went
        wrong!" instead of 404, and the log carried a TypeError rather than the
        actual condition.
Pinned: chatbot.controller.test.ts — "answers a 500 for a short url id that
        matches no chatbot", "answers a 500 for an unknown short url id"
Fixed:  Both check for null and answer 404. (Corrected in an earlier strict-mode
        pass; the pinning tests were rewritten with it and are recorded here for
        completeness.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOT COVERED — CHATBOT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - The external AI service behind CHATBOT_API_URL is a separate codebase and was
    not read. Everything this module does to it is mocked at the axios boundary:
    what is pinned is which url, body and headers are sent, and what the module
    does with the reply. 21.12 was a question about that boundary — whether the
    remote service scopes a chatbotId by the JWT's org — and is now answered on
    this side regardless of what the remote does.
  - chatbot.rout.ts and chatbot.public.route.ts wiring. The authenticated tree
    carries checkSession, requireFeature(TeamFeature.ai_chatbot) and per-route
    checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN","ADMIN"]) on every write; note the message
    endpoint (POST /) and the source listing (GET /) deliberately have no role
    check, so any member can query the bot and see its sources. The public tree
    still has no session — see 21.3's remainder — but both of its writes are now
    rate limited and its message endpoint honours publicPageStatus.
  - The rate limits (60/hour messages, 20/hour history deletes, per IP) are
    judgement calls, not measured against real widget traffic. They fail OPEN if
    Redis is unreachable, by the limiter's design.
  - Whether the frontend checks publicPageStatus before rendering the widget was
    not established; 21.10 was about the server not checking it, and now it does.
  - getTextSummaryByAI is called from outside this module (the survey summary
    path). Its callers' handling of the error it throws was not traced.
  - 21.5's contact sweep now excludes contacts bound to another chatbot. Whether
    every org intends the flag to reach contacts with no chatbot binding at all
    was not established with a product owner; that is the behaviour it has always
    had, minus the cross-chatbot overwrite.

================================================================================
22. CONTACT FORM MODULE (src/modules/contactForm)
================================================================================

One test file, 35 tests. The smallest module in the codebase — 88 lines across
four files — backing the "contact us" form on the marketing site: validate,
store a row, email the RateUp team.

The first finding still frames the rest: THE MODULE IS NOT WIRED UP. Nothing
outside src/modules/contactForm imports any of its files, and
contactFormPublicRoutes appears in registerRoutes.ts nowhere. Everything below
was therefore latent — real in the code, unreachable in production. All of it is
fixed; the registration is deliberately NOT done (see 22.1), so wiring the module
up is now a one-line change with nothing left to fix first.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [22.1] MEDIUM — STILL OPEN (deliberate) — The whole module is dead code
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.public.route.ts (contactFormPublicRoutes)
        src/registerRoutes.ts (no reference to it)
Now:    contactFormPublicRoutes is exported and never registered. A search across
        src for `contactFormPublicRoutes`, for the module's file paths, and for
        its two exported functions (createContactMsgHandler, createMsg) returns
        only matches inside the module itself. emailBodyContactForm in
        src/utils/email/ has this module as its only caller.
        The ContactForm model does exist (prisma/schema.prisma:1171), so the
        table is real and presumably has rows from an earlier wiring.
Impact: The marketing site's contact form either posts somewhere else entirely or
        is broken — this code cannot be what serves it. Either way ~90 lines plus
        an email template are carried, compiled and typechecked for nothing.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "registers exactly one POST and nothing else",
        "is not registered anywhere in registerRoutes"
Decision:
        Left unregistered on purpose. Whether this endpoint should exist is a
        question about where the marketing site posts, which is outside this
        repository — mounting it would add a live public endpoint on a guess.
        What HAS changed is that the module is now safe to wire up: 22.2 through
        22.7 are all fixed, so registering it is one line in registerRoutes.ts
        rather than one line plus this section. The route file says so at the top,
        and a test asserts the module is still absent from registerRoutes so this
        entry cannot go stale silently.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — The honeypot announced itself
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.service.ts (createMsg)
        src/modules/contactForm/contact.controller.ts
Was:    `if (address?.length > 0) return "Error";` — the branch returned the STRING
        "Error" rather than throwing or returning a sentinel the caller
        recognised. The controller had no special case for it, so it took the
        normal success path and answered `201 { success: true, data: "Error" }`.
Impact: The entire point of a honeypot is that a bot cannot tell whether it was
        caught. Two requests settled it: a clean submission returned an object, a
        tripped one returned the literal string "Error". Once a bot knows the
        field name it simply stops filling it, and the module's only abuse control
        is gone.
        The check itself was in the right place — before any write — so nothing
        was stored or emailed when it tripped. It was the response that leaked.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "returns the literal string Error rather than
        throwing", "reports a tripped honeypot as a successful submission",
        "refuses before writing anything when the honeypot is filled"
Fixed:  createMsg returns null when the honeypot is filled, and the handler answers
        `201 { success: true }` for BOTH cases — the stored row is no longer echoed
        back, so there is nothing in the response to compare. A test asserts the
        two replies are deeply equal.
        Timing is still observable in principle (the clean path writes a row and
        queues a job); that is not something a response-shape fix can hide, and it
        is a far weaker signal than a differing body.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — The notification email interpolated the submitter's
    markup unescaped
File:   src/utils/email/emailBodyContactForm.ts
Was:    `<li><strong>Name:</strong> ${name}</li>` and the same for email and
        message. No escaping anywhere, and the value goes into the `html` field
        of the queued notification.
Impact: All three fields come from an unauthenticated visitor, so the visitor
        chose the markup that rendered in the RateUp team's inbox: working links
        to anywhere, remote images that report when the mail is opened, or content
        styled to impersonate the rest of the message. Modern mail clients strip
        script, but links, images and layout all survive.
        This was the same defect as the BookDemo mailer (issues.txt 16.1/16.2) in
        a second template — the pattern was repeated rather than isolated.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "interpolates the submitter's markup into the email
        unescaped", "does not escape angle brackets or quotes anywhere"
Fixed:  All three interpolations go through escapeHtml — the helper written for
        16.1, so the two templates now handle visitor input the same way. The
        template's own markup is untouched, which a test pins alongside.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.4] LOW — FIXED — A queue failure lost the submission from the visitor's
    point of view
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.service.ts
Was:    The row was created, then the notification queued in a try/catch whose
        catch rethrew `new Error("Email sending failed. E:" + error)`. There was
        no transaction and no compensating delete.
Impact: A Redis outage left the message stored but unseen, while the visitor was
        told the submission failed — so they sent it again, and the table
        accumulated duplicates nobody was notified about. Storing first is the
        right order; reporting the whole thing as a failure was not.
        The rethrow also concatenated the caught value onto a string, producing
        "Email sending failed. E:Error: redis down" and discarding the original
        stack.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "keeps the stored row when the notification cannot
        be queued", "discards the original error's stack when it rethrows"
Fixed:  The queue failure is logged — with the stored row's id, so the submission
        can be recovered from the table — and the submission is reported as the
        success it is. The string-concatenating rethrow is gone with it; the
        original error goes to the logger as `err`, stack intact.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.5] LOW — FIXED — A guard that could never be false
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.service.ts
Was:    `if (user) { ...queue the email... }` where `user` is the result of
        `prisma.contactForm.create`, which either resolves with a row or rejects.
Impact: None at the time — it read as though the email were conditional on the
        write having succeeded, which is true but not because of this check. It is
        the kind of guard that invites a future reader to add an else branch that
        can never run.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "the guard around the email can never be false"
Fixed:  Dropped.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.6] LOW — FIXED — The public handler returned the raw error message
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.controller.ts
Was:    `reply.code(500).send({ success: false, message: e.message })`.
Impact: On an endpoint with no authentication, that handed the caller whatever the
        driver put in the message — a prisma error naming the table and column, or
        the "Email sending failed. E:..." wrapper naming the queue. Every other
        public endpoint in the codebase sends a fixed string.
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "leaks the raw error message to the caller"
Fixed:  The error goes to the logger; the caller gets a fixed
        `{ success: false, message: "Something went wrong" }`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [22.7] LOW — FIXED — No rate limit and no length ceiling
File:   src/modules/contactForm/contact.public.route.ts
        src/modules/contactForm/contact.schema.ts
Was:    The route registered one POST with a body schema and nothing else — no
        preHandler, no config, no hook. The schema set minimum lengths on name
        and message and no maximum on anything.
Impact: Were the route registered, one unauthenticated endpoint would write a row
        and queue an email to the team inbox on every request, with the
        self-announcing honeypot (22.2) as the only control. The message field had
        no ceiling, so a single request could store a very large string and put
        it, unescaped, into an email (22.3).
Pinned: contactForm.test.ts — "adds no hook, no preHandler and no rate limit",
        "puts no upper bound on the message length"
Fixed:  Rate limited per IP at 5/hour, the same limit and the same limiter the
        other public marketing forms use (issues.txt 16.2). The schema now bounds
        name (100), email (254), message (5000) and the honeypot field (200) as
        well as flooring them.
        No captcha: adding one is a frontend change and a third-party dependency,
        and it belongs with the decision about whether this endpoint should exist
        at all (22.1).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOT COVERED — CONTACT FORM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - Whether the ContactForm table has live rows, and therefore whether this
    module was wired up at some point and later unwired, needs the database. The
    model is still in prisma/schema.prisma either way.
  - Where the marketing site's contact form actually posts was not traced — it is
    outside this repository. 22.1 says only that it cannot be this code, which is
    also why the route is still not registered.
  - The notification worker that consumes the queued `emailData` job lives in the
    notification module and was not read here; what is pinned is the job name and
    payload this module enqueues.
  - `z.email()` at contact.schema.ts is Zod 4's top-level email validator. Its
    exact acceptance rules were not enumerated beyond confirming an obviously
    malformed address is rejected.
  - The 5000-character message ceiling is a judgement call, not a measured one.
    It is long enough for a genuine enquiry and short enough that the email stays
    readable.

================================================================================
23. DASHBOARD MODULE (src/modules/dashboard)
================================================================================

One test file, 29 tests. 177 lines across three files: three raw-SQL reads
backing the main dashboard — headline counts, the caller's active surveys, and a
team roster.

Two findings dominated. The headline-counts query selected from a table a
migration dropped six months ago, so the endpoint could not succeed (23.1); and
the team roster was not scoped to an org, so any authenticated user could read
any team's members and their contact details (23.2). All seven entries are fixed.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [23.1] HIGH — FIXED — The main stats query read a table the loyalty migration
    dropped
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts (getOverallStats)
        prisma/migrations/20260120044921_new_loyalty/migration.sql:143
Was:    getOverallStats is a single statement of eight scalar sub-selects, one of
        which was
        `(SELECT COUNT(*)::INTEGER FROM "LoyaltyCustomer" WHERE "orgId" = ...)`.
        Migration 20260120044921_new_loyalty — the loyalty rewrite that section 20
        covers — runs `DROP TABLE "LoyaltyCustomer"` (and LoyaltyCustomerMap),
        replacing them with CustomerLoyaltyAccount. There is no LoyaltyCustomer
        model left in prisma/schema.prisma.
Impact: Postgres resolves relations for the whole statement before executing any
        of it, so this did not merely zero the loyalty tile — it raised
        `relation "LoyaltyCustomer" does not exist` and took EVERY count with it.
        `GET /api/dashboard/stats` therefore returned 500, and with it the new
        chats, active campaigns, published flows, automations, active web
        surveys, teams and tickets figures.
        The migration is dated 2026-01-20, so the main dashboard's headline
        numbers had been failing for roughly six months. The handler answered
        with a bare Error that serialises to `{}` (23.6), which is very likely
        why nobody traced it: the response was an empty 500 with no message.
Pinned: dashboard.test.ts — "counts from a table the loyalty migration dropped"
Fixed:  Counts CustomerLoyaltyAccount joined to LoyaltyProgram on programId, with
        the org taken from `lp."orgId"` — which is where a loyalty customer's
        organisation lives after the rewrite (LoyaltyProgram.orgId is @unique, one
        program per org, so the join cannot multiply the count).
        23.6's `{ message }` fix means the next breakage of this kind arrives with
        something to read instead of an empty body.
Still to do:
        No smoke test executes this statement against a migrated database — the
        suite mocks $queryRaw, so what is pinned is the SQL text and the bound
        values, not that postgres accepts it. That is the same limit every raw-SQL
        test in this repository has, and it is what let 23.1 live for six months;
        a migrated-database smoke test belongs to its own piece of work.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [23.2] HIGH — FIXED — Any authenticated user could read any team's roster
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts (getTeamMemberStats)
        src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.controller.ts (its handler)
Was:    The route is `GET /teamStats/:teamId` behind checkSession only — no
        requireFeature, no role check. The handler read `request.params.teamId`
        and nothing else; `request.user` was never touched. The query filtered on
        `tm."teamId" = ${teamId}` with no org condition anywhere.
Impact: A team id was enough for a user of any organisation to read that team's
        entire roster: every member's name, EMAIL ADDRESS, profile photo, role,
        last-login time, assigned-ticket and assigned-chat counts, and whether
        they are currently online.
        That is a directory of another company's staff, their seniority and their
        current workload, from one authenticated GET. Team ids appear in other
        responses, so they are not secret.
        Same family as issues.txt 1.1 / 11.1 / 18.5 / 20.18 — but this is the
        instance that returned colleagues' email addresses.
Pinned: dashboard.test.ts — "filters on the team id alone, with no org
        restriction", "team stats are read from the path id without consulting
        the session"
Fixed:  The service takes the caller's orgId. It resolves the team within that org
        first and returns null when it is not the caller's, which the handler
        answers 404 to — a team another org owns is indistinguishable from one
        that does not exist — and the roster query itself joins Team and filters
        `t."orgId"` as well, so the scoping does not rest on the pre-check alone.
        The OrgMember join is now scoped to the same org too. It matched on
        `orm."userId"` alone, so a user who belongs to several organisations could
        have their role and availability read from whichever membership row came
        back first — a smaller bug found while fixing this one, recorded here
        rather than as its own entry.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [23.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — Opening the dashboard erased stale last-login
    timestamps
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts (the backfill, now removed)
Was:    For any member whose `lastLoggedInAt` was absent or dated 2020 or earlier,
        the loop asked SuperTokens for their live sessions and took the newest
        `timeCreated`. It then wrote:
        `if (lastLoggedInAt || (user.lastLoggedInAt && !lastLoggedInAt))
           await updateLastLoggedInTime(user.id, lastLoggedInAt)`.
        The second arm fired exactly when the member HAD an old timestamp and had
        NO live sessions — and `lastLoggedInAt` is null at that point.
Impact: A member who last signed in years ago, long enough that their sessions had
        expired, had their recorded date overwritten with NULL. The one piece of
        evidence about when they were last active was destroyed, and the trigger
        was somebody else opening a team page.
Pinned: dashboard.test.ts — "erases an old last-login when the member has no live
        sessions", "writes nothing when the member has neither a timestamp nor a
        session"
Fixed:  The destructive arm is gone because the whole backfill is gone — see 23.4.
        The endpoint reports the stored timestamp as it is, old or null.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [23.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — A read endpoint that wrote, one member at a time
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts (getTeamMemberStats)
Was:    getTeamMemberStats ran a `for` loop over the members. Each iteration that
        needed a backfill made one SuperTokens call to list session handles and
        one more per handle, then a prisma write — all sequential, all inside the
        GET.
Impact: `GET /teamStats/:teamId` was a data migration wearing a read endpoint's
        clothes. A twenty-person team with stale timestamps made twenty
        `getAllSessionHandlesForUser` calls plus one `getSessionInformation` per
        live session plus twenty writes, in series, before the page rendered — and
        it repeated on every load until the timestamps stopped being stale, which
        for members with no sessions was never (23.3 nulled them again each time).
        A SuperTokens outage turned a roster listing into a 500: the loop had no
        try/catch, so one failed lookup aborted the whole response.
Pinned: dashboard.test.ts — "makes one SuperTokens round trip per member plus one
        per session, serially", "lets a SuperTokens failure take the whole
        listing down"
Fixed:  The backfill is removed and the endpoint is a read: one team lookup, one
        query, a map. No SuperTokens call, no write, and a SuperTokens outage no
        longer touches it at all — the imports are gone, which a test asserts.
Note:   The backfill was DROPPED rather than moved to a one-off job. Logins write
        `lastLoggedInAt` (config.ts calls updateLastLoggedInTime on sign-in), so
        the column fills itself for anyone who signs in; the only rows it left
        behind are users who have not signed in since before the column existed,
        for whom the true answer is "unknown" and the backfill's answer was
        "whenever their oldest live session started" — or, thanks to 23.3, null.
        If those rows are wanted, a one-off script is the place for it, not a GET.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [23.7] MEDIUM — FIXED — The module depended on a dayjs plugin another module
    registers
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts
Was:    The backfill formatted with `dayjs(lastLoggedIn).utc().format()`. `.utc()`
        comes from dayjs's utc plugin, and dashboard.service.ts never called
        `dayjs.extend(utc)` — nor imported the plugin. Only survey.service.ts and
        response.service.ts do, and dayjs is a module singleton, so extending it
        anywhere patches it everywhere.
Impact: It worked in the running app purely because registerRoutes.ts pulls in the
        survey and response trees, which happen to load first. Nothing expressed
        or enforced that dependency. Loading this service from a leaner entrypoint
        — a worker, a script, a trimmed route set, or a unit test — threw
        `dayjs(...).utc is not a function`, taking the roster with it. It was
        found by writing the tests, which had to register the plugin themselves.
Pinned: dashboard.test.ts — "depends on a dayjs plugin another module registers"
        (and the `dayjs.extend(utc)` the test file had to perform at the top)
Fixed:  The dependency is gone rather than declared: the backfill was dayjs's only
        use in this module (23.4), so the import went with it and the test file no
        longer extends the singleton on the module's behalf. A test asserts the
        service does not mention dayjs at all.
        The wider problem — that survey.service.ts and response.service.ts extend
        a shared singleton other modules then rely on — is untouched and belongs
        to those modules.

[x] [23.5] LOW — FIXED — The active-survey list ordered inside a CTE and read it
    back unordered
File:   src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts (getActiveSurveyStats)
Was:    `WITH filtered_surveys AS (... ORDER BY s."createdAt" DESC)` followed by
        `(SELECT * FROM filtered_surveys WHERE type = 'web' LIMIT 10)
         UNION ALL
         (SELECT * FROM filtered_surveys WHERE type = 'waFlow' LIMIT 10)`.
        Neither branch had its own ORDER BY, and neither did the union.
Impact: SQL does not guarantee that a subquery's ordering survives into the outer
        query, so "the ten most recent surveys of each type" was what this
        reliably produced only by accident of the current plan. A plan change —
        a new index, a different row count — can silently return ten arbitrary
        surveys instead of the ten newest.

        the union", "returns at most ten of each survey type"
Fixed:  Each branch carries `ORDER BY "createdAt" DESC` next to its own LIMIT, so
        each one takes the ten newest of its type by the standard's rules rather
        than by the plan's habits. The CTE's own ORDER BY, which decided nothing,
        is removed.
Note:   The two blocks' order RELATIVE to each other is still unspecified — an
        outer ORDER BY would impose one, but it would also interleave the two
        types, and the union's shape says the caller wants them in blocks. Left as
        it is deliberately; a consumer that cares should sort or group by type.

NOT COVERED — DASHBOARD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - The three statements are raw SQL and are pinned by what is bound into them
    and what the service does with the rows, not by what postgres computes. That
    is exactly the gap 23.1 lived in for six months; see its "Still to do".
  - 23.5 is a claim about SQL ordering guarantees, not an observed misordering —
    reproducing it needs a database with enough rows to change the plan. The fix
    is likewise verified as SQL text, not as a different row order.
  - The `GROUP BY s.id, wf."dbFlowId"` in getActiveSurveyStats selects
    `wf."version"`, which is not in the group list. That IS valid here because
    dbFlowId is WaFlow's primary key (prisma/schema.prisma:1415) and postgres
    recognises functional dependency on a PK — checked rather than assumed, and
    recorded as a non-finding.
  - dashboard.route.ts carries checkSession and nothing else. Unlike most feature
    trees it has no requireFeature gate; the commerce module is guarded the same
    way (issues.txt 19, NOT COVERED), so this is a consistent convention rather
    than a gap unique to this module — and 23.2's per-resource scoping now lives
    in the handler, where a route guard could not have put it.
  - Whether any client depends on the old `GET /teamStats/:teamId` answering 200
    for a foreign team, or on the roster response having refreshed
    `lastLoggedInAt`, was not traced. Both changes are deliberate.

================================================================================
24. FILE UPLOAD MODULE (src/modules/fileUpload)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 24.1 and 24.3-24.8 are fixed. 24.2 — the unauthenticated bulk delete —
is rate limited but STILL OPEN: closing it needs a decision about whether that
endpoint may stay public, and org-prefixed keys to scope it by.

One test file, 28 tests. 294 lines across three files, mounted at
/api/public/upload (registerRoutes.ts:71) — three endpoints, none of them
authenticated: upload a file, upload a file with a thumbnail, and delete files
from the S3 bucket by key.

This is the most exposed module in the codebase. It is unauthenticated by
design — the survey widget and the public forms need it — but the type allowlist
can be switched off by the uploader (24.1) and the delete endpoint accepts any
key (24.2). Both are reachable from the open internet with no credential.

The size and type rules themselves live in src/utils/upload/validate.ts, which
already has its own test file; this section covers what the controllers do with
them.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [24.2] HIGH — STILL OPEN — Anyone can delete any object in the bucket
File:   src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:170-191
        src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.public.route.ts:36-45
        src/registerRoutes.ts:71 (prefix /api/public/upload)
Now:    `PUT /api/public/upload/file/delete` is registered with a body schema and
        nothing else — no checkSession, no preHandler, no hook anywhere in the
        route file. The handler reads `fileKeyArray` from the body and passes it
        verbatim as the `Objects` of a DeleteObjectsCommand against the whole
        bucket.
        There is no ownership check, no key-prefix restriction, and no org in the
        module at all: keys are minted as `${Date.now()}-${sanitizedFilename}`,
        so they carry no tenant information to check against even if someone
        wanted to.
Impact: Any unauthenticated caller who knows or guesses a key can delete that
        object, and the endpoint takes an array so it can be done in bulk. Keys
        are a millisecond timestamp plus the original filename, and the resulting
        URLs are handed to clients on every upload and stored in survey and
        message payloads — so they are neither secret nor hard to enumerate.
        The blast radius is every file the platform has ever stored: survey
        images, uploaded documents, WhatsApp media, thumbnails — across all
        organisations, since the bucket is shared.
Pinned: fileUpload.controller.test.ts — "deletes any key it is handed, in bulk,
        with no ownership check"
Fix:    Authenticate the route; scope keys by org at upload time
        (`${orgId}/${...}`) and refuse keys outside the caller's prefix.

Status: STILL OPEN, and it needs your decision. The endpoint is rate limited per
        IP now (60/hour), which bounds bulk deletion, but it is still unauthenticated
        and any key it is handed is deleted. Closing it means putting checkSession on
        the delete route — which breaks any anonymous client that removes its own
        upload — and, to stop a cross-org delete, minting keys as `<orgId>/<name>` so
        there is something to scope against. Both are calls about what the survey
        widget is allowed to do, so neither was made here.
