[x] [24.1] HIGH — FIXED — The uploader can switch off the file-type allowlist
File:   src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:15-24
        src/utils/upload/validate.ts:32-35
Now:    uploadController parses a `metadata` FORM FIELD out of the multipart
        request and passes `metadata.allowAll` into validateFile. validateFile's
        first statement is `if (allowAll) return { valid: true }` — ahead of the
        type check, the size check and the truncation check.
        The flag is entirely caller-supplied; nothing on the server sets it.
Impact: `POST /api/public/upload/file` with `metadata={"allowAll":true}` accepts a
        file of ANY type, unauthenticated, bounded only by the 100 MB multipart
        limit. The per-type ceilings in validate.ts (5 MB for images, 16 MB for
        audio) are bypassed with it.
        What that is worth depends on how the bucket is served. Files are handed
        back as CloudFront URLs (getFileUrlFromKey), so anything uploaded is
        fetchable over the company's CDN domain — an HTML or SVG file stored with
        its own content type is then hosted, and same-origin content on a
        product domain is the usual concern.
        Even setting content aside, it is unmetered storage on the company's
        account.
        Note the per-type size check is separately toothless: validate.ts reads
        the size from `data.fields.size`, another caller-supplied field, so
        omitting it scores 0. Only the multipart `truncated` flag is real.
Pinned: fileUpload.controller.test.ts — "lets the uploader switch off every check

        already pinned the validator half)
Fix:    Never take allowAll from the request. If some internal caller needs it,
        pass it from server-side code on an authenticated route.

Fixed:  The `metadata` form field is not read at all any more, so `allowAll` cannot
        arrive from a request; nothing server-side ever set it. The unguarded
        JSON.parse that read it (half of 24.5) went with it.

[x] [24.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — Both S3 helpers swallow failures, so failures report success
File:   src/utils/upload/s3Upload.ts:11-36 (uploadFile)
        src/utils/upload/s3Upload.ts:38-46 (deleteFile)
        src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:70-77, :180-186
Now:    uploadFile wraps its work in try/catch and the catch is
        `console.log(e)` with no rethrow — so it RESOLVES to undefined on
        failure. deleteFile's catch is `return err` — it resolves with the error
        object.
        Both controllers then treat a resolved value as success.
Impact: A failed upload answers `200 { message: "File uploaded successfully",
        data: { fileUrl: undefined, key: "..." } }`. The client is told the file
        is stored, is given a key that points at nothing, and typically saves that
        key into a survey or a message — producing a broken reference that only
        surfaces later, when someone tries to load it.
        A failed delete answers `200 { message: "File delete successfully" }`
        whether S3 refused, the bucket was wrong, or the credentials expired.
        The controllers' own try/catch blocks exist but can only fire if the
        helper itself throws, which it has been written not to do.
Pinned: fileUpload.controller.test.ts — "reports success with an empty url when
        the S3 upload failed", "reports success even when the delete failed"
Fix:    Rethrow in both helpers and let the controllers' catches do their job.

Fixed:  Both helpers propagate. uploadFile no longer catches-and-resolves-undefined
        and deleteFile no longer returns its own error, so the controllers' try/catch
        blocks do what they were written to do: a failed upload or delete is a 500
        with a message instead of a 200 and a broken key.
        Fallout handled: shareSettings' deleteFileFromS3 fires without awaiting, so it
        catches and logs rather than becoming an unhandled rejection.

[x] [24.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — A failed transcode escapes the handler and leaks its temp files
File:   src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:36-68 (outside the try)
        src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:78-83 (the finally)
Now:    For audio/mpeg, audio/mp4 and audio/ogg the controller writes the stream
        to ./tmp, runs ffmpeg, and reads the result back — all BEFORE the
        try/catch that wraps only the S3 upload.
        The finally block cleans up only when
        `["audio/mp4","audio/ogg"].includes(params.ContentType)`, and `params` is
        reassigned with the converted mimetype only AFTER a successful convert.
Impact: An ffmpeg failure — a corrupt file, a codec it cannot read, a missing
        binary — escapes the handler as an unhandled rejection rather than a 500,
        and because params still carries the ORIGINAL mimetype the finally's
        check does not match, so both temp files are left in ./tmp. Every failed
        audio upload adds two files that nothing removes. On an unauthenticated
        endpoint that is a disk-fill primitive.
        The cleanup is also fs.unlinkSync inside a finally: if either file is
        already gone it throws from the finally and masks whatever the original
        error was.

        fails", "removes both temp files once the upload finishes"

        params.ContentType; use unlink with a catch.

Fixed:  The transcode moved inside the try, so an ffmpeg failure is a 500 rather
        than an unhandled rejection; cleanup keys off the temp paths themselves rather
        than off params.ContentType, so a FAILED transcode cleans up too; and the
        unlinks are fs/promises with a catch, so a file that is already gone cannot
        throw from the finally and mask the real error.

[x] [24.6] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — Whole files are held in memory and on the event loop
File:   src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:62 (readFileSync)
        src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:80-81 (unlinkSync)
        src/modules/fileUpload/fileUpload.controller.ts:129 (toBuffer)
Now:    The audio path reads the converted file back with `fs.readFileSync` and
        removes both temp files with `fs.unlinkSync`. The thumbnail path calls
        `data.toBuffer()`, materialising the entire upload before sharp runs.
        The multipart limit is 100 MB.
Impact: Every synchronous call blocks the event loop for the whole process — not
        just that request — for as long as the read takes. `toBuffer` holds up to
        100 MB per concurrent request in heap, on an endpoint anyone can call
        without a credential, so a handful of parallel large uploads is enough to
        pressure memory.
        The plain upload path streams correctly (`Body: file`); it is only the
        audio and thumbnail branches that buffer.
Pinned: fileUpload.controller.test.ts — "reads the converted file synchronously",
        "buffers the whole upload into memory first"
Fix:    fs/promises for the read and unlink; stream into sharp.

Fixed:  The audio path reads and unlinks through fs/promises, so neither blocks the
        event loop for the whole process.
Still open:
        The thumbnail path still buffers the whole upload — both the original and the
        thumbnail need the same bytes, so one of them has to be materialised. What
        bounds it now is 24.7's image-only check on that endpoint.

        other silently — unlikely per user, less so across a busy public endpoint.

        other. The dead uploadFileSchema is deleted. All three handlers send

NOT COVERED — FILE UPLOAD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - Whether the S3 bucket is public-read, and what Content-Disposition or CSP
    CloudFront applies, decides how much 24.1 is worth. Neither is in this
    repository — the bucket policy and distribution config are infrastructure.
    The finding is that the server accepts arbitrary types; what happens to them
    afterwards was not established.
  - src/utils/upload/validate.ts already has its own suite
    (src/utils/upload/validate.test.ts) covering the size table, the type list and
    the allowAll bypass. This section does not duplicate it; it covers how the
    controllers feed it.
  - ffmpeg, sharp and the AWS SDK are mocked at their boundaries. What is pinned
    is which codec, size and params they are asked for, not what they produce.
  - The 100 MB multipart limit is set in the route file and is not exercised here;
    the truncated flag it produces is, via validateFile.
  - getFileUrlFromKey and getAWSObject in s3Upload.ts are used by other modules
    and were read only far enough to establish that uploaded keys become
    CloudFront URLs (24.1). getAWSObject has its own defect — it console.logs the
    object body and swallows errors — but belongs to whichever module calls it.

================================================================================
25. EMAIL MODULE (src/modules/email)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 25.5 and 25.7-25.12 are fixed, and all three routes are rate limited per
IP. The four that decide what this module IS — 25.1 (the open relay), 25.2 (the OTP
identity), 25.3 (nothing consults the verdict) and 25.6 (the OtpStore shape) — are
STILL OPEN: each needs either a frontend contract change or a schema migration, so
each needs a decision. 25.4 is partly closed by the rate limit.

Three test files, 98 tests. 430 lines across six files: three unauthenticated
POST routes at /api/email — an arbitrary-email sender, an OTP issuer and an OTP
checker — plus the OTP email template and the general-purpose template that the
team-invite flow also uses.

This module is the most directly abusable surface catalogued so far. The first
finding is that /api/email/send-email will deliver a caller-composed message,
with caller-composed HTML, to a caller-chosen recipient, From the SES-verified
hello@rateup.app, with no authentication and no rate limit. The next three are
that the OTP flow beneath it does not establish anything: the code is issued for
one identity and mailed to an unrelated address (25.2), the verdict is never
recorded or consulted by the code that stores the "verified" answer (25.3), and
a wrong guess costs nothing (25.4).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [25.1] CRITICAL — STILL OPEN — /api/email/send-email is an open mail relay on a verified domain
File:   src/modules/email/email.public.route.ts:6-14 (the route)
        src/registerRoutes.ts:113 (registered at /api/email, no session check)
        src/modules/email/email.service.ts:7-15 (sendEmailService)
        src/utils/email/sendAWSEmail.ts:31 (the hardcoded From)
        src/modules/email/email.util.ts:20-121 (the general template)
Now:    The route takes { toEmail, subject, purpose, text, data } and passes the
        body straight to sendEmailService, which copies to/subject/text verbatim
        and renders html from the caller's `purpose` and `data`. With
        `purpose: "GeneralTemplate"` the caller also supplies data.body,
        data.buttonLink and data.title, and all three are interpolated into the
        HTML with no escaping — body into a <p>, buttonLink into an href, title
        into an <h1>. sendAWSEmail then sends it with
        `from = "RateUp.app <hello@rateup.app>"`.
        There is no checkSession, no preHandler, no API key, no rate limit
        anywhere on the route, in registerRoutes.ts, or globally — plugins/index.ts
        registers no rate limiter at all. CORS restricts browsers; it does nothing
        to a curl.
Impact: Anyone who can reach the API can send mail that is SPF-aligned,
        DKIM-signed and From RateUp's own verified sender, with arbitrary
        subject, arbitrary body text and arbitrary HTML including working links
        to anywhere. That is a turnkey phishing platform aimed at RateUp's own
        customers, who have every reason to trust the domain — and because the
        markup is unescaped, the message can reproduce the real product's
        branding exactly (the template already supplies it).
        The second-order damage is the sending reputation: `toEmail` is
        z.string(), so a comma-separated list is a nodemailer address list and
        one request addresses many recipients. Unmetered bulk send from a shared
        SES identity ends in SES suspension and domain blocklisting, which takes
        every legitimate email with it — OTPs, team invites, campaign mail.
        Nothing in the codebase calls sendEmailService except these routes, so
        whatever the frontend uses this for, it is a client calling an endpoint
        that trusts it completely.
Pinned: email.service.test.ts — "takes recipient, subject and text verbatim from
        the caller", "the caller's markup reaches the html body unescaped",
        "passes a multi-recipient string through untouched", "sets no from,
        reply-to or attachments of its own"
        email.controller.test.ts — "delivers to any address the caller names",
        "adds no authentication, hook or rate limit"
        email.util.test.ts — "puts the link straight into the href with no scheme
        check", "a quote in the link breaks out of the href attribute"
Fix:    This endpoint should not be public. Put it behind checkSession and derive
        the recipient from the server's own records rather than the body; if a
        public sender is genuinely required, restrict `purpose` to an enum, drop
        the caller-supplied html payload, escape every interpolation, and rate
        limit by IP.

Status: STILL OPEN, and it needs your decision. What HAS changed: the route is
        rate limited per IP (10/hour), `purpose` is a z.enum so a caller can no longer
        name a template that does not exist, and every interpolation in both templates
        is escaped with a scheme check on the link (25.9, 25.12) — so the endpoint can
        no longer be used to send RateUp-branded markup of the caller's choosing.
        What has NOT: it will still deliver a caller-composed subject, text and
        recipient from the verified hello@rateup.app, unauthenticated. Closing that
        means putting checkSession on /send-email and deriving the recipient from the
        server's own records, which breaks whatever frontend posts there today.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [25.2] HIGH — STILL OPEN — The OTP is issued for one identity and mailed to an unrelated address
File:   src/modules/email/email.controller.ts:21-36 (sendOTPEmailHandler)
        src/modules/email/email.schema.ts:11-14 (GenerateOtpSchema)
Now:    The body carries `userId`, `surveyId` and `toEmail` as three independent
        strings. The handler mints a code, stores it against `userId`, writes it
        into `data.otp` and sends the message to `toEmail`. Nothing checks that
        the address belongs to the user, nothing looks the user up, and
        request.session is never touched — the route has no session to consult.
Impact: The point of an emailed OTP is to prove the person holds the address.
        Here the caller names both the identity the code is filed under and the
        inbox it is delivered to, so the proof is circular: POST
        { userId: "<victim>", toEmail: "attacker@evil.test" }, read the code out
        of your own inbox, then POST it to /otp/validate with the victim's
        userId and get `{ isValid: true }`.
        The survey frontend appears to use the respondent's email as the userId
        (the stored answer is `verified email Id: ...`), which makes this a
        direct impersonation: claim any address, receive the code somewhere else,
        and the response is recorded as a verified email.
        OtpStore.userId is also @unique on its own (prisma/schema.prisma:1836),
        so one request overwrites whatever code that userId currently holds —
        an unauthenticated caller can invalidate a legitimate respondent's
        in-flight code at will.
Pinned: email.controller.test.ts — "sends the code for one userId to an unrelated
        address", "never consults a session for the identity", "nothing limits
        how many codes one caller may request"
        email.service.test.ts — "issuing a second code for the same user rewrites
        the first"
Fix:    Derive the recipient address from the userId server-side, or bind the
        code to the address it was sent to and check that binding at validate.

Status: STILL OPEN, and it needs your decision. `userId` and `toEmail` are still
        independent fields. Binding them means either deriving the address from the
        userId server-side or refusing a mismatch, and which is right depends on what
        the survey frontend actually sends as `userId` — the NOT COVERED note below
        records that this was inferred, not established. The route is rate limited
        (15/hour per IP), which bounds the code-overwriting half of it.

[~] [25.4] HIGH — PARTLY FIXED — A six digit code with no attempt limit and a ten minute window
File:   src/modules/email/email.service.ts:38-62 (validateOtp)
        src/modules/email/email.public.route.ts:25-33 (the route)
Now:    validateOtp hashes the guess, looks for a matching row and returns a
        boolean. A miss deletes nothing, records nothing and increments nothing.
        The route has no rate limit, and neither does the server. The code is six
        digits (10^6) and lives for ten minutes.
Impact: The whole keyspace can be walked from one client. The endpoint is a
        single small POST with no session to establish, no captcha and no
        backoff, so the only limiter is the API's own throughput — and the
        attacker needs the userId, which under 25.2's reading is just an email
        address. A live code is very likely to fall inside its own window.
        Two design choices make it worse: the code is not consumed on success
        (25.5), so a hit stays valid for the rest of the ten minutes; and the
        code is not invalidated on repeated failure, so guessing never disturbs
        the legitimate user.
Pinned: email.service.test.ts — "a wrong guess neither clears the code nor records
        the attempt"
        email.controller.test.ts — "nothing limits how many guesses one caller may
        make"
Fix:    Count attempts on the row, delete after ~5 failures, and rate limit the
        route by IP and by userId.

Fixed:  /otp/validate is rate limited to 30 attempts an hour per IP and
        /otp/send-email to 15, so 10^6 codes cannot be walked from one address, and a
        validated code is now consumed (25.5) so a hit does not stay usable.
Still open:
        No attempt counter and no lockout — both need a column on OtpStore. A
        distributed caller is slowed, not stopped.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [25.5] MEDIUM — FIXED — A validated code is never consumed
File:   src/modules/email/email.service.ts:48-49
Now:    `if (storedOtp) { if (parseInt(storedOtp.expiryDate) > Date.now()) return true; ... }`
        — the success branch returns immediately. The only delete in the function
        is on the expired path.
Impact: A code that has done its job keeps working for the remainder of its ten
        minutes, for as many checks as anyone cares to make. Combined with 25.2
        (the code is delivered wherever the caller says) and 25.4 (guesses are
        free), a code that leaks — a forwarded email, a shared screen, a mail
        client preview on a locked phone — stays usable rather than being spent
        on first use.
        Rows are also never cleaned up after a successful check, so OtpStore
        accumulates one live row per user indefinitely; nothing sweeps it,
        because expiryDate is a String column (25.6) that no scheduled query can
        compare against.
Pinned: email.service.test.ts — "leaves the code in place after a successful
        check", "rejects and clears an expired code"
Fix:    Delete the row inside the success branch, before returning true.

Fixed:  The success branch deletes the row before returning true, so a code is
        spent on first use and the abandoned rows that used to accumulate after a
        successful check are gone with it.

[ ] [25.6] MEDIUM — STILL OPEN — The OTP table cannot hold one code per survey, and expiry is a string
File:   prisma/schema.prisma:1834-1840 (model OtpStore)
        src/modules/email/email.service.ts:17-36 (storeOtp)
        src/modules/email/email.service.ts:40-46 (the findUnique where)
Now:    OtpStore is { id, userId @unique, otp, expiryDate String, surveyId String? }.
        `userId` is unique on its own; `surveyId` is part of no constraint and
        `otp` is a plain column. storeOtp nonetheless upserts on
        `where: { userId, surveyId }` and validateOtp reads with
        `where: { userId, surveyId, otp }` — in both, only userId can select the
        row and the rest ride along as filters.
Impact: Three separate consequences.
        (a) One row per user, globally. A respondent working through two surveys
        cannot hold two codes; the second issue overwrites the first, or — since
        the upsert's create branch must still satisfy the unique userId when the
        surveyId filter misses the existing row — fails outright with P2002,
        which 25.8 hands back to the caller verbatim.
        (b) Prisma drops undefined keys rather than matching NULL
        (strictUndefinedChecks is not enabled — same root as issues.txt 18.x and
        21.4), so a call that omits surveyId matches the user's row whichever
        survey it was issued for. The surveyId filter is therefore optional in
        the attacker's favour: pass it and it constrains, omit it and it does not.
        (c) expiryDate is a String holding a stringified epoch. No index, no
        comparison, no sweep — expiry exists solely as the parseInt in
        validateOtp, which is why 25.5's abandoned rows accumulate untouched.
Pinned: email.service.test.ts — "keys the upsert on userId with surveyId as an
        extra filter", "issuing a second code for the same user rewrites the
        first", "omits the survey filter entirely when no surveyId is given",
        "writes the expiry as a string, not a date", "filters on userId, surveyId
        and the hash together"
Fix:    Make (userId, surveyId) a compound unique, drop the @unique on userId,
        and store expiryDate as DateTime.

Status: STILL OPEN. All three halves need the schema: a compound unique on
        (userId, surveyId), dropping @unique from userId, and expiryDate as a DateTime.
        The last one is a String→DateTime migration on live data, so it is left for an
        explicit decision rather than pushed.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [25.7] MEDIUM — FIXED — A failed send is indistinguishable from a delivered one
File:   src/utils/email/sendAWSEmail.ts:43-45
        src/modules/email/email.service.ts:14
        src/modules/email/email.controller.ts:28-31
Now:    sendAWSEmail wraps the whole transport call in a try/catch whose catch is
        `log.error(...)` with no rethrow, so it always resolves. sendEmailService
        awaits it and returns undefined either way; both handlers then answer
        `reply.code(200).send(res)`.
        In the OTP handler the code is stored first and sent second, so the write
        has already committed when the send silently fails.
Impact: Every caller of this module is told the mail went out. A respondent who
        never receives a code sees a success screen and a stored code they cannot
        obtain; requesting another only overwrites it (25.6a). An SES throttle, a
        suppression-list hit or a bad address all present identically to a
        delivery, and the only trace is a log line.
        This is the same shape as the S3 helpers in issues.txt 24.3 — a shared
        utility written not to throw, so the callers' own error handling can
        never run.
Pinned: email.service.test.ts — "would propagate a transport error if the
        transport ever threw" (with the NOTE recording that it never does),
        "resolves with nothing, so the handler answers 200 with an empty body"
        email.controller.test.ts — "stores the code before attempting to send it"
Fix:    Rethrow from sendAWSEmail and let the handlers report the failure.

Fixed:  sendAWSEmail returns whether the message went out, and sendEmailService
        throws when it did not — so a failed send is a 500 rather than a 200.
        A boolean rather than a throw deliberately: several callers (the Stripe hooks,
        the subscription-expiry mailers) fire sendAWSEmail without awaiting it, and
        turning those into unhandled rejections would be a worse failure than the one
        being fixed. Those callers still ignore the result, as they did before.

[x] [25.12] LOW — FIXED — Templates interpolate caller data unescaped, including the shared one

        makes the relay a phishing tool rather than a spam tool. Independently,
        createGeneralEmailBodyTemaple is also used by the team-invite flow
        (team.service.ts:227 and :856), where the interpolated values come from

NOT COVERED — EMAIL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - generateOTP is CORRECT and is recorded here as a non-finding so it is not
    re-litigated: it uses crypto.randomInt (not Math.random), the range
    `randomInt(0, 10**6)` is uniform over 000000-999999 inclusive, and padStart
    preserves the full width for small draws. Its only rough edge is that
    randomInt cannot represent a range wider than 2^48, so a length of 15 or
    more throws — pinned, but unreachable at the one call site, which passes 6.
    The stored code is sha256'd, so a database read does not yield live codes.
    Unsalted and unstretched is defensible for a ten-minute six-digit value
    whose keyspace is trivially enumerable anyway (25.4 is the real exposure);
    it does mean identical codes have identical digests across users.
  - 25.6(a) is established from the schema: userId @unique permits at most one
    row per user, so per-survey codes cannot be stored. Whether Prisma's upsert
    resolves the surveyId-mismatch case by raising P2002 or by overwriting
    depends on whether it can use a native INSERT ... ON CONFLICT with a
    non-unique field in the where, which needs a live database to settle. Both
    outcomes are bad and both are described; the tests pin the arguments, not
    the resolution.
  - Whether the survey frontend sends the respondent's email address as `userId`
    was inferred from response.service.ts:1409 storing the answer as
    "verified email Id: ..." and from there being no other identity on a public
    survey. The frontend is outside this repository. 25.2 holds either way — a
    code delivered to a caller-chosen address proves nothing regardless of what
    the userId denotes — but the impersonation reading depends on it.
  - Whether SES is in sandbox mode, and what sending quota and suppression rules
    the account has, is infrastructure outside this repository. 25.1 says the
    application imposes no limit; a sandboxed SES identity would restrict
    delivery to verified addresses and blunt it considerably. This was not
    assumed either way.
  - The three routes are exercised through their handlers and through the
    registration function; no request is put through a real Fastify instance, so
    Zod's rejection of a malformed body is pinned by parsing the schemas
    directly rather than by observing a 400.
  - What the frontend does with `{ isValid: false }` — whether it blocks the
    respondent, and for how long — was not traced. 25.3 is about the server
    keeping no record either way.
  - sendAWSEmail's transport is mocked throughout. That SES is reached, and that
    the From header is what the module claims, is read from
    src/utils/email/sendAWSEmail.ts:31 rather than observed.

================================================================================
26. INTEGRATIONS MODULE (src/modules/integrations and subfolders)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all three CRITICALs (26.1, 26.2, 26.3) are fixed, along with 26.4-26.9
and 26.12-26.17. 26.10 (Shopify's privacy webhooks) and 26.11 (the PetPooja order
event) are STILL OPEN — both are decisions about features, not corrections — and
26.18 is half fixed: the cron route is gone, the shared plan id is not.

Six test files, 194 tests. 8,594 lines across 27 files — the largest module in
the codebase. It holds the credential store that every third-party connection
depends on (Integration.credential, a Json column carrying OAuth refresh
tokens, Shopify Admin API session tokens, Zoho tokens and Zapier key hashes)
plus five integration trees: Shopify, Zapier, Zoho, PetPooja and Meta
Conversions.

Three findings are unauthenticated or cross-tenant paths into that credential
store, and they are the three worst entries in this document so far.

  - 26.1 — the entire Zapier API, seventeen endpoints covering contacts,
    messaging and team invitations, is registered with no authentication and
    takes its target organisation from the query string.
  - 26.2 — GET /api/integrations/all returns every organisation's integration
    row, credential column included, to any authenticated user.
  - 26.3 — PUT /api/integrations/shopify/session/:shop re-parents any Shopify
    store, and its Admin API token, to the caller's organisation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [26.1] CRITICAL — FIXED — The whole Zapier API is unauthenticated and org-selectable
File:   src/modules/integrations/external/zapier/zapier.route.ts (the whole file)
        src/modules/integrations/external/zapier/zapier.controller.ts:45-105
        src/modules/integrations/external/external.route.ts (no hook)
        src/registerRoutes.ts:125 (prefix /api/integration/external)
Now:    zapierAuthenticationHandler reads a Bearer key, bcrypt-compares it
        against the org's stored hash, and on success assigns
        `request.user = { orgId, role: SUPER_ADMIN }` — then replies only `if
        (request.url.includes("/auth"))`. That is the shape of a preHandler.
        It is registered as the HANDLER of POST /auth and nowhere else. A search
        across src finds exactly two occurrences: its definition and that one
        route. zapierRoutes contains no addHook and no preHandler on any of its
        eighteen routes; externalIntegrationRoutes adds nothing; registerRoutes
        only maps plugins to prefixes.
        Every remaining handler therefore runs with no authentication, and each
        takes its organisation from `request.query.orgId` rather than from a
        session — because it was written expecting the preHandler to have
        already proven the caller owns that org.
Impact: Seventeen endpoints against any organisation in the platform, chosen by
        query string, by anyone who can reach the API:
          POST /invite-member    — invite an arbitrary address into an arbitrary
                                   org's team. This is account takeover: name
                                   the victim org, name any member's email as
                                   invitedByEmail so the invite looks internal,
                                   and receive a working invitation.
          GET  /sample-data      — dump contacts with names, emails and phone
                                   numbers, paginated, for any org.
          GET/DELETE /contact    — read, search and DELETE any org's contacts.
          POST /send-message     — send WhatsApp messages as any org, from its
                                   verified business number, at its expense.
          POST /subscribe        — register an arbitrary hookUrl against any org
                                   and receive its contact, message and ticket
                                   events from then on.
          POST /contact, PUT /contact, PUT /contact-lifecycle, POST /ticket,
          POST /add-group-member, GET /teams, /group, /lifecycle,
          /ticket-boards, /ticket-column — the rest of the CRM surface.
        The by-id variants are worse still: getContactHandler with a contactId
        calls the contact module's getContactById, which is not org-scoped
        (issues.txt 18.5), so no orgId is needed at all — and deleteContactById
        likewise.
        Nothing about this is subtle to exploit; the org id appears in ordinary
        API responses and the endpoints are documented by the Zapier app.
Pinned: zapier.test.ts — "adds no hook and no preHandler to anything",
        "registers eighteen routes, of which exactly one is the auth endpoint",
        "the parent adds nothing either", "every route other than /auth carries
        a data-bearing handler", "send-message dispatches WhatsApp for whatever
        org is named", "send-message never looks at request.user",

Fix:    `server.addHook("preHandler", zapierAuthenticationHandler)` at the top of
        zapierRoutes, and take orgId from request.user rather than the query
        string once it is there. The auth handler itself is sound — it is simply
        not wired in.

Fixed:  `server.addHook("preHandler", zapierAuthenticationHandler)` at the top of
        zapierRoutes. The handler was already sound — it bcrypt-compares the Bearer key
        against the named org's stored hash and sets request.user — it was simply
        never wired in. It returns its reply for the /auth probe, which ends the
        lifecycle there, so that route still answers as before.
        The handlers keep reading `request.query.orgId`, which is now safe: the hook
        only lets a request through when the caller's key matches THAT org's hash.

[x] [26.3] CRITICAL — FIXED — Any user can claim any Shopify store and its API token
File:   src/modules/integrations/shopify/shopify.controller.ts:103-121
        src/modules/integrations/shopify/shopify.service.ts:106-116
        src/modules/integrations/shopify/shopify.route.ts:18-26
Now:    `PUT /api/integrations/shopify/session/:shop` reads the shop domain from
        the path and the org from the session, and calls
        `updateShopifyIntegrationByShop(shop, { orgId })`, which is
        `prisma.integration.updateMany({ where: { credential: { path:
        ["session","shop"], equals: shop } }, data })`. The shop's current owner
        is never read: the handler's only lookup is getShopifyInfo(orgId), which
        fetches the CALLER's row, not the target's.
Impact: A shop domain — public information, it is the store's own address — is
        the entire authorisation. Any authenticated user PUTs it and the
        Integration row holding that store's Shopify session token is re-parented
        to their organisation. From that moment every Shopify endpoint in the
        module resolves through getShopifyInfo(attackerOrg) and operates on the
        victim's store: read products and orders, list and delete webhooks,
        create draft orders, capture payments, create fulfilments.
        The victim org silently loses the connection, and because the handler
        deletes the caller's own Shopify row first when it differs, one request
        both drops whatever the attacker had and takes the target.
        updateMany also means every row matching that shop is rewritten, not one.
Pinned: shopify.test.ts — "re-parents a shop named in the path to the caller's
        org", "never reads the shop's current owner before overwriting it",
        "deletes the caller's existing shopify row before claiming another shop",
        "selects rows by the shop domain alone", "updates every matching row,
        not one"
Fix:    Only allow the claim when the row has no orgId (the OAuth callback
        creates it unowned), and prove the caller controls the store — the
        session state from the OAuth handshake is the natural token.

Fixed:  Only an UNOWNED row can be claimed — `orgId: null`, the state the OAuth
        callback leaves it in — so a shop domain is no longer the whole authorisation.
        A shop another org already owns answers 409.
        The caller's own Shopify row is no longer deleted first: an org that already
        has a different store gets a 409 telling it to disconnect, so a failed claim

        tripped mid-claim).

[x] [26.5] HIGH — FIXED — Rotating a Zapier API key cannot succeed
File:   src/modules/integrations/integrations.service.ts:281-297
Now:    The same missing destructure carries `updateKey` — a control flag, not a
        column — into the Prisma update data. Prisma rejects unknown arguments,
        so `prisma.integration.update({ data: { credential, updateKey: true } })`
        throws `Unknown argument 'updateKey'`.
        The rotation itself happens first: a new key is generated and its hash
        written into data.credential.accessToken before the doomed write.
Impact: The documented way to rotate an org's Zapier key — PUT with
        `updateKey: true` — always 500s. The org cannot rotate a leaked key
        through the product at all, which matters precisely because 26.2 hands
        every org's key hash to any authenticated user.
        The generated key is also returned to the caller in the response the
        handler never sends, so a partial failure could leave the operator
        believing a rotation happened.
        Related: `updateIntegrationByName(orgId, "zapier", { updateKey: true })`
        with no credential throws a TypeError one line earlier, because the
        zapier branch assigns into data.credential unguarded.

        rotate when updateKey is absent", "only the exact zapier name triggers
        rotation"

Fixed:  `updateKey` is destructured out before the update is built, so the rotation
        write is one Prisma will accept. The missing-credential throw one line above is
        unchanged — it is the right refusal.

        hookUrl it actually holds; a URL that matches nothing is a no-op, so the SSRF
        primitive is gone. The route also declares a body schema, which it had none of.

[x] [26.13] MEDIUM — FIXED — The Zapier contact-search schema rejects every valid request
File:   src/modules/integrations/external/zapier/zapier.schema.ts:91-103
Now:    `.refine((data) => !data?.contactId.length && !data?.phone.length, {
          message: "Either contactId or phone number should be provided" })`
        The predicate passes only when BOTH values are empty — the inverse of its
        own message. Both fields are `.optional()`, and `.length` is read without
        a guard, so a request that omits one throws inside the refine rather than
        failing validation.
Impact: GET /contact and DELETE /contact are the search and delete actions of the
        published Zapier app. `?contactId=x` and `?phone=y` are both rejected as
        "Either contactId or phone number should be provided"; omitting either
        raises a TypeError out of the validator. The only input that validates is
        one supplying two empty strings, which the handler cannot act on.
        Both endpoints are therefore broken for every real Zap — which is
        probably the only reason 26.1 has not already been exploited through
        them, and is not a control.
Pinned: zapier.test.ts — "rejects a search that supplies a contactId", "rejects a
        search that supplies a phone", "accepts only the search that supplies
        neither", "throws rather than failing when a field is omitted", "throws
        on an empty object too"
Fix:    `(data) => Boolean(data.contactId?.length || data.phone?.length)`.

Fixed:  `(data) => Boolean(data?.contactId?.length || data?.phone?.length)`. The
        search and delete actions of the published Zapier app accept a contactId or a
        phone again, and reject a request that supplies neither.

NOT COVERED — INTEGRATIONS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - This is 8,594 lines across 27 files and the tests are not exhaustive. They
    concentrate on the credential store, the route/auth topology of all four
    trees, and the pure functions. Large stretches of shopify.service.ts (the
    order/draft-order/fulfilment REST and GraphQL calls, resolveShopifyVariants'
    four-step matching, createShopifyProduct, fetchShopifyShippingRates) and
    almost all of zoho.service.ts (1,420 lines of field mapping and token
    refresh) are read but not pinned.
  - 26.1 is established from the route files and a search for
    zapierAuthenticationHandler across src, not by issuing an HTTP request.
    Fastify's encapsulation is the mechanism that would matter if a hook existed
    somewhere; none does in this tree, and registerRoutes adds none globally.
  - 26.3 and 26.4 pin the Prisma arguments, not the resulting rows. Confirming
    the write lands requires a database.
  - 26.8: whether Shopify rejects a callbackUrl with a trailing space at
    subscription time or accepts it and delivers to the encoded path is Shopify's
    behaviour, not this repository's. The finding is that the URL registered is
    not the URL served; which of the two failure modes results was not
    determined.
  - 26.9(a) is proven from the code alone — the read targets a field the query
    does not select — rather than from a live Shopify response. Whether Shopify
    additionally rejects `edges { nodes }` as an invalid selection was not
    tested.
  - 26.10 states what the handlers do. Whether Shopify's app-review process
    currently enforces these three webhooks, and what the merchant's own
    obligations are, is outside the repository.
  - handleSubscriptionUpdateFromShopify calls activateSubscriptionThroughShopify
    WITHOUT await (shopify.service.ts:805), so a failure there is an unhandled
    rejection while the webhook reports success — and the function writes Order,
    Payments and Subscription rows. It also hardcodes a 29-day expiry regardless
    of the billing interval it queries. Both are recorded here rather than as
    numbered findings because the billing tables belong to the subscription
    module, which has not been covered yet.
  - handleShopifyOrderEvents' FULFILLMENT_ORDERS_CANCELLED branch opens with
    `let hasNextPage = false; while (hasNextPage)`, so the loop never runs and
    the event always returns at the "no customer phone" check. Inside that dead
    loop is `data.data.fulfillmentOrder.order.cutsomer.phone` — a typo the dead
    loop conceals. Recorded as a non-finding only because the outer effect is
    "the event does nothing", which the topic's own survey config would also
    produce.
  - The Shopify webhook signature check is the library's
    (shopify.webhooks.process), not this module's, and was not exercised.
    shopifyWebhooksHandler catches everything and never replies on the error
    path, so a failed verification leaves the request without a response.
  - The Zoho webhook does verify a per-org shared secret — the only webhook in
    the module that authenticates its caller. It is a stored random id compared
    with !==, not an HMAC over the body, so it is replayable by anyone who has
    seen one delivery; that is noted rather than raised, since the token is
    generated per integration and travels over TLS.
  - callWaAmeliaApi declares `Promise<AxiosResponse>` and returns `res?.data`,
    and sets `validateStatus: () => true`. getWaAmeliaTimeSlot then catches and
    returns undefined. The Amelia integration has no route in this module and
    was not traced further.
  - Whether the Zapier endpoints are reachable from the public internet or only
    from within a VPC is deployment configuration outside this repository.
    26.1's severity assumes the same exposure as every other /api route.

27. LIFECYCLE MODULE (src/modules/lifecycle)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: everything except 27.5 is fixed. 27.5 needs @@unique([orgId, name]) on
the model, a schema change against data that may already hold duplicates.

One test file, 51 tests. 287 lines across four files backing the contact
pipeline stages — New Lead, Engaged Lead, Customer and so on — registered at
/api/lifecycles behind checkSession.

The module is small and mostly conventional, and it contains its own
counter-example: reorderLabels scopes its writes to the caller's org, in one
transaction, with a comment saying why. The update and delete beside it do
neither, which is what makes 27.1 and 27.2 hard to read as anything but
oversights.

[x] [27.1] HIGH — FIXED — Any user can rewrite any organisation's pipeline stages
File:   src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.service.ts:33-40 (updateLifecycle)
        src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.controller.ts:61-79 (its handler)
        src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.route.ts:39-48 (the route)
Now:    `prisma.lifecycle.update({ where: { id: lifecycleId }, data })` — the
        where is the bare id. The handler reads request.params.lifecycleId and
        never touches request.user; the route carries checkSession and no role
        check. reorderLabels at line 62 of the same file writes
        `where: { id, orgId }` with the comment "Ensure the label belongs to the
        Organization", so the omission here is visible against its own neighbour.
Impact: A stage id is enough for a user of any organisation to rewrite that
        stage: its name, emoji, description, colour, ordering, isDefault,
        category and isWon. The whole body is passed through unchanged.
        isWon is not cosmetic. conversions.service.ts:38 reads
        `lifecycle.isWon` to decide whether a contact entering the stage fires a
        Meta Conversions API event, so flipping it on another org's "Cold Lead"
        makes every contact that reaches it report as a conversion to that org's
        Meta dataset — corrupting their ad optimisation with data they did not
        generate. Flipping it off their "Customer" stage silently stops
        conversion reporting altogether.
        category and isDefault drive where contacts sit in the pipeline; renaming
        or recategorising a stage moves every contact in it at once, and there is
        no audit trail of who did it.
        Same family as issues.txt 18.5 / 20.18 / 23.2 / 26.3 — but this one is
        write access to another tenant's CRM taxonomy.
Pinned: lifecycle.test.ts — "selects the row by id alone, with no org
        condition", "the handler never consults the session org", "a cross-tenant
        update can flip the flag that drives conversion reporting", "writes the
        request body through unchanged", "constrains every update to the caller's
        org" (the reorder counter-example)
Fix:    `where: { id: lifecycleId, orgId }` and take orgId from request.user, as
        reorderLabels already does.

Fixed:  `where: { id, orgId }` with the org from request.user, exactly as
        reorderLabels beside it has always done, and P2025 answers 404 — so a stage
        another org owns is indistinguishable from one that does not exist.

[x] [27.2] HIGH — FIXED — Deleting a stage is unscoped, and silently unassigns its contacts
File:   src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.service.ts:51-57 (deleteLifecycleById)
        src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.controller.ts:81-93
        prisma/schema.prisma:876-877 (Contact.Lifecycle, no onDelete)
Now:    `prisma.lifecycle.delete({ where: { id } })`. No org, no check that the
        stage is empty, no soft delete. On the model side,
        `Lifecycle Lifecycle? @relation(fields: [lifecycleId], ...)` declares no
        referential action, and lifecycleId is nullable — so Prisma's default
        for an optional relation is SetNull rather than Restrict.
Impact: Any authenticated user can delete any organisation's pipeline stage by
        id. The delete is not refused when contacts still reference it: every
        contact in that stage has its lifecycleId set to NULL, so the org loses
        both the stage and the record of which contacts were in it. Nothing
        anywhere stores the previous value, so this is not recoverable from the
        application.
        Deleting an org's default stage compounds it — new contacts have no
        stage to land in, and contact.service.ts's createContact already throws
        for any contact with an absent lifecycleId (issues.txt 18.1).
        A stage id that does not exist raises P2025, which falls through to the
        generic 500, so a probe cannot be distinguished from an outage in the
        logs either.
Pinned: lifecycle.test.ts — "deletes by id alone, with no org condition", "the
        handler passes the path id straight through", "nothing checks whether
        contacts still reference the stage", "a missing row answers 500 rather
        than 404"
Fix:    Scope the delete to the caller's org, and refuse when the stage still
        holds contacts (or require a target stage to move them to).

Fixed:  The delete is scoped to the caller's org, and refused with 409 while the
        stage still holds contacts — so their lifecycleId can no longer be NULLed as a
        side effect. A missing or foreign stage answers 404.
        Moving the contacts to another stage first is left to the client, which is the
        one that knows where they should go.

[x] [27.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — Reordering a freshly seeded org clears its default stage
File:   src/modules/lifecycle/lifecycle.service.ts:69
        src/modules/update/update.controller.ts:140-195 (seedDefaultStages)
Now:    reorderLabels derives `isDefault: lifecycle.ordering === 0 &&
        lifecycle.category !== "lost"`. seedDefaultStages writes the five
        starting stages with orderings 1, 1, 2, 3 and 4 — one-based — and marks
        "New Lead" (ordering 1) as the default.
Impact: The seeder and the reorder disagree about where the pipeline starts. A
        client that sends back the orderings it was given — one-based, as
        seeded — sets isDefault to false on every stage in the payload, so the
        first drag-and-drop reorder after signup leaves the organisation with no
        default stage at all.
        There is nothing to restore it: isDefault is written by exactly two
        paths in the codebase, the seeder (which runs once) and this one, and
        the update route cannot set it back org-safely (27.1 is the only way to
        reach it, by id).
        Nothing rejects two entries claiming the same position either, so a
        client that does send zeroes can produce two default stages at once —
        and the seeder itself already writes two stages at ordering 1, so the
        list order between them is undefined.
Pinned: lifecycle.test.ts — "makes nothing default when the orderings are
        one-based", "only position zero is treated as the default", "two entries
        at position zero both become default", "a lost stage at position zero is
        never made default"
Fix:    Pick one convention. Simplest is to mark the first entry of the array
        rather than the one whose ordering happens to be 0.

Fixed:  The FIRST entry of the list (skipping a "lost" one) is the default,
        whatever numbering the client sends — so a reorder of a freshly seeded org no
        longer clears isDefault everywhere, and duplicate orderings can no longer
        produce two defaults.

[x] [27.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — The body schema accepts a stage the database cannot store

Impact: `POST /api/lifecycles` with `{ name, category, color: null }` passes
        validation, reaches Prisma with neither column set, and fails with a
        missing-argument error that the handler reports as
        `500 { message: "Error creating lifecycle" }`. A caller following the
        schema gets an opaque server error instead of a 400 naming the field.
        The requirements are exactly inverted between the two layers: the two
        fields the database insists on are optional here, and the one it allows
        to be absent is the one the schema insists on.

Fixed:  emoji and description are required and color is optional, which is what the
        database says. A body the schema accepts can now be written.

[ ] [27.5] MEDIUM — STILL OPEN — Two handlers promise a uniqueness the model does not have

Now:    Both createLifecycleHandler and updateLifecycleHandler carry

Status: STILL OPEN. Both 409 branches are still unreachable, and two stages of the
        same name in one org are still created without complaint. Making them real
        means adding @@unique([orgId, name]) to the model — a schema change against
        live data that may already hold duplicates, which is a decision plus a cleanup,
        not a correction. The branches are left in place, ready for it.

[x] [27.7] LOW — FIXED — The reorder discards the category the client sent

        and it is honoured only when it is "lost"; otherwise it is recomputed
        from isWon.
Impact: A stage sent as category "won" with isWon false is written back as

        decorative.

Now:    Every other schema in the file is a `z.infer`. LifecycleBodySchema is a
        hand-written interface declaring all eight fields required and
        non-nullable, while the Zod schema it is supposed to describe makes
        ordering, isDefault, description, emoji and isWon optional and color
        nullable.
Impact: updateLifecycle and createLifecycle both take a LifecycleBodySchema and
        pass it straight into Prisma, so TypeScript believes every field is
        present on the way in. That is exactly the assumption 27.4 breaks at
        runtime: the type says emoji is a string, the validator lets it be
        absent, and Prisma rejects the write.
        The interface is also the thing that would have caught 27.4 had it been
        a z.infer.

        "that body reaches Prisma with both columns absent"

        so the type now describes the body the validator accepts, which is what would
        have caught 27.4.

NOT COVERED — LIFECYCLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 27.2's central claim — that deleting a stage NULLs its contacts rather than
    being refused — is read from prisma/schema.prisma:876-877, where the
    relation is optional and declares no onDelete, and Prisma's documented
    default for an optional relation is SetNull. It was not observed against a
    database, and the tests pin only that nothing in the application checks for
    referencing contacts first. If a migration has since set an explicit action
    on that foreign key, the severity changes but the missing org scope does not.
  - Whether a partial reorder can leave an org with two default stages depends
    on what the frontend sends. The tests pin that the server accepts duplicate
    orderings and a partial list; what the UI actually posts is outside this
    repository.
  - Nothing in src reads Lifecycle.isDefault at runtime — a search finds only
    the seeder that writes it and the reorder that recomputes it. Its
    consequences are therefore inferred from the field's name and from the
    listing endpoints that expose it to the frontend, not traced to a consumer.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked; what is pinned is the argument each writer
    builds, not the rows that result.
  - lifecycle.route.ts registers /reorder before /:lifecycleId, so the literal
    wins and no stage can be shadowed by it. Checked rather than assumed, and
    recorded as a non-finding.
  - seedDefaultStages lives in the update module and writes two stages at
    ordering 1, so `orderBy: { ordering: "asc" }` has no defined order between
    "New Lead" and "Cold Lead". Recorded here because it feeds 27.3, but the
    seeder itself belongs to a module not yet covered.

28. TICKET BOARD AND BOARD LABEL (src/modules/ticket/ticketBoard,
    src/modules/ticket/ticketBoardLabel)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 28.1, 28.2, 28.4, 28.6 and 28.7 are fixed. 28.3 is half fixed — a board
can no longer be left with no teams, but deletion is still gated on membership
alone, since which role should be required is a policy decision. 28.5 (no creator
and no orgId on TicketBoard) is a schema change and STILL OPEN.

Two test files, 85 tests. 476 lines in ticketBoard across five files and 216 in
ticketBoardLabel across four, registered under /api/tickets behind checkSession
and requireFeature(TeamFeature.tickets).

TicketBoard carries no orgId (prisma/schema.prisma:1539-1552). The only tenant
boundary in either subfolder is team membership, expressed by isBoardMember and
enforced by the verifyBoardAccess preHandler. The board subfolder implements
that correctly on all four of its by-id routes. The label subfolder does not use
the preHandler at all: it re-implements the check inside each handler, reading a
property the session does not set — so the check passes for everyone (28.1).

[x] [28.1] CRITICAL — FIXED — Every board-label endpoint authorises any authenticated user
File:   src/modules/ticket/ticketBoardLabel/ticketlabel.controller.ts:17, :32,
        :50, :65, :83 (all five handlers)
        src/utils/checkSession.ts:20-23 (what the session actually sets)
        src/modules/ticket/ticketBoard/board.service.ts:166-184 (isBoardMember)
Now:    checkSession sets two things on the request:
          `(request as any).userId = userId;`
          `(request as any).user = { userId, role, orgId, email };`
        There is no `id` on request.user. Every handler in the label controller
        reads it anyway:
          `if (!user || !(await isBoardMember(ticketBoardId, user.id)))`
          `if (!user || !(await labelService.verifyLabelAccess(id, user.id)))`
        so the value passed to both membership checks is always undefined.
        Inside isBoardMember that lands in
          `prisma.teamMember.findFirst({ where: { teamId: { in: [...] },
             userId: undefined } })`
        and Prisma drops undefined keys rather than matching NULL
        (strictUndefinedChecks is not enabled — the same root as issues.txt 18.x,
        21.4, 25.6b). The filter collapses to "any member of any team attached to
        this board", which is true of every board that has a team with a member
        in it. verifyLabelAccess degrades identically.
Impact: The 403 in all five handlers is unreachable for any board in normal use.
        A board id — which appears in the URL of any board page and in
        /api/tickets/boards responses — is enough for any authenticated user of
        any organisation to:
          GET    /api/tickets/boards/:id/labels   read another org's label set,
          POST   /api/tickets/boards/:id/labels   add labels to their board,
          PUT    /api/tickets/labels/:id          rename and recolour theirs,
          DELETE /api/tickets/labels/:id          delete one, and
          DELETE /api/tickets/boards/:id/labels   delete them in bulk.
        Deleting a label cascades TicketLabelMap (prisma/schema.prisma:1567-1569),
        so it also strips that label off every card on the victim's board. The
        label set is how a support team triages, so this is destructive to their
        working state, not just to a lookup table.
        The check holds only in the cases where it was never going to matter — a
        board with no teams, or teams with no members, has nothing to protect.
        The sibling board subfolder calls the same helper correctly, with
        request.userId, so both readings of the property exist side by side in
        one module.
Pinned: ticketlabel.test.ts — "passes undefined as the user id to isBoardMember",
        "the membership query degrades to any member of the board", "lets a
        non-member read another board's labels", "lets a non-member create a
        label on another board", "lets a non-member bulk delete another board's
        labels", "lets a non-member rename another board's label", "the same
        undefined reaches the by-id check", "still refuses when the board has no
        members at all", "the correct property yields a real membership filter"
        board.test.ts — "the preHandler reads request.userId, which the session
        sets"
Fix:    `user.userId` in all five handlers — every other module in the codebase
        reads request.user.userId. Better, drop the in-handler checks and put
        verifyBoardAccess / a label equivalent in a preHandler, as the board
        subfolder does. Enabling Prisma's strictUndefinedChecks would have made
        this a runtime error rather than an open door.

Fixed:  All five handlers read `user.userId`, the property checkSession actually
        sets — corrected in an earlier typing pass and pinned here. The membership
        checks now receive a real user id, so the 403 they were written to produce is
        reachable, and Prisma no longer drops the filter.

[x] [28.2] HIGH — FIXED — Any user can attach any team to a board they create or own

        carried, and it sits behind verifyBoardAccess, which only asks whether
        the caller is on the board — not whether they are on the teams they are
        adding.
Impact: Two directions, both cross-tenant.
        Outward: POST /api/tickets/boards/create with

        reach (own team) and that appears in the board listing of every member
        of the victim's team, because getBoards selects boards by the caller's
        team memberships. The victim's staff see an unexplained board in their
        ticket UI and may file real tickets on it; the attacker reads everything
        on it.
        Inward: the same teamIds trick on PUT /:id attaches the attacker's team
        to a board they already share, or attaches a victim team to their own.
        Once a team is attached, getBoardById returns that board's full contents
        — every card, comment, assignee, and the linked contact's name and phone
        number, plus the contact behind each survey response.

        board now spans two organisations.

Fix:    Restore the team check on create and apply the same one to the teamIds
        in update: every id in the list must be a team the caller belongs to.

Fixed:  Both writers check every incoming team id against the caller's own
        memberships and refuse with 403 otherwise — the check that was commented out on
        the create route, applied to the update path as well, where verifyBoardAccess
        could never have covered it.

NOT COVERED — TICKET BOARD AND BOARD LABEL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 28.1's mechanism is Prisma dropping an undefined key from a where clause.
    That behaviour is established from this codebase's own configuration
    (strictUndefinedChecks is not enabled) and is the same root as several
    earlier findings, but the resulting SQL was not observed against a database.
    The tests pin that undefined is what reaches the query and that the helper
    returns true when the row it finds belongs to somebody else.
  - Whether a board in practice always has at least one team with at least one
    member — the condition under which 28.1's bypass succeeds — was not measured.
    A board created with an empty teamIds array (which the schema allows) would
    still refuse.
  - The cascade claims in 28.1 and 28.3 are read from the schema's onDelete
    declarations on TicketLabelMap, TicketBoardTeam and TicketBoardLabel, not
    observed.
  - ticketColumn, ticketCard and the ticket-level comment routes are siblings of
    these two subfolders and were not covered. ticket.route.ts:28 has a second
    commented-out access check (`// preHandler: [verifyTicketAccess]`) on the
    comment route, which looks like the same omission as 28.2 — it belongs to
    the ticket module proper and is left for that pass. card.service.ts:38 also
    has an unused labelIds variable that oxlint already flags.
  - boardRoutes is registered with `prefix: "boards"` — no leading slash. Fastify
    normalises this, and the label subfolder registers its own /boards/... paths
    in a different encapsulation context; no collision results because the paths
    differ (/boards/:id/labels vs /boards/:id/team-members). Checked rather than
    assumed, and recorded as a non-finding.
  - getBoardTeamMembersById returns id, name and profilePhoto and deliberately
    no email address, unlike the dashboard module's roster (issues.txt 23.2).
    Recorded as a non-finding so the contrast is not lost.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked throughout; what is pinned is the argument each
    function builds, not the rows that result.

================================================================================
29. TICKET CARD AND COLUMN (src/modules/ticket/ticketCard,
    src/modules/ticket/ticketColumn)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: everything except 29.6 is fixed — every route in both subfolders now
carries an access check. 29.6 needs somewhere for a deletion record to live that
does not cascade with the card, which is a small schema decision.

Two test files, 89 tests. 1,434 lines in ticketCard across five files and 405 in
ticketColumn across five, registered under /api/tickets/cards and
/api/tickets/columns behind checkSession and requireFeature(TeamFeature.tickets).

Both subfolders follow the pattern section 28 established: a verify*Access
preHandler on every by-id route, resolving card → column → board → team
membership, and both read request.userId correctly. The gap is elsewhere and it
is identical in both: the routes that take their target from the REQUEST BODY
rather than from the path — create and reorder — carry no access check at all,
because there is no path id for a preHandler to check.

[x] [29.1] HIGH — FIXED — Create and reorder are unguarded in both subfolders
File:   src/modules/ticket/ticketCard/card.route.ts:30-41 (POST /create)
        src/modules/ticket/ticketCard/card.route.ts:104-112 (PUT /reorder)

        src/modules/ticket/ticketColumn/column.route.ts:90-98 (PUT /reorder)

        src/modules/ticket/ticketColumn/column.service.ts:101-117 (reorderColumns)
Now:    Four routes, none with a preHandler. Each takes its target from the body:
        createCard reads ticketColumnId, createColumn reads ticketBoardId, and
        both reorders take a bare array of `{ id, order }`. The services do not
        compensate — reorderCards updates `where: { id }` and reorderColumns the
        same, with no board condition anywhere.
Impact: Any authenticated user with the tickets feature can, against any board on
        the platform:
          POST /api/tickets/columns/create  — add a column to it,
          POST /api/tickets/cards/create    — add a card to any of its columns,
          PUT  /api/tickets/cards/reorder   — rewrite the order of its cards,
          PUT  /api/tickets/columns/reorder — rewrite the order of its columns.
        Reordering another team's board is a denial-of-usability: a kanban board
        whose columns and cards shuffle is unusable, and nothing records who did
        it (the reorders write no activity).
        Creating is worse than noise. createCard fires the board's configured
        notifications — email and WhatsApp to every notificationRecipient — and
        raises the TICKET_CREATED Zapier event. All of it is stamped with the
        CALLER's orgId, taken from their session, not the org that owns the
        board. So an outsider's card delivers mail to the victim's staff through
        the attacker's WhatsApp account and pushes the card into the attacker's
        own Zapier hooks.
        The board subfolder has the same shape at the top of the tree
        (issues.txt 28.2), so an attacker can create the board, the column and
        the cards without ever holding access to anything.
Pinned: card.test.ts — "takes the target column from the body without checking
        access", "the handler passes the body's column through untouched", "uses
        the caller's org for the events raised on another org's board", "updates
        cards by id alone, with no board condition", "leaves create and reorder
        with no access check"
        column.test.ts — "takes the target board from the body without checking
        access", "updates columns by id alone, with no board condition", "leaves
        create and reorder with no access check", "writes without reading the
        current order"
Fix:    Resolve the board from the body's target and run the same membership
        check the by-id routes use, in a preHandler or at the top of each
        service.

Fixed:  Four new body-based preHandlers — verifyColumnAccessFromBody and
        verifyCardsReorderAccess in the card subfolder, verifyBoardAccessFromBody and
        verifyColumnsReorderAccess in the column one — resolve the target from the
        BODY and run the same membership check the by-id routes use. Every route in
        both subfolders now carries an access check.

NOT COVERED — TICKET CARD AND COLUMN

  - card.service.ts is 944 lines and roughly half of it is activity logging for
    checklists, attachments and members. The tests cover the branches that carry

    rest; the checklist diffing in particular is pinned only by its shape.
  - 29.4 asserts that Fastify validates params before running preHandlers. That
    is the documented lifecycle order, and the schema/path mismatch is visible
    in the files, but no request was put through a real Fastify instance. The
    tests pin the mismatch itself and what the handler does with the wrong name.
  - 29.7 cannot be settled without a database. Both outcomes are described and
    the tests pin only which statements carry the cast.
  - The cascade claims — TicketCard from TicketColumn, TicketActivity from
    TicketCard — are read from the schema, not observed.
  - Whether reorderCards and reorderColumns are reachable at their registered
    paths given that PUT /:id is registered first was checked: Fastify's router
    prefers a static segment over a parametric one regardless of registration
    order, and idParamsSchema requires a uuid, so "reorder" could not match
    /:id in any case. Recorded as a non-finding.
  - card.service.ts:38 destructures `labelIds` out of the input and never uses
    it, reading data.labelIds directly instead. oxlint already flags it; it is
    harmless because both refer to the same value.
  - autoGenerateTicket calls createCard with a fixed title for survey responses
    and WhatsApp automations. Its callers are in the response and whatsapp
    modules and were not traced here — but note that it reaches createCard, so
    29.1's missing check is on the path those callers use too.
  - The ticket module's own comment routes (ticket.route.ts:25-36) carry a
    commented-out `// preHandler: [verifyTicketAccess]` — the same omission as
    29.1 — and were left for the ticket module pass.

30. TICKET MODULE ROOT (src/modules/ticket)

FIX PASS: all six are fixed, with one half of 30.2 left open: the senders now
respect the service window, but the recipient is still looked up in Contact rather
than User, which is why they rarely fire at all.

sections found holds one more time — the comment route's access check exists as

level: the WhatsApp notifications gate on the inverse of the rule they are
trying to respect (30.2), and three quarters of the schema file describes a

[x] [30.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — The WhatsApp ticket notifications send only when they may not
File:   src/modules/ticket/ticket.service.ts:27-39 (assigned)
        src/modules/ticket/ticket.service.ts:88-100 (created)
        src/modules/dashboard/dashboard.service.ts:20 (what the column means)
Now:    Both senders resolve the recipient and then abort when
          `!contact || (contact.lastReceivedMessageTimeStamp &&
             dayjs().diff(dayjs(contact.lastReceivedMessageTimeStamp), "hour") < 24)`
        so a message goes out only when the contact has NOT written to the org
        in the last 24 hours — or has never written at all.
        lastReceivedMessageTimeStamp is inbound recency: dashboard.service.ts:20
        counts `lastReceivedMessageTimeStamp > NOW() - INTERVAL '24 hours'` as
        the org's active conversations.
Impact: The guard is the inverse of the WhatsApp customer-service window. Inside
        the 24 hours, when a free-form message is the one thing that is allowed,
        it refuses to send; once the window has closed, and for contacts who
        have never messaged the org at all, it sends. The log line calls this
        "message sent within the last 24 hours", which reads as a de-duplication
        rule and is why the inversion is easy to miss.
        There is a second, larger reason these rarely fire: the recipient is a
        staff member, and the lookup is getContactByPhoneNumberAndOrgId — the
        Contact table, which holds customers. A staff phone number is not a
        contact unless it happens to have been added as one, so `!contact` is
        true for most recipients and the notification is dropped before the
        window check is reached.
        The net effect is that board-level WhatsApp ticket notifications are
        mostly silent, and the cases that do send are the ones outside the
        window. The senders pass skipConversationLimit: true, so the platform's
        own rate gate does not catch it either.
Pinned: ticket.test.ts — "aborts when the staff member is not also a contact",
        "refuses to send inside the twenty-four hour window", "sends once the
        window has closed", "sends to a contact that has never messaged the org
        at all", "the boundary of exactly twenty-four hours sends", "marks the
        send as exempt from the conversation limit"
Fix:    Invert the comparison, and resolve the recipient's phone from User
        rather than Contact.

Fixed:  The comparison is inverted: both senders now send INSIDE the 24-hour
        service window and abort once it has closed, which is the rule the guard was
        trying to respect. The log line says so too.
Still open:
        The recipient is still resolved through getContactByPhoneNumberAndOrgId — the
        Contact table, which holds customers — so a staff phone number that is not also
        a contact still aborts the send. Resolving it from User instead is a change to
        what these notifications mean and is left for its own piece of work; it is the
        larger reason these rarely fire.
