NOT COVERED — TICKET MODULE ROOT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 30.2's reading of lastReceivedMessageTimeStamp as inbound recency is taken
    from dashboard.service.ts:20, which counts rows newer than 24 hours as the
    org's active conversations, and from conversation.service.ts:199 and
    contact.service.ts:1859, which both treat its presence as "this contact has
    written to us". Whether Meta in fact rejects the resulting free-form sends
    is outside this repository — what is pinned is that the guard permits sends
    exactly when the window is closed and blocks them when it is open.
  - Whether staff phone numbers are in practice also present in the Contact
    table was not measured. The tests pin that the lookup is against Contact and
    that a miss aborts the send; how often that happens depends on the data.
  - deleteComment is correct — `where: { id, createdById: userId }` means only
    the author can delete — and is recorded as a non-finding so the contrast
    with 30.1 is not lost. createdById is not part of a unique constraint, so it
    rides along as a filter and a miss raises P2025; the security outcome is
    right and only the status code is wrong (30.3).
  - The two notification senders are only reached from card.service.ts, which
    section 29 covers. Their callers' access problems are 29.1 and 29.5; what is
    pinned here is what the senders themselves do.
  - createEmailBodyTicketAssigned and createEmailBodyTicketCreated live in
    src/utils/email/tickets/ and are used as-is; their markup was not reviewed
    for the escaping problems recorded against other templates (issues.txt 16.2,
    22.3, 25.12).
  - The Prisma calls are mocked; what is pinned is the argument each function
    builds.

================================================================================
31. TESTIMONIAL COLLECTION (src/modules/testimonial/testimonialCollection)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all seven entries are fixed. The module had no tenancy at all outside
one listing; every entry point now resolves the collection through its team to
the caller's org, and testimonial ids through their survey response. 31.1's
public-republishing chain is closed at both ends — see 32.1 for the other half.

One test file, 41 tests. 439 lines across four files, registered at
/api/testimonial/collections behind checkSession — plus one handler this
subfolder exports that is registered on the UNAUTHENTICATED tree instead, at
/api/public/testimonials/:id (testimonialPublic.route.ts, registerRoutes.ts:74).

Collections are the "wall of love" widget: a named, themed set of testimonials
that a customer embeds on their own website, so the public read is deliberate.
What makes this module worth a section is what sits on the other side of that
public read. Seven handlers take a collection id, a team id or a list of
testimonial ids from the request, and exactly one of them — the fallback branch
of the listing — consults request.user.orgId. The rest are keyed on the id
alone. TestimonialCollection.teamId → Team → orgId is right there in the schema
and is used in that one place.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [31.1] HIGH — FIXED — Any org's testimonials can be republished at a public URL
File:   src/modules/testimonial/testimonialCollection/testimonialCollection.service.ts:112-133
        src/modules/testimonial/testimonialCollection/testimonialCollection.controller.ts:96-111
        src/modules/testimonial/testimonialPublic.route.ts:6-14
        src/registerRoutes.ts:74
Now:    addMultipleTestimonialToCollection takes a collection id from the path
        and a list of testimonial ids from the body. It reads
        testimonialCollectionMap to skip duplicates and then writes the join
        rows. It never resolves the collection to a team, never resolves a
        testimonial to anything, and the handler never touches request.user.
        getTestimonialCollectionById — the read that follows — includes
        `testimonials: { select: { testimonial: true } }`, the whole row, and is
        registered on the public tree with no session check.
Impact: Three ordinary requests turn another organisation's testimonials into a
        page the attacker controls:
          1. POST /api/testimonial/collections/create — make a collection,
          2. POST /api/testimonial/collections/:mine/testimonials with the
             victim's testimonial ids — accepted unchecked,
          3. GET /api/public/testimonials/:mine — unauthenticated, returns the
             full Testimonial.testimonial JSON.
        Testimonial rows are derived from survey responses
        (prisma/schema.prisma:1448-1457), so the payload is respondent-authored
        content — names and free text a customer gave one business in
        confidence. Step 3 needs no credentials at all, so the resulting URL can
        be handed to anyone.
        The same absence works destructively in reverse: the delete-multiple
        endpoint takes a collection id and testimonial ids with the same lack of
        checks, so an attacker can empty another org's live widget.
        This is the read-primitive shape of issues.txt 29.2, but with a public
        endpoint on the far end rather than an authenticated one.
Pinned: testimonialCollection.test.ts — "links any testimonial id to any
        collection id", "reads only the collection's existing links, never its
        owner", "the handler never consults the session org", "the read that
        follows returns the full testimonial rows", "the by-id read selects the
        whole testimonial, not a projection", "removes links by collection and
        testimonial id alone", "the public tree exposes the by-id read with no
        session check", "the by-id read is absent from the authenticated tree"
Fix:    Resolve the collection's team → org and require it to match the caller's,
        and reject testimonial ids whose survey response belongs to another org.

Fixed:  addMultipleTestimonialToCollection and deleteMultipleTestimonialFromCollection
        both take the caller's orgId now. assertCollectionIsTheOrgs resolves the
        collection through TestimonialCollection -> Team -> orgId, and
        assertTestimonialsAreTheOrgs checks every testimonial id through
        SurveyResponse -> SurveyTeamMap -> Team -> orgId — the same join the by-org
        listing in testimonial.service.ts already used. A foreign collection is 404,
        a foreign testimonial is 403, and neither writes anything.
        The source of the ids the chain needed is closed too: see 32.1.

[x] [31.2] HIGH — FIXED — Update and delete are keyed on the collection id alone
File:   src/modules/testimonial/testimonialCollection/testimonialCollection.service.ts:20-47

        and `prisma.testimonialCollection.delete({ where: { id: collectionId } })`.
        Both handlers read request.params.id and neither reads request.user.
Impact: A collection id is enough for any authenticated user to rename another
        organisation's published widget, replace its theme, replace its entire
        testimonial membership, or delete it outright. All four are visible on
        the customer's own website within a cache cycle, because the public read
        serves whatever the collection currently holds.
        Renaming and re-theming are defacement of a page the victim's visitors
        see. Replacing the membership is 31.1 again through a different door —
        `testimonialIds` on the update path is written with no ownership check
        either. Deleting cascades TestimonialCollectionMap and leaves the
        embedded widget serving `{}` (31.4).
        Same family as issues.txt 27.1 / 28.2 / 29.2, and the module contains
        its own counter-example: getCollectionsByOrgId, twenty lines away,
        filters on `team: { orgId }`.
Pinned: testimonialCollection.test.ts — "selects the collection by id alone",

        "clears the whole membership before writing the new set", "the org
        listing joins through the team"
Fix:    Add `team: { orgId }` to both where clauses.

Fixed:  The update resolves the collection first and then writes with
        `where: { id, team: { orgId } }`; the delete uses the same extended where and
        a P2025 miss becomes 404. testimonialIds on the update path go through the
        same ownership check as 31.1's endpoint, so replacing the membership is no
        longer a second door into it.

[x] [31.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — The public read answers 200 for a collection that does not exist

Now:    The service ends

        and `collection` may be null. Spreading null yields {}, so a miss

        handler sends it with a 200. There is no 404 anywhere in the module.

        from "this collection is empty", so a deleted or mistyped collection
        renders as an empty wall on the customer's site rather than failing
        visibly. Nobody is alerted.
        It also flattens the signal an id-enumeration attempt would produce:
        every id returns 200, so the public endpoint gives no negative response
        to rate-limit or alarm on — which matters because 31.1's third step is
        exactly a request to this endpoint.
        The same read leaks teamId into the public payload, so a widget on any
        customer's website discloses the internal team id that owns it.

        public handler answers 404. The read is a projection — id, name, theme and the

[x] [31.5] MEDIUM — FIXED — Replacing a collection's membership is not transactional
File:   src/modules/testimonial/testimonialCollection/testimonialCollection.service.ts:24-36
Now:    `if (testimonialCollection?.testimonialIds?.length)` then deleteMany
        every map row for the collection, then createMany the new set, then
        update the collection. Three statements, no $transaction.
Impact: A failure between the delete and the create — a bad testimonial id
        raising a foreign-key violation is the likely one, since nothing
        validates them — leaves the collection with no testimonials at all and
        answers 500. The customer's published widget goes blank, and the
        previous membership is not recorded anywhere to restore from.
        The guard is also `?.length`, so an empty array is falsy and skips the
        whole block: there is no way to empty a collection through the update
        endpoint, only to replace its contents with a non-empty set. A caller
        clearing the list in the UI would see no change.
Pinned: testimonialCollection.test.ts — "a failure after the clear leaves the
        collection empty", "an empty testimonialIds array changes no membership",
        "omitting testimonialIds leaves the membership alone", "clears the whole
        membership before writing the new set"
Fix:    Wrap the three writes in prisma.$transaction and treat an empty array as
        an explicit clear.

Fixed:  The clear, the rewrite and the collection update run inside one
        prisma.$transaction, so a failure cannot leave the published widget empty. The
        guard is `testimonialIds !== undefined` rather than `?.length`, so an explicit
        empty array clears the membership and an omitted field leaves it alone.

NOT COVERED — TESTIMONIAL COLLECTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - The parent module — testimonial.controller.ts, testimonial.service.ts,
    testimonial.schema.ts and testimonials.route.ts — was not covered; this
    section is the testimonialCollection subfolder plus the one public route
    that serves it. Where testimonial ids come from, and whether the testimonial
    listing is org-scoped, belongs to that pass and would determine how easily
    an attacker obtains the ids 31.1 needs.
  - 31.1's step 2 assumes the attacker holds valid testimonial ids. They are
    uuids and were not shown to be enumerable from this module. Note that the
    unauthenticated Zapier endpoints (issues.txt 26.1) expose survey and contact
    data for arbitrary orgs, and testimonials hang off SurveyResponse, so an id
    source may well exist elsewhere in the codebase.
  - Whether the public widget is fronted by a CDN, and therefore how long a
    defaced or emptied collection would serve, is deployment configuration
    outside this repository.
  - Testimonial.testimonial is a Json column whose shape is set by the
    testimonial module, not here. The tests treat it as opaque; what it contains
    in practice determines how sensitive 31.1 is.
  - TestimonialCollectionMap cascades from both sides (prisma/schema.prisma:
    1471-1479), so deleting a collection or a testimonial removes the links.
    Read from the schema, not observed.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked; what is pinned is the argument each function
    builds, not the rows that result.

32. TESTIMONIAL MODULE ROOT (src/modules/testimonial)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 32.1, 32.2, 32.4 and 32.5 are fixed. 32.3 is PARTLY FIXED — the
schema is applied, so hollow rows are no longer stored, but the length and
photo-scheme bounds are still open because a bound here silently discards a
respondent's answer rather than rejecting the request.

One test file, 26 tests. The four files at the top of the module — 164 lines —
that section 31 left: two authenticated reads at /api/testimonials, the writer
the response pipeline calls, and the schema file.

Section 31 recorded an open question: 31.1's chain needs testimonial ids, and
nothing in the testimonialCollection subfolder showed where an attacker would
get another organisation's. This section answers it. GET /api/testimonials/
:surveyId is not org-scoped, returns the testimonial id and the full content,
and needs only a survey id (32.1). The chain is complete without leaving the
module.

As in the subfolder, the correctly scoped version of the same query sits a few
lines below the unscoped one.

[x] [32.1] HIGH — FIXED — Any survey's testimonials can be read by any authenticated user
File:   src/modules/testimonial/testimonial.service.ts:30-48
        src/modules/testimonial/testimonial.controller.ts:5-17
        src/modules/testimonial/testimonials.route.ts:11-19
Now:    getAllTestimonialBySurveyId filters on
          `surveyResponse: { surveyTeamMap: { surveyId } }`
        and stops there. getAllTestimonialByOrgId, eighteen lines below, walks
        one relation further —
          `surveyResponse: { surveyTeamMap: { team: { orgId } } }`
        — so the scoped join exists in the same file and is not used here.
        The handler reads request.params.surveyId and never touches
        request.user. The route carries checkSession and nothing else, and the
        params schema accepts any string.
        There is no `select`, so every scalar of Testimonial comes back: the id,
        the responseId, and the whole testimonial Json — the respondent's name,
        job title, free text, rating and photo.
Impact: A survey id is enough for any authenticated user of any organisation to
        read that survey's testimonials in full. Survey ids are not secret; they
        appear in survey URLs and in other API responses.
        This is disclosure on its own — respondent-authored content given to one
        business, readable by any other tenant. It is also the missing step in
        issues.txt 31.1: that finding could republish arbitrary testimonials at
        a public URL but had no shown source of ids. This endpoint is the
        source, and it returns exactly the id field 31.1 consumes. The two
        together are a complete path from "know a survey id" to "serve another
        org's testimonials from a page you control, to anyone, unauthenticated".
        The include compounds it: `surveyResponse: { select: { surveyTeamMap:
        true } }` returns the entire mapping row per testimonial, so the caller
        also learns the internal surveyId, teamId and shortUrlId.

        project the fields the caller needs.

        `surveyTeamMap: { team: { orgId } }` join the by-org read beside it already
        used, so a survey id alone no longer reads another tenant's testimonials. The
        unprojected row and the whole-SurveyTeamMap include are replaced by
        `select: { id, createdAt, testimonial }`, so the internal surveyId, teamId and
        shortUrlId no longer travel with each row.
        This is the id source issues.txt 31.1 needed; both ends are now closed.

        has already answered the respondent. The block sits in a try/catch whose
        handler calls `reply.code(500).send(e.message)` on a reply that has
        already been sent.
        createMany is called without skipDuplicates, against a model whose
        unique key is (testimonial, responseId) — a Json column
        (prisma/schema.prisma:1456).
Impact: Promise.all rejects on the first failure, so anything createTestimonial
        throws abandons whichever of the five operations had not yet resolved.
        The respondent sees no sign of it — their 201 went out before the batch
        started — so a submission can be recorded with its Google Sheet row
        missing, its webhook never delivered, or its Zapier event never queued,
        and the only trace is a console.error plus a duplicate-send warning.
        Two ways in. A survey with two testimonial questions answered
        identically — same name, same text, same rating — produces two rows with
        an equal Json value and the same responseId, which is exactly what the
        unique constraint forbids. And a payload whose response object is shaped
        differently throws before the write.
        Silent partial completion is the harm here rather than a failed
        submission: the org's downstream integrations diverge from the response
        table with nothing reporting the gap.
Pinned: testimonial.test.ts — "does not skip duplicates", "propagates a
        constraint failure to its caller", "throws on a responses array that is
        absent", "writes one row per testimonial answer"
        response.test.ts — "answers 201 before any post-processing runs", "a
        post-processing failure attempts a second send", "one failing side
        effect abandons the rest of the batch"
Fix:    createMany with skipDuplicates, and run the post-response work off
        Promise.allSettled so one failure cannot abandon the others.

Fixed:  createMany passes `skipDuplicates: true`, so two identical testimonial
        answers in one submission no longer raise P2002. And the response handler's
        post-processing batch is Promise.allSettled rather than Promise.all, with each
        rejection logged against the response id — one failing side effect can no
        longer abandon the Google Sheet write, the webhook, the Zapier event or the
        notification email, and the handler no longer attempts a second send on a
        reply that went out at line 256.
        The reply-already-sent 500 itself belongs to section 37 (37.3).

================================================================================
33. SURVEY THEME MODULE (src/modules/surveyTheme)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 33.1, 33.2, 33.3, 33.5, 33.6 and 33.7 are fixed. 33.4 is PARTLY
FIXED — every route validates its params and the two name-carrying bodies, but
the theme JSON stays a declared-but-open object: the module's own page schemas
describe the LEGACY shape and would reject every request the current frontend
sends.

One test file, 45 tests. 740 lines across six files at /api/surveyTheme,
governing how a survey looks: background images, button styling, and the
welcome, survey, thankyou and language page copy.

The copy is stored as raw HTML. defaultTheme.ts ships
`<h3 style="text-align: center">Welcome</h3><p ...>` as the welcome title and
the same shape for the thankyou and language pages, so a theme is not a set of
colours — it is markup that the public survey renderer puts on the page. Every
write in this module is keyed on an id with no organisation condition, and not
one of the seven routes declares a schema.

The module also has an unusual auth arrangement: rather than route-level
preHandlers it installs an `onRoute` hook that assigns `preHandler = [checkSession]`
to everything except routes flagged `isPublic`. That works, and is the reason
the by-id read is the only unauthenticated one.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

        organisation's theme JSON. Because titles are HTML, that is stored

        into a URL by getImgUrl, which prefixes a bare value with a hardcoded
        `http://d2uj946b0i8y27.cloudfront.net/` host but passes any absolute URL

        by every respondent.
        The survey update at the end of the same function is separately
        unscoped: `prisma.survey.update({ where: { id: surveyId } })` repoints
        any survey at any theme, and it runs even when the theme write was
        skipped. The two writes are not in a transaction.

================================================================================
34. WEBHOOK MODULE (src/modules/webhook)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 34.1 and 34.3-34.7 are fixed. 34.2 — unsigned deliveries — is STILL
OPEN: signing needs a secret column on the model, a published HMAC scheme, and a
migration path for existing receivers, all of which are your call.

One test file, 44 tests. 109 lines across four files at /api/webhook, plus the
worker in src/jobs/outgoingWebhook.worker.ts that consumes what it queues: an
organisation registers one HTTPS URL and a list of events, and the platform
POSTs to it when those events occur.

This is the best-scoped module in the document so far. Every read and write
takes orgId from the session and nothing from the request body; the URL must be
HTTPS by schema; and a save is refused unless the receiver echoes a challenge
token back. Nothing here is a tenancy failure.

The findings are of two other kinds. The verification step makes the server
fetch a URL the caller chose (34.1), and the deliveries it later sends are
unsigned and unretried (34.2, 34.3) — so a receiver cannot tell a genuine
delivery from a forged one, and a receiver that is briefly down loses the event.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [34.1] MEDIUM — FIXED — The save endpoint fetches any URL the caller names
File:   src/modules/webhook/webhook.service.ts:21-33 (verifyWebhookUrl)
        src/modules/webhook/webhook.controller.ts:12-15
        src/modules/webhook/webhook.schema.ts:4-6
Now:    `fetch(\`${url}?challenge=${encodeURIComponent(verifyToken)}\`,
           { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000) })`
        The URL is the caller's, validated only as a well-formed URL beginning
        with `https://`. There is no host allowlist, no resolution of the name
        to an address, and no check that the address is public. `redirect` is
        not set, so fetch's default of "follow" applies.
Impact: An authenticated user makes the server issue a GET to a host of their
        choosing, from inside the network. The response body is not returned,
        but the outcome is: the endpoint answers 200 or 400, and those two
        answers separate "reachable and echoed the token" from everything else —
        while the 5-second timeout separates "refused" from "hung". That is
        enough to sweep internal hostnames and ports from the outside.
        The https-only rule is weaker than it looks. Because redirects are
        followed, a caller registers `https://their-host.test/hook`, has it
        answer 302 to `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`, and the server
        follows — the scheme check only ever governed the first hop.
        Rated MEDIUM rather than HIGH because the response body is not echoed
        back, so this reads internal state rather than exfiltrating it, and
        because the request requires a session. Compare issues.txt 26.6, where
        the same primitive exists with no schema at all and an unvalidated body.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "issues a GET to the caller's URL with the challenge
        appended", "fetches an internal host as readily as an external one",
        "sets no redirect policy, so the https rule covers only the first hop",
        "a connection failure is a distinguishable false, and is logged", "a
        timeout is also a false rather than an error", "bounds the request at
        five seconds", "requires https"
Fix:    Resolve the host and reject private, loopback and link-local addresses,
        set `redirect: "manual"`, and re-check the address after any redirect.

Fixed:  verifyWebhookUrl resolves the host with dns.lookup before issuing anything
        and refuses loopback, link-local (including 169.254.169.254), the RFC1918
        ranges, carrier-grade NAT, multicast, and the IPv6 equivalents — a literal IP
        is checked without a lookup, and a name that resolves to several addresses is
        refused if ANY of them is private. `redirect: "manual"` is set, so the https
        rule is no longer limited to the first hop, and the https check is repeated in
        the service rather than trusted from the schema.
Note:   This is application-level egress control. Whether the deployment can reach
        a private address at all is infrastructure, as the NOT COVERED note says; this
        removes the application's own contribution to it.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [34.2] MEDIUM — STILL OPEN — Deliveries are unsigned, so a receiver cannot authenticate them
File:   src/jobs/outgoingWebhook.worker.ts:29-33
        src/modules/webhook/webhook.service.ts:5-11 (what is stored)
        prisma/schema.prisma:2775-2783 (model Webhook)
Now:    The worker POSTs with `headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" }`
        and nothing else — no signature, no timestamp, no shared secret, no
        delivery id. The model has columns for id, timestamps, orgId, url and
        events; there is nowhere a signing secret could live, and the
        verifyToken the caller sends at save time is used for the challenge and
        then discarded rather than stored.
Impact: A receiver has no way to distinguish a delivery from RateUp from a POST
        by anyone who knows the endpoint URL. Webhook URLs leak in the ordinary
        way — browser history, CI logs, a screenshot of the settings page — and
        the receiver is typically wired to act on what it receives: update an
        order, mark a message delivered, notify a customer. Forging one needs
        only the URL.
        The reverse also holds: a receiver cannot replay-protect, because there
        is no timestamp or delivery id to deduplicate on, and the payload
        (message status, recipient phone number) is identical across retries of
        the same underlying event.
        This is the one thing a webhook feature is conventionally expected to
        provide, and the challenge handshake at registration shows the intent to
        establish trust — it just does not extend past setup.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "stores only a url and an event list", "the verify
        token is not persisted", "passes the payload through without filtering"
Fix:    Store a per-org secret at registration and send an HMAC of the body plus
        a timestamp on every delivery.

Status: STILL OPEN, and it needs your decision. Signing deliveries means adding a
        secret column to the Webhook model — a schema change — generating and showing
        that secret at registration, and publishing the HMAC scheme (which header,
        which canonical string, which timestamp tolerance) for receivers to implement.
        Every existing receiver would have to be updated or the signature made
        advisory for a period. None of that is a call to make inside a fix pass.
        The related hardening that did land: deliveries are retried (34.3), the
        registration handshake still proves the receiver is reachable, and the save is
        now admin-only (34.7) so a low-privileged member cannot repoint the stream.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [34.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — A failed delivery is never retried, though the code asks for one
File:   src/modules/webhook/webhook.service.ts:41-51 (enqueueWebhookDispatch)
        src/utils/redisConnection.ts:39-41 (the queue)
        src/jobs/outgoingWebhook.worker.ts:29-38
Now:    The worker throws on a non-2xx — `Webhook POST to ... failed with status
        ...` — which is how a BullMQ processor asks for a retry. But
        enqueueWebhookDispatch passes no job options, and the queue is
        constructed with `{ connection }` and no defaultJobOptions, so BullMQ's
        default of one attempt applies. The throw produces a log line and
        nothing else.
        The outgoing POST also sets no timeout, unlike the verification fetch
        five lines above it in the same module.
Impact: A receiver that is redeploying, rate-limiting, or briefly unreachable
        loses the event permanently. Nothing records the loss beyond a log line,
        and there is no delivery log or replay endpoint, so neither side can
        discover what was missed.
        The missing timeout is the other half: with concurrency 10 and no
        AbortSignal, ten slow receivers occupy every slot in the worker
        indefinitely and stall deliveries for every other organisation. A
        receiver does not have to be malicious for this — a hung TCP connection
        is enough.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "sets no retry policy on the job", "queues a dispatch
        job carrying the phone id, event and payload"
Fix:    Set attempts and an exponential backoff on the job, and give the
        outgoing POST an AbortSignal.

Fixed:  enqueueWebhookDispatch sets `attempts: 5` with exponential backoff from
        five seconds, so the worker's throw on a non-2xx now asks for a retry that
        actually happens. The outgoing POST carries `AbortSignal.timeout(15_000)`, so
        a hung receiver can no longer occupy a worker slot indefinitely — with
        concurrency 10, ten of them used to stall deliveries for every other org.
Note:   There is still no delivery log or replay endpoint; after five attempts the
        event is lost with only a log line, same as before. That is a bigger feature
        than this entry asks for.

[x] [34.4] LOW — FIXED — The optional verify token cannot be omitted
File:   src/modules/webhook/webhook.schema.ts:8
        src/modules/webhook/webhook.service.ts:23, :28
Now:    `verifyToken: z.string().optional()`. When it is absent,
        `encodeURIComponent(undefined)` yields the literal string "undefined",
        so the challenge sent is `?challenge=undefined`; and the comparison is
        `text.trim() === verifyToken`, which is a comparison against undefined
        that no string satisfies.
Impact: A save with no verifyToken always fails with "Webhook URL challenge
        verification failed", whatever the receiver does — including echoing the
        string "undefined" back. The field is optional in the contract and
        mandatory in behaviour, and the error message points at the receiver
        rather than at the missing field, so the caller debugs the wrong end.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "the schema marks it optional", "omitting it sends the
        string undefined as the challenge", "omitting it fails verification
        whatever the receiver echoes", "so a save with no token is always
        rejected"
Fix:    Make it required, or generate one server-side when it is absent.

Fixed:  `verifyToken: z.string().min(1).max(256)` — required, as the Fix line's
        first option. The controller's existing guard stays as a second line for a
        body that reaches the handler unvalidated.

[x] [34.5] LOW — FIXED — The challenge is appended without regard for an existing query
File:   src/modules/webhook/webhook.service.ts:23
Now:    The challenge is concatenated as `${url}?challenge=...` rather than set
        on a parsed URL, so a registered URL that already carries a query string
        receives two question marks.
Impact: `https://host/hook?tenant=1` becomes
        `https://host/hook?tenant=1?challenge=TOKEN`. The receiver sees a single
        parameter `tenant` whose value is `1?challenge=TOKEN`, so a framework
        reading `challenge` finds nothing and the verification fails for a URL
        that is otherwise correct.
        Registering a webhook with query parameters is ordinary — a tenant id or
        a routing key — so this rejects a legitimate configuration and the
        message blames the receiver.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "appends a second question mark to a URL that already
        has a query", "encodes the token into the query string"
Fix:    `const u = new URL(url); u.searchParams.set("challenge", token);`

Fixed:  `const target = new URL(url); target.searchParams.set("challenge", token)`.
        A registered URL that already carries a query gets `&challenge=`, so a webhook
        configured with a tenant id or a routing key verifies.

[x] [34.7] LOW — FIXED — Any member can repoint the org's webhook, and events are unchecked
File:   src/modules/webhook/webhook.route.ts:11-15
        src/modules/webhook/webhook.schema.ts:7
        src/modules/whatsapp/whatsappSurvey/wasurvey.controller.ts:360
Now:    The tree carries checkSession and no role check, so any member of an
        organisation can save, replace or delete its webhook. orgId is @unique
        on the model, so a save silently replaces whatever was there.
        `events: z.array(z.string())` is unconstrained, and the only event the
        platform ever emits is "message_status" — a single call site.
Impact: The lowest-privileged member can redirect the org's outgoing event
        stream to an address they control. The challenge handshake does not
        prevent it; it only requires that the new destination answer. The
        previous URL is overwritten with no record, so nobody is notified that
        deliveries now go elsewhere. Given 34.2, the new receiver's traffic is
        indistinguishable from the old one's to anyone downstream.
        The unvalidated event list is the quieter half: subscribing to
        "order.created" or a misspelled "mesage_status" is accepted and simply
        never fires, with nothing to indicate the name matched nothing.
Pinned: webhook.test.ts — "no route declares a role requirement", "a second save
        replaces the org's existing webhook", "takes the org from the session,

        emits", "accepts an empty event list, which subscribes to nothing",
        "puts no bound on the URL length or the number of events"
Fix:    Require an admin role, and make events a z.enum of what the platform
        emits.

Fixed:  POST and DELETE carry `checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN", "ADMIN"])`; the GET
        is left open to any member, since reading the configured URL is not the
        dangerous half. `events` is `z.array(z.enum(WEBHOOK_EVENTS)).min(1)`, where
        WEBHOOK_EVENTS is the single event the platform emits — so a misspelled or
        invented name is a 400 rather than a silent subscription to nothing, and an
        empty list is refused. The URL is bounded at 2048 characters.
Note:   Requiring an admin role is a behaviour change a non-admin member will
        notice. It follows the Fix line, and matches the gate put on the integrations
        writers in 26.17.

NOT COVERED — WEBHOOK
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 34.1's redirect claim rests on fetch defaulting to `redirect: "follow"` and
    nothing overriding it. That the option is absent is pinned; the follow was
    not demonstrated against a real redirecting host, and whether a private
    address is reachable from the deployment at all is infrastructure outside
    this repository. If egress is already restricted to a proxy, the finding is
    limited to what that proxy can reach.
  - 34.3's retry claim rests on BullMQ's documented default of a single attempt
    when neither the job nor the queue sets `attempts`. What is pinned is that
    enqueueWebhookDispatch passes no options and that redisConnection.ts:39
    constructs the queue with only a connection.
  - The worker itself is exercised only by reading; its behaviour is described
    from src/jobs/outgoingWebhook.worker.ts rather than pinned by tests, since
    it registers a BullMQ Worker at import time. The phoneId → whatsappSettings
    → org → Webhook resolution it performs was read, not run.
  - Whether an org's webhook URL is visible to non-admin members through the GET
    — and therefore whether 34.7's replacement is detectable — depends on the
    frontend; the endpoint returns the row to any member.
  - The Prisma calls and global fetch are mocked; what is pinned is the argument
    each function builds and the request it would issue.

================================================================================
35. SHARE SETTINGS MODULE (src/modules/shareSettings)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all six entries are fixed. 35.1's structural problem is closed from
both sides — the resource name is an enum and the survey queries carry their own
org condition — so the preHandler is no longer the only thing standing between a
caller and another org's data.

One test file, 45 tests. 243 lines across six files at /api/shareSettings: the
QR-code appearance a survey or a generated QR is shared with — colours, logo
size, and an uploaded logo image stored in S3 and served from CloudFront.

Two routes, GET and PUT, both on `/:resourceName/:id`, where resourceName
selects which table is read. That one path segment carries the whole design:
which resource is touched, and — through a string comparison in the preHandler —
whether an access check runs at all. The two resources are protected by
different mechanisms, in different files, and neither protects the other (35.1).

The rest of the module is the logo lifecycle, which inherits the swallowed S3
errors recorded in issues.txt 24.3 and turns them into stored data (35.2).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [35.1] MEDIUM — FIXED — Authorisation depends on a string match against a free-form path segment
File:   src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.hooks.ts:4-10
        src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.service.ts:6-26, :33-59
        src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.schema.ts:3-6
Now:    The preHandler runs checkSession, then
          `if (resourceName === "survey") await verifySurveyAccess(req, rep);`
        The service switches on the same value. The two resources are guarded in
        opposite places:
          - survey: the queries are `where: { id }` with no org condition at
            all, and the ONLY thing scoping them is that preHandler branch;
          - generateQr: no hook check, and the queries constrain
            `ownerOrgId: req.user.orgId` themselves.
        resourceName is `z.string().optional()` — any string, or absent.
Impact: The survey path's entire tenancy is a string comparison on a value the
        caller supplies. Today it holds, and a mismatched name — "Survey",
        "surveys", nothing at all — is refused, but only because the service's
        switch has no `default` case and therefore touches no table. The
        protection is a coincidence of two files agreeing, not a check.
        That makes the module fragile in a specific way: adding a `default:` to
        the switch, or a third resource whose name the hook does not know,
        silently produces an unauthenticated write path. A future
        `case "linktree":` added to the service without a matching branch in the
        hook is org-scoped only if whoever writes it remembers.
        The failure mode when the names disagree is also poor: the controller
        destructures the service's undefined return, so an unknown resource is a
        TypeError and a 500 rather than a 404.
Pinned: shareSettings.test.ts — "the survey read is keyed on the id alone", "the
        survey write is keyed on the id alone", "the QR read and write constrain
        the owning org", "an unrecognised resource touches no table and returns
        undefined", "the comparison is exact, so a differently cased name skips
        the check", "adds the survey access check only for the survey resource",
        "throws for a resource the switch does not know", "the params schema puts
        no constraint on the resource name"
Fix:    Make resourceName a z.enum, and scope the survey queries on orgId the way
        the generateQr ones already are, so the hook is defence in depth rather
        than the only defence.

Fixed:  Both halves, so the hook is defence in depth rather than the only defence.
        `resourceName` is `z.enum(["survey", "generateQr"])` — required — so a
        mismatched or absent name is a 400 at the route instead of a value that
        reaches a switch and happens to match nothing. And the survey queries carry
        their own org condition now, `{ id, OR: [{ orgId }, { teams: { some: { team:
        { orgId } } } }] }`, the same shape used in 33.1. A third resource added to the
        switch without a matching branch in the hook is no longer an unscoped write
        path, and the service's ShareSettingsResource type makes the enum and the
        switch the same list.

[x] [35.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — A failed logo upload stores the four-character string "null"
File:   src/modules/shareSettings/utils.ts:25-27
        src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.controller.ts:26-27
        src/utils/upload/s3Upload.ts:12-33 (issues.txt 24.3)
Now:    uploadFileToS3 does `const key = resultObj ? resultObj["Key"] : null`,
        and uploadFile swallows every error and resolves undefined — so a failed
        upload yields key === null. The controller then writes
          `settingsObj.logoFile = \`${key}\`;`
        through a template literal, which stringifies null to "null".
Impact: The save reports success. The stored settings carry `logoFile: "null"`,
        which is a truthy string, so getSettings turns it into
        `https://cdn.example/null` and the client renders a broken image with no
        indication anything failed.
        It compounds on the next edit: the update path reads the old value,
        finds the truthy "null", treats it as a real S3 key and asks the bucket
        to delete an object called "null" — a delete request against a key that
        was never written, on behalf of an org that never uploaded it.
        Nothing anywhere distinguishes this from a real key, so the state is
        sticky: every subsequent save keeps carrying it forward.
Pinned: shareSettings.test.ts — "a failed upload stores the literal string null",
        "and the save still reports success", "the next update tries to delete an
        object named null", "returns null when the upload helper swallows a
        failure", "uploads a new file and stores the returned key"
Fix:    Throw when the upload returns no key, rather than interpolating it.

Fixed:  uploadFileToS3 throws LogoUploadError when the upload returns no key,
        rather than yielding null for the controller's template literal to stringify
        to "null". The save fails instead of reporting success, so the sticky state —
        a truthy "null" the read turned into `https://cdn/null` and the next update
        handed to S3 as a key to delete — cannot be created.

[x] [35.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — The logo upload validates nothing
File:   src/modules/shareSettings/utils.ts:4-15 (onFile)
        src/modules/shareSettings/utils.ts:17-28 (uploadFileToS3)
        src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.route.ts:9
Now:    onFile buffers the whole part and takes the extension as
        `filename.split(".").pop()`. uploadFileToS3 writes the object with
        `ContentType: mimetype` — the part's own header — and
        `Key: \`${randomUUID()}.${fileExtension}\``. The multipart plugin is
        registered with `{ attachFieldsToBody: "keyValues", onFile }` and no
        `limits`, so no fileSize, files or fieldSize bound is set.
Impact: An authenticated caller controls the content type and the extension of
        an object written to the shared uploads bucket and served from
        CloudFront. Nothing restricts either to images: text/html with a .html
        extension is accepted as readily as image/png.
        Whether that becomes a served HTML page depends on the bucket and
        distribution configuration, which is outside this repository — but the
        application imposes no restriction of its own, and the fileUpload module
        has a validateFile helper (issues.txt 24) that exists precisely for
        this and is not used here.
        The absent multipart limits are the other half: the part is fully
        buffered in memory before anything looks at it, so a large upload is an
        allocation of that size per request.
Pinned: shareSettings.test.ts — "stores the caller's content type and extension
        unchecked", "names the object with a random uuid and the caller's
        extension", "takes whatever follows the last dot", "a filename with no
        dot becomes its own extension", "nothing inspects the buffer's size or
        content", "passes no multipart limits"
Fix:    Allowlist the content type and extension, and pass limits to the
        multipart plugin.

Fixed:  onFile refuses any content type outside an image allowlist (png, jpeg,
        webp, gif, svg+xml) and derives the extension FROM the allowlisted type, so
        the caller no longer names the extension of an object CloudFront serves and a
        filename with no dot no longer becomes its own extension. The buffer is bounded
        at 2MB, and the multipart plugin is registered with
        `limits: { fileSize, files: 1, fields: 16, fieldSize }`.
Note:   image/svg+xml is on the allowlist because a QR logo is plausibly an SVG,
        and SVG is scriptable when served inline. If the distribution serves these
        with `Content-Disposition: inline` and no sandboxing, dropping svg+xml from
        ALLOWED_LOGO_TYPES is a one-line change — it depends on the CloudFront
        configuration, which is outside this repository.

[x] [35.5] LOW — FIXED — The session check answers and the hook carries on
File:   src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.hooks.ts:6-9

Now:    checkSession signals failure by calling `reply.code(401).send(...)` and
        returning; it does not throw. The hook awaits it and then unconditionally
        evaluates the survey branch:
          `await checkSession(req, rep);
           if (resourceName === "survey") await verifySurveyAccess(req, rep);`
Impact: On an invalid session the 401 is already sent, and the hook then runs
        verifySurveyAccess with `request.userId` undefined. hasAccessToSurvey
        throws for an empty userId and its catch returns false, so the second
        handler tries to send a 403 on a reply that has already been answered —
        a duplicate-send warning per rejected request rather than a clean 401.
        Not exploitable: Fastify stops the lifecycle once a reply is sent, so
        the route handler does not run either way. It is noise on the rejection
        path, and it means the hook's ordering assumption is unstated.

        check has answered", "checks the session for every resource"

        clean 401 rather than a 401 followed by a verifySurveyAccess call with an

[x] [35.6] LOW — FIXED — Two conditions that cannot do what they read as

        src/modules/shareSettings/shareSettings.schema.ts:15
Now:    `if (typeof logoFile === "object" && logoFile !== "null")` — the second
        clause compares an object against the four-character string "null",
        which is never equal, so it is always true and adds nothing. The same
        comparison in the else branch IS meaningful, because there logoFile is a
        string.
        Separately, `isLogoEnabled: z.coerce.boolean()` treats every non-empty
        string as true, and multipart sends booleans as strings.
Impact: The dead clause is only misleading — but `typeof null === "object"` in

        for logoFile, which is the only reason that is unreachable.

        could not take the upload path even if the body schema stopped rejecting it.
        `isLogoEnabled` reads the literal strings multipart sends
        (`z.union([z.boolean(), z.enum(["true","false"])])` with a transform) instead
        of coercing, so posting `isLogoEnabled=false` now turns the logo off.

NOT COVERED — SHARE SETTINGS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 35.1 describes a structure rather than a reachable exploit. The tests pin
    that the survey queries carry no org condition, that the hook branches on an
    unconstrained string, and that a mismatched name currently touches no table.
    No path was found that reaches the unscoped survey write today.
  - verifySurveyAccess is mocked here; whether hasAccessToSurvey correctly
    scopes a survey to the caller's teams belongs to the survey module, which
    has not been covered. 35.1's severity assumes it does — if it does not, the
    survey path has no protection at all.
  - 35.3's impact depends on the bucket's public-read setting and what
    CloudFront sends for Content-Type and Content-Disposition. Both are
    infrastructure outside this repository. What is pinned is that the
    application allows any type and extension through.
  - The multipart plugin's own defaults may impose a file size limit even though
    none is passed; @fastify/multipart's defaults were not verified against the
    installed version. The finding is that the module sets none of its own.
  - uploadFile's success shape — that lib-storage's done() returns an object
    carrying Key — is read from @aws-sdk/lib-storage's contract, not observed.
    The failure shape, which is what 35.2 turns on, follows from the catch in
    s3Upload.ts returning undefined and is pinned directly.
  - The Prisma calls and the S3 helpers are mocked; what is pinned is the
    argument each function builds.

================================================================================
36. REVIEW SUGGESTION MODULE (src/modules/reviewSuggestion)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 36.1, 36.2, 36.3, 36.4 and 36.6 are fixed. 36.5 is PARTLY FIXED — the
public read is rate limited and its query is validated, but checking the
language against the ones the team actually has is a frontend contract question
rather than a fix.

One test file, 37 tests. 283 lines across five files at /api/reviewSuggestion:
the canned review texts a survey offers a respondent before sending them to a
Google review page, stored per team and per language.

The route file is the whole story. Six routes, and the guards on them are
applied one at a time rather than by a hook, with a different mechanism on
almost every line:

  GET  /:teamId                  [checkSession, verifyTeamAccess]
  GET  /public/:surveyTeamMapId  (none — deliberate, respondents read it)
  GET  /:teamId/count            [checkSession, verifyTeamAccess]
  POST /:teamId                  [checkSession, verifyTeamAccess]
  PUT  /:reviewSuggestionId      [checkSession] + an in-handler team check
  DELETE /:reviewSuggestionId    (none)

The delete is the odd one out, and it is not a public read — it is a
destructive write with no session at all (36.1).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [36.1] HIGH — FIXED — Deleting a review suggestion needs no session at all
File:   src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.route.ts:71-79
        src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.controller.ts:81-88
        src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.service.ts:73-79
Now:    The route's options object is `{ schema: { params: byReviewSuggestionIdSchema } }`
        and nothing else — no preHandler key, so neither checkSession nor any
        team check runs. The handler reads request.params.reviewSuggestionId,
        calls `prisma.reviewSuggestion.delete({ where: { id } })`, and sends the
        deleted row back.
        Every other route in the file carries at least checkSession. The PUT
        directly above it carries checkSession AND calls hasAccessToReview
        before writing.
Impact: `DELETE /api/reviewSuggestion/:id` is reachable by anyone on the
        internet. A uuid is the entire authorisation, and the row is removed
        from whichever team owns it.
        What it destroys is customer-facing: these are the suggested review
        texts a respondent is shown at the end of a survey, immediately before
        being sent to the org's Google review page. Emptying a team's set
        degrades that flow silently — the survey still works, the suggestions
        simply stop appearing, and nothing in the product reports the deletion.
        There is no soft delete and no audit row, and the response body returns
        the deleted record including its teamId, so a successful guess also
        confirms which team it belonged to.
        Ids are v4 uuids and are not exposed by the public read, which projects
        to `{ review, id }` — so the id IS returned to unauthenticated survey
        respondents for any survey they can open. That closes the loop: read the
        ids from /public/:surveyTeamMapId, then delete them.
Pinned: reviewSuggestion.test.ts — "the delete route carries no preHandler of any
        kind", "the handler reads neither a user nor a team", "nothing looks the
        review up before deleting it", "sends the deleted row back to the
        caller", "the three team routes carry both the session and the team
        check", "the update route carries the session check and does its own",
        "the team listing returns whole rows while the public one projects"
Fix:    `preHandler: [checkSession]` plus the hasAccessToReview check the update
        route already performs.

Fixed:  `preHandler: [checkSession]` on the delete route, and the handler runs the
        same ownership check the update does — hasAccessToReview resolves the
        review to its team and requires the caller to belong to it. A foreign
        review is 403, a missing one 404, and neither reaches prisma.delete.
        The public read that hands those ids to unauthenticated respondents is
        still public (that is deliberate) but is rate limited now — see 36.5.

[x] [36.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — The update body is unvalidated and spread into the write
File:   src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.route.ts:61-70

Now:    The PUT declares `schema: { params: byReviewSuggestionIdSchema }` and no

        this body — `{ review, language }` — and is imported by the controller
        for its type only. It is never attached to a route.
Impact: ReviewSuggestion is `{ id, teamId, review, language }`
        (prisma/schema.prisma:1202-1208), so every column is writable. A PUT
        carrying `{ "teamId": "<attacker's team>" }` moves the suggestion out of
        the team that owns it and into one the caller controls — and the
        in-handler check has already passed at that point, because it was
        evaluated against the row's ORIGINAL teamId.
        That is a one-request transfer: read a suggestion you can see, PUT it
        into your own team, and it now appears in your survey flow and no longer
        in theirs. `review` is also unbounded here, while the create path caps
        it at 1000 characters.
Pinned: reviewSuggestion.test.ts — "forwards a teamId in the body into the

        declares a body schema", "the create schema bounds each review and
        requires at least one"
Fix:    Attach updateReviewSuggestionSchema to the route.

Fixed:  Both halves. updateReviewSuggestionSchema is attached to the PUT, so an
        extra field is stripped before the handler sees it; and the service writes
        `data: { review, language }` by name rather than the body verbatim. A PUT
        carrying another team's id can no longer move the suggestion into it after
        the in-handler check has passed against the row's original teamId.

[x] [36.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — The update's refusal never completes the reply
File:   src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.controller.ts:73-76
Now:    ```
        if (!(await hasAccessToReview(request.userId, reviewSuggestionId))) {
          reply.code(403);
          return;
        }
        ```
        `reply.code()` sets the status and returns the reply for chaining; it
        does not send. The async handler then returns undefined.
Impact: Fastify treats an async handler that resolves undefined without a sent
        reply as a promise to answer later, so the refusal path does not answer
        403 — it leaves the request open until the client or the platform times
        it out. The success path two lines below calls reply.send and is fine.
        A denial that holds the connection is worse than one that answers: it
        occupies a socket per attempt, and to the caller it is indistinguishable
        from the server hanging, so a client retries.
        Every other refusal in the module — verifyTeamAccess's 400 and 403, the
        error handler's 500 — calls send.
Pinned: reviewSuggestion.test.ts — "the refusal sets a status but sends no body",
        "a caller outside the team is refused before the write", "the success
        path does send"
Fix:    `return reply.code(403).send({ message: "..." })`.

Fixed:  `return reply.code(403).send({ message })`, through a shared `refuse`
        helper both by-id writers use. The refusal completes the reply instead of
        leaving the request open until something times it out.

[x] [36.4] LOW — FIXED — An unknown review id throws rather than answering 404
File:   src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.auth.ts:5-8
        src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.controller.ts:73
        src/modules/reviewSuggestion/reviewSuggestion.service.ts:73-79
Now:    hasAccessToReview looks the row up and `if (!review) throw new Error(
        "Review not found")`. The handler does not catch it, so it reaches the
        module's error handler, which answers a fixed 500. The delete path
        reaches the same place by a different route: prisma.delete raises P2025
        for a missing row.
Impact: Both by-id endpoints answer 500 for an id that does not exist. A 404 is
        the correct answer and would not leak anything the endpoints do not
        already reveal, since both are per-row operations the caller must
        already be able to name.
        The practical cost is diagnostic: a client cannot tell a stale id from a
        server fault, and the logs fill with "Review not found" errors from
        ordinary races — deleting a suggestion twice, or editing one another
        member just removed.
        Using an exception for a not-found condition inside an authorisation
        helper also means the helper cannot be used defensively; a caller that
        wants "does this exist and may I touch it" gets a throw for the first
        half and a boolean for the second.
Pinned: reviewSuggestion.test.ts — "an unknown review throws instead of answering
        404", "a missing row escapes to the error handler", "the error handler
        answers a fixed 500 and logs", "the access check resolves the review's
        team"
Fix:    Return false for a missing row and answer 404 in the handler.

Fixed:  hasAccessToReview returns "not-found" | "forbidden" | "allowed" instead
        of throwing for a missing row and returning a boolean otherwise, so the
        helper can be used defensively — which is what let the delete adopt it for
        36.1. The handlers answer 404 and 403 respectively, and the delete path no
        longer reaches prisma.delete for an id that does not exist, so its P2025
        route to the same 500 is closed too.

NOT COVERED — REVIEW SUGGESTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 36.3 rests on Fastify's async-handler contract: a handler that resolves
    undefined without having sent leaves the request open. What is pinned is
    that reply.code is called and reply.send is not, and that the handler
    resolves undefined. The hang itself was not reproduced against a running
    server, and a plugin or a server-level timeout could answer for it.
  - 36.1 says the delete is registered with no preHandler and that the handler
    reads no identity. Whether /api/reviewSuggestion is reachable from outside
    the deployment is infrastructure outside this repository — the finding
    assumes the same exposure as every other /api route.
  - verifyTeamAccess and hasAccessToTeam are mocked here; whether they correctly
    scope a team to the caller belongs to the team module, which has not been
    covered. 36.2's severity assumes the in-handler check works, since the point
    is that it is evaluated against the row's original teamId and then bypassed
    by the write.
  - getGoogleUrlBySurveyTeamMapId belongs to the survey module and is mocked;
    what it returns and whether it is org-scoped was not examined.
  - verifyTeamAccess itself continues after sending a 400 or a 403 rather than
    returning (team.auth.ts:41-47) — the same shape as issues.txt 35.5. It is
    recorded here because this module depends on it, but it belongs to the team
    module's pass.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked; what is pinned is the argument each function
    builds, not the rows that result.

================================================================================
37. RESPONSE MODULE (src/modules/response)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all six entries are fixed. 37.1 took three separate changes — the
exemption, the unscoped map-id lookup, and the empty-team-list fall-through —
because any one of them alone still left the export open.

One test file, 34 tests. 3,479 lines across six files — a 2,696-line service, an
authenticated tree at /api/response and one unauthenticated POST at
/api/public/response that records every survey submission the platform receives.

The authenticated tree is guarded properly at the top: two hooks, checkSession
then verifyAccess, applied to every route, with verifyAccess resolving the
survey behind the path and calling hasAccessToSurvey. Three destructive routes
carry an additional role check. That is a better arrangement than most modules
in this document.

The findings are the places the arrangement is stepped around. verifyAccess
exempts two routes by comparing URL strings, and one of those two exports
responses in bulk from a survey id in the body (37.1). The delete route is
checked against one path parameter and acts on a different one (37.2). And the
public submit answers the respondent before it does most of its work, which
means a class of failure is invisible — and which corrects what section 32 said
about this same code (37.3).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [37.1] HIGH — FIXED — The bulk export route is exempt from the access check
File:   src/modules/response/response.auth.ts:13-17 (the exemption)
        src/modules/response/response.controller.ts:114-140
        src/modules/team/team.service.ts:734-747 (getTeamIdsBySurveyMapId)
        src/modules/response/response.service.ts:103-113 (getAllResponses)
Now:    verifyAccess opens with
          `if (request.routeOptions.url === "/api/response/generate" ||
               request.routeOptions.url === "/api/response/download-response") return;`
        /generate carries assertRootOrg of its own. /download-response carries
        nothing beyond checkSession.
        Its handler takes surveyId straight from the body. The team filter comes
        from `request.body.filterTeams` — a list of survey-team-map ids —
        resolved by getTeamIdsBySurveyMapId, which is a bare
        `findMany({ where: { id: { in: mapId } } })` with no user or org
        condition. Only when filterTeams is empty does it fall back to
        getTeamIdsByUserId, which is scoped.
        getAllResponses then builds
          `teamIds?.length > 0 ? { surveyId, teamId: { in: teamIds } }
                               : { surveyId }`
        so an empty team list removes the team condition rather than matching
        nothing.
Impact: `POST /api/response/download-response` with another organisation's
        surveyId and that survey's map ids in filterTeams returns their full
        response set as a spreadsheet. Nothing in the request path is checked
        against the caller: the hook returned early, the survey id is
        unvalidated, and the map ids resolve to teams by pure lookup.
        Survey responses are the most sensitive collection this platform holds —
        free-text answers, the respondent's phone number through the Contact
        relation, IP address and resolved location, device fingerprint. The
        endpoint exists to produce exactly that as a downloadable file.
        The empty-list path is the second way in: a caller who belongs to no
        team gets `teamIds = []`, which drops the team filter and returns every
        team's responses for the named survey.
        The exemption is also brittle in itself — it is an exact string
        comparison against the registered URL, so it protects nothing if the
        route is ever re-registered at a different path, and conversely it
        silently disables the check for whatever route occupies that string.
Pinned: response.test.ts — "returns without checking anything for the two exempt
        urls", "exports whatever survey id the body names", "resolves the team
        filter from map ids the caller supplied", "falls back to the caller's own
        teams only when filterTeams is empty", "an empty team list is passed
        through rather than refused", "the exemption is an exact string match on
        the registered url", "the download route declares a body schema and no
        extra guard"
Fix:    Drop the exemption and resolve the survey from body.surveyId, and require
        every id in filterTeams to belong to the caller.

Fixed:  Three changes, because the finding had three parts.
        The /download-response exemption is gone — verifyAccess resolves the survey
        from `body.surveyId` when the path names none, and refuses a caller without
        access. Only /generate stays exempt, which carries assertRootOrg of its own
        and names no survey to resolve.
        getTeamIdsBySurveyMapId now takes the caller's userId and filters
        `team: { members: { some: { userId } } }`, so map ids belonging to another
        organisation resolve to nothing rather than to that organisation's teams.
        And getAllResponses treats an EXPLICIT team list as a scope even when it is
        empty — `teamIds.length > 0` used to drop the team condition entirely for
        an empty array, so a caller belonging to no team received every team's
        responses. `undefined` still means unscoped, which is what the two internal
        callers inside response.service.ts pass.
Note:   The remaining exemption is still an exact string comparison against the
        registered URL, as the entry says. That is now fail-CLOSED in the dangerous
        direction: a route re-registered at a different path loses its exemption
        rather than gaining one.

[x] [37.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — The public submit answers before it does most of its work
File:   src/modules/response/response.controller.ts:239 (the reply)
        src/modules/response/response.controller.ts:259-277 (the batch)
        src/modules/response/response.controller.ts:279-282 (the catch)
Now:    createResponseHandler creates the SurveyResponse row, immediately calls
        `reply.code(201).send()`, and only then runs the post-processing —
        location lookup, Google Sheet write, notification email, IP/location
        update, outgoing webhook, testimonial creation and the Zapier trigger —
        inside a single `await Promise.all([...])`. The surrounding catch calls
        `reply.code(500).send(e.message)` on a reply that has already gone out.
Impact: Promise.all rejects on the first failure and abandons whichever of the
        seven operations had not yet settled. The respondent never learns: their
        201 was sent before the batch began. The only trace is a console.error
        and a duplicate-send warning from Fastify.
        So a submission can be recorded with its Google Sheet row missing, its
        webhook never delivered, or its Zapier event never queued — and the
        organisation's downstream systems drift from the response table with
        nothing reporting the gap. That is worse than a visible failure, because
        a visible failure would be retried.
        This entry also CORRECTS issues.txt 32.2. That finding said a
        createTestimonial failure makes the respondent "see a 500 and the form
        report failure". It does not: the 201 is already sent. 32.2 has been
        amended in place; the constraint problem it describes is real, but its
        consequence is silent partial completion, not a failed submission.
        Answering early is a deliberate choice — the comment at line 254 says so
        — and it is the right one for latency. The defect is that nothing
        supervises what happens afterwards.
Pinned: response.test.ts — "answers 201 before any post-processing runs", "the
        respondent's 201 carries no body", "a post-processing failure attempts a
        second send", "the second send carries the underlying error message",
        "one failing side effect abandons the rest of the batch", "a failure
        before the reply answers 500 once", "the WhatsApp platform skips the
        whole post-processing block"
Fix:    Promise.allSettled, and log each rejection with the response id so the
        gap is discoverable.

Fixed:  The post-processing batch is Promise.allSettled, with every rejection
        logged against the response id and its index. One failing side effect can
        no longer abandon the Google Sheet write, the notification email, the
        IP/location update, the webhook, the testimonial or the Zapier trigger, and
        the catch no longer attempts a second send on a reply that went out at line
        256. Answering early is kept — it is the right call for latency, and the
        defect was that nothing supervised what followed.
        This is the same change 32.2 asked for from the other side; both entries
        are closed by it.

        } catch (e) {
          console.log(e);

        The middle branch reads `e.response.data.error` with no guard. Only an

================================================================================
38. REFERRALS MODULE (src/modules/referrals)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 38.2, 38.3, 38.4 and 38.5 are fixed. 38.1 and 38.6 are PARTLY FIXED:
the counters are rate limited and deduplicated per IP per day, and the totals are
logged before a delete, but recording visits as rows and archiving the totals are
both schema decisions left for you.

One test file, 35 tests. 190 lines across three files at /api/referrals: an
influencer referral tracker. Each influencer gets a code; visits and signups
attributed to that code are counted on the row.

Referral carries no orgId (prisma/schema.prisma:2809-2817) — it is a
platform-level table, and the three administrative routes are gated on
`assertRootOrg`, which requires request.user.orgId to equal process.env.ROOT_ORG.
That is the right shape for a table with no tenant, and there is no cross-tenant
finding here.

What there is instead: both counters on the row are incremented from
unauthenticated input, and the codes that address them are generated with
Math.random on a path that can skip its own uniqueness check.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[~] [38.1] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — Both referral counters are incremented from unauthenticated input
File:   src/modules/referrals/referrals.route.ts:36 (no preHandler)
        src/modules/referrals/referrals.controller.ts:9-27
        src/modules/referrals/referrals.service.ts:31-47
        src/modules/user/user.service.ts:144-151 (the signup side)
Now:    `POST /api/referrals/track-visit` is registered as
        `server.post("/track-visit", trackVisitHandler)` — two arguments, so no
        options object, no preHandler and no schema. The handler reads
        `request.body.code`, looks the referral up and increments visit_count.
        It reads nothing else: no session, no IP, no fingerprint, and it records
        nothing about who asked.
        signup_count is incremented on the other side by user.service.ts:146,
        `updateReferral(referralCode, "signup")`, where referralCode comes from
        the signup body — also unauthenticated, since signing up is.
Impact: Anyone who knows or guesses a code can raise that influencer's visit
        count without limit, and anyone completing a signup can attribute it to
        any code they choose. Nothing deduplicates, rate limits, or records a
        source, so the two numbers the module exists to produce are both
        directly writable by the people they measure.
        If influencers are paid or ranked on these figures — which is what a
        referral tracker is for — the figures cannot support that. There is also
        no way to audit after the fact: the row holds only the totals, so an
        inflated count is indistinguishable from a real one.
        The signup path is at least wrapped in a try/catch that logs and
        continues, so a bad code does not break account creation.
Pinned: referrals.test.ts — "track-visit carries no guard at all", "consults no
        identity and no request metadata", "counts every call, with no
        deduplication", "increments the visit count for the code in the body",
        "increments only the counter it was asked for", "the three
        administrative routes require the platform root org"
Fix:    Rate limit by IP, record a visit row rather than a bare counter so
        duplicates can be collapsed, and treat the totals as derived.

Fixed:  Two defences, neither of which needs a schema change. `POST /track-visit`
        carries `rateLimit({ max: 60, windowSeconds: 3600 })`, which bounds the
        RATE; and the handler keys a Redis entry on `referral:visit:<code>:<ip>`
        with a 24-hour expiry, so one IP contributes at most one visit per code per
        day — which bounds the TOTAL a single source can add. Both fail OPEN on a
        Redis outage, for the same reason the limiter itself does. The route also
        validates its body now (38.4).
Status: The AUDIT TRAIL is still open and needs your decision. "Record a visit row
        rather than a bare counter, and treat the totals as derived" means a new
        model — a Visit table with an IP, a timestamp and a source — plus a
        migration and a decision about retention, which is not a call to make
        inside a fix pass. Until then the row still holds only totals, so an
        inflated count remains indistinguishable from a real one after the fact.
        The signup side is unchanged: user.service.ts still attributes a signup to
        whatever code the signup body names. Signing up IS unauthenticated, so the
        defence there has to be the same visit-row shape rather than a session.

[x] [38.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — The code generator abandons its uniqueness check on two paths
File:   src/modules/referrals/referrals.service.ts:49-72
Now:    generateUniqueReferralCode lowercases the name, strips non-alphanumerics,
        truncates to fifteen characters, and appends
        `Math.floor(100 + Math.random() * 900)`. It checks each candidate against
        the database and retries up to ten times.
        Two paths leave that loop without a check:
          - an empty base — a name with no alphanumeric characters at all —
            returns `Math.random().toString(36).substring(2, 9)` immediately;
          - ten consecutive collisions return the same expression.
        Neither result is looked up before being written.
Impact: The unchecked fallback can collide with an existing code, and the create
        then fails on the referral_code unique constraint — reported to the
        operator as "Email already exists" (38.3), which points at the wrong
        field entirely.
        The fallback's length is also whatever the draw happens to give.
        `substring(2, 9)` of a base-36 float yields seven characters for a
        typical draw, one character for 0.5, and an EMPTY STRING for a draw of
        exactly 0 — which would store a referral with no code at all, matching
        any request that omits one only because the handler rejects a falsy
        code first.
        Math.random is not a CSPRNG and the suffix space is 900 values per name,
        so codes for a known influencer name are enumerable — which matters
        given 38.1, where knowing a code is the whole requirement.
Pinned: referrals.test.ts — "the fallback after ten attempts is never checked for
        uniqueness", "a name with no alphanumerics skips the uniqueness check
        entirely", "the fallback length varies with the draw", "a draw of exactly
        zero produces an empty code", "draws the suffix from Math.random rather
        than a CSPRNG", "retries when the generated code is already taken",
        "builds the code from the name plus a three digit suffix"
Fix:    Generate from crypto.randomBytes, check every candidate including the
        fallback, and loop until one is free.

Fixed:  Every candidate is checked against the database, including the one used
        when the name has no alphanumeric characters — both paths that used to
        leave the loop unchecked are gone, and exhausting ten attempts raises
        ReferralCodeError rather than writing a code that may collide. The suffix
        is drawn from `crypto.randomInt` rather than Math.random, and is six
        base-36 characters (~2.1 billion per name) rather than three digits (900),
        so a known influencer's code is no longer enumerable. The
        `Math.random().toString(36).substring(2, 9)` fallback — whose length was
        seven for a typical draw, ONE for 0.5 and an EMPTY STRING for exactly 0 —
        is replaced by a fixed twelve characters.
