39. QUESTION MODULE (src/modules/question)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: 39.1, 39.2, 39.3, 39.5, 39.6 and 39.7 are fixed. 39.4 is PARTLY
FIXED — the ticket flag is atomic and no longer fires on a null, but setting it
BOTH ways would override the operator's own toggle in survey settings, so which
writer wins is your call.

One test file, 51 tests. 1,307 lines across six files, mounted at
/api/question/:surveyId — every route inherits the survey id from the prefix.

The access model is the cleanest in this document: two tree-wide hooks,
checkSession then verifySurveyAccess, and because the survey id is in the prefix
rather than in a body or a query, there is no route the check can miss and no
second identifier for a handler to act on instead. Nothing here is a tenancy
finding.

The findings are all about the same thing from different angles: a survey's
questions are a single Json column, so every write replaces the whole array, and
almost nothing validates that the replacement is coherent. Three endpoints can
destroy a survey's questions with an ordinary, well-formed request.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [39.1] HIGH — FIXED — Reordering questions deletes any question the request omits
File:   src/modules/question/question.service.ts:101-109 (reorderQuestions)
        src/modules/question/question.schema.ts:234 (the body schema)
        src/modules/question/question.route.ts:84-93
Now:    ```
        const questions = await getQuestionsBySurveyId(surveyId);
        let orderedQuestions = newOrder.map((id) => questions.find((q) => q.id === id));
        await updateQuestionsOnSurvey(surveyId, orderedQuestions);
        ```
        The new array is MAPPED from the request, not merged with the existing
        one, and the result is written over the survey's questions column. The
        body schema is `z.array(z.string())` — no minimum, no requirement that
        it be a permutation of the current ids, no check that every existing id
        appears.
Impact: `PUT /api/question/:surveyId/reorder` with a short list deletes every
        question not named in it. `[]` empties the survey outright. There is no
        confirmation step, no soft delete, and the questions column is the
        survey — losing it loses the wording, the logic, the variables and the
        mapping that recorded responses were keyed against.
        This is reachable by ordinary use, not just by abuse: a client that
        computes the new order from a stale or filtered view of the questions
        sends a short array and silently truncates the survey. A drag-and-drop
        reorder issued against a survey another operator has just added a
        question to is exactly that case.
        An id that matches nothing is worse still. It becomes `undefined` in the
        array, and updateQuestionsOnSurvey writes the array FIRST
        (question.service.ts:144) and only then iterates it to check for ticket
        flags — where it dereferences that undefined and throws. So the corrupt
        array is already persisted when the request fails, updateSurveyVariables
        never runs, and the caller receives a 500 that reads like nothing
        happened.
Pinned: question.test.ts — "a partial order silently drops every question it
        omits", "an empty order empties the survey", "an unknown id is persisted
        as an undefined entry and then throws", "and the survey variables are
        never rewritten after that throw", "a duplicated id is stored twice",
        "the body schema accepts any array of strings, including empty",
        "rewrites the questions in the order it was given"
Fix:    Reject an order that is not a permutation of the current ids, and write
        the questions only after the whole array has been resolved.

Fixed:  reorderQuestions requires the new order to be a permutation of the
        survey's current ids — same length, no repeats, every id resolving — and
        raises QuestionOrderError, which the handler answers 400 for. Nothing is
        written unless the whole array resolves first, so the "persist the corrupt
        array, then throw dereferencing the undefined entry" path is gone too: a
        short list, an empty list, a repeated id and an unknown id all now leave the
        survey exactly as it was.
Note:   The body schema is still `z.array(z.string())`. Being a permutation of
        the CURRENT ids is not a shape a schema can express, so the service is the
        check — which is also where the current ids are known.

        entire question set. `looseObject` keeps the passthrough the flow builder
        needs, since a question is a PrismaJson type with a large evolving shape.
Note:   What the schema does NOT do is stop a type change. The update endpoint

        separate decision, but the destructive case is closed.

[x] [39.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — Five handlers set a status code and never send
File:   src/modules/question/question.controller.ts:35, :49, :55, :68, :83-88,
        :104, :127, :135, :156
Now:    Across createQuestionHandler, getQuestionByIdHandler,
        getQuestionsBySurveyIdHandler, deleteQuestionByIdHandler,
        reorderQuestionsHandler, updateQuestionHandler and
        replaceQuestionsHandler, every catch is `reply.code(500)` and every
        not-found branch is `reply.code(404)` — with no `.send()`.
        reply.code sets the status and returns the reply for chaining; it does
        not send. These are async handlers that then resolve undefined.
        deleteQuestionByIdHandler is the worst case: neither branch sends. The
        success path awaits the delete and returns, and the 404 path sets a code
        and returns.
Impact: Fastify treats an async handler that resolves undefined without a sent
        reply as a promise to answer later, so these paths do not answer — they
        hold the connection until something times it out.
        `DELETE /api/question/:surveyId/:id` therefore never responds at all,
        even when it succeeds: the question is removed, the survey is rewritten,
        the logic jumps are pruned, and the client waits. A client that retries
        on timeout deletes the next thing it names.
        Every error path in the module behaves the same way, so a survey that
        does not exist, a malformed reorder, or any database failure produces a
        hung request rather than a 500. The only handler that consistently sends
        is importAllowedEmailIdsHandler.
Pinned: question.test.ts — "the delete answers nothing on the success path",
        "the delete answers nothing for a question that does not exist either",
        "the by-id read sets 404 without sending", "every error path sets 500
        without sending", "the by-id read does send when the question exists",
        "the update does send on its success path"
Fix:    `.send()` on every one of them.

Fixed:  Every path sends. The catches go through a shared `fail` helper (500
        with a fixed message, or 400 for a QuestionOrderError) and the not-found
        branches through `notFound` (404). deleteQuestionByIdHandler — where
        NEITHER branch sent, so the endpoint never answered even when it succeeded —
        sends `{ success: true }` on success and a 404 otherwise. console.log is
        replaced by the logger throughout.

[~] [39.4] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — Enabling tickets is a one-way switch
File:   src/modules/question/question.service.ts:153-167
Now:    After writing the questions, updateQuestionsOnSurvey loops them and, on
        the first question carrying a `ticketIssue` key that is not undefined,
        sets `enableTickets: true` on the survey and breaks. There is no branch
        that sets it false.
Impact: Adding a question with a ticket issue turns ticket creation on for the
        survey permanently. Removing that question — or replacing the whole set
        with questions that have no ticket configuration — leaves enableTickets
        true, so the survey keeps raising tickets for responses that no longer
        have a rule behind them.
        The flag is written by a second prisma.survey.update issued after the
        first, so the two are not atomic: a failure between them leaves the
        questions written and the flag not.
        The check is `!== undefined`, so a question carrying `ticketIssue: null`
        or `ticketIssue: ""` also switches it on.
Pinned: question.test.ts — "a ticketIssue key switches tickets on", "removing the
        last such question does not switch it back", "the switch fires once
        however many questions carry the key", "writes the questions array to
        the survey"
Fix:    Compute the flag from the whole array each time and set it either way,
        in the same update as the questions.

Fixed:  The flag rides along in the SAME prisma.survey.update as the questions
        rather than a second one issued after it, so a failure between the two can
        no longer leave the questions written and the flag not. And the condition is
        `!== undefined && !== null`, so a question carrying `ticketIssue: null` —
        which is what clearing the field produces — no longer switches ticket
        creation on.
Status: The ONE-WAY half is open and it needs your decision. The Fix line asks
        for the flag to be computed from the whole array and set EITHER way, and
        that is not safe as written: `enableTickets` is also a field of the survey
        update endpoint (survey.schema.ts:130), so an operator can turn it on in
        survey settings — and computing it from the questions on every question
        write would silently turn it back off. Which writer wins is a product
        question. Until it is answered, removing the last question with a ticket
        issue still leaves the survey raising tickets.

[x] [39.5] LOW — FIXED — Creating a question at position zero appends instead
File:   src/modules/question/question.service.ts:16-19
Now:    ```

        if (!position) { position = questions.length; }
        ```
        `0` is falsy, so an explicit request to insert at the front is replaced
        by the append position.
Impact: A client asking for position 0 — the first question, which is the one
        respondents see first — gets the question added at the end instead, with
        a 201 and no indication the position was ignored. The bounds check two
        lines below explicitly allows 0, so the intent to support it is clear.
        The only way to place a question first is to create it anywhere and then
        reorder, which is the endpoint that can delete the rest of the survey
        (39.1).
Pinned: question.test.ts — "an explicit position of zero appends instead of
        prepending", "inserts at the requested position", "appends when no
        position is given", "refuses a position outside the current range"
Fix:    `if (position === undefined)`.

Fixed:  `if (position === undefined)`. An explicit 0 now prepends, which the
        bounds check two lines below always allowed, so placing a question first no
        longer requires a create followed by a reorder — the endpoint that could
        delete the rest of the survey (39.1).
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [39.6] LOW — FIXED — One bad row discards an entire email import
File:   src/modules/question/question.service.ts:189-221
        src/modules/question/question.utils.ts:356 (the pattern)
Now:    processEmail throws `new Error("Invalid Email")` on the first row whose
        email does not match the pattern, abandoning the whole file. The handler
        returns that message to the caller.
Impact: An operator uploading an allow-list of a few thousand addresses gets a
        single "Invalid Email" for the whole file, with no row number, no count
        and no partial result. Finding the offending row means bisecting the
        spreadsheet by hand.
        A file with no `email` column fails the same way on its first row rather
        than saying the column is missing, and a file whose rows are all empty
        reports "No email IDs found" — two different messages for two problems
        that look identical to the person uploading.
        The pattern itself is a reasonable approximation and rejects addresses
        that are valid but unusual; that is a defensible trade for an allow-list.
Pinned: question.test.ts — "one bad row discards the entire file", "a file with
        no usable rows is refused", "a missing email column fails on the first
        row", "reads the email column from a csv", "normalises the header casing
        and whitespace"
Fix:    Collect the failures and report them with row numbers.

Fixed:  Bad rows are collected rather than thrown on, and reported together with
        their row numbers — counting from 2, so the number matches what the person
        looking at the spreadsheet sees — up to twenty, with ", and more" beyond
        that. A file whose rows carry no `email` key answers "The file has no
        'email' column" instead of the same "Invalid Email" a malformed address
        produces, so the two problems are no longer indistinguishable. A file with
        no rows at all still answers "No email IDs found".
Note:   Still all-or-nothing: a file with any bad row imports none of it. Partial
        import would need a decision about what the caller sees, and the error now
        carries enough to fix the file and retry.

[x] [39.7] LOW — FIXED — Pruning logic jumps assumes a shape nothing enforces
File:   src/modules/question/question.service.ts:76-98
Now:    removeObjectsByQuestionId iterates every key of the flow's logicJumps
        Json and does
          `data[key].filter((item) => !item.conditions.some(...))`
        with no guard on either `data[key]` being an array or `item.conditions`
        existing.
Impact: Deleting a question from a survey that has a WhatsApp flow throws a
        TypeError if any logic-jump entry lacks a conditions array — and by then
        the questions have already been rewritten and the survey variables
        updated, so the survey is left in a half-migrated state with the flow
        untouched.
        updateLogicJump has the same assumption from the other direction: it
        does `delete data[screenId]` on flow.logicJumps without checking the
        column is an object, so a null logicJumps throws.
        Both are reachable only through a survey that has a flow, which is why
        this is LOW rather than higher.
Pinned: question.test.ts — "prunes logic jumps that referenced the deleted
        question", "a logic jump entry with no conditions throws", "no flow means
        no pruning", "removes the question and rewrites the variables"
Fix:    Guard both, and run the question write and the flow write together.

Fixed:  removeObjectsByQuestionId guards that the logicJumps value is an object,
        that each entry is an array, and that `item.conditions` exists before
        calling `.some` on it. updateLogicJump falls back to `{}` for a null
        logicJumps column. Deleting a question from a survey with a WhatsApp flow no
        longer throws part-way through, leaving the questions rewritten and the flow
        untouched.
Note:   The question write and the flow write are still two statements rather
        than one transaction, which is the second half of the Fix line. They touch
        different tables through different clients in the same request; wrapping
        them is a larger change to deleteQuestionById's shape than this entry's
        severity warrants, and the failure it guards against is now the guarded
        one.

NOT COVERED — QUESTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - question.utils.ts is 500 lines and is mocked here apart from emailPattern.
    getQuestionsArrUpdatedGRTypeQuestion sits between every write and the
    database — it is stubbed to a pass-through, so what it does to the questions
    array on the way past is not covered and could change any of the write
    findings' details.
  - 39.3 rests on Fastify's async-handler contract, as issues.txt 36.3 does: a
    handler that resolves undefined without having sent leaves the request open.
    What is pinned is that reply.code is called and reply.send is not. The hang
    was not reproduced against a running server, and a server-level timeout may
    answer for it.
  - hasAccessToSurvey is mocked. The whole tree rests on it and it belongs to
    the survey module, which has not been covered — the same dependency recorded
    against issues.txt 35.1 and 37.
  - The questions column is Json and the tests pin what is written to it, not
    what Postgres stores. In particular, whether an `undefined` entry in the
    array is stored as JSON null was not observed.
  - createSurveyDefaultVariableForQuestion and updateSurveyVariables belong to
    the survey module and are mocked; the coupling between a question's id and
    the survey variables that reference it was not traced.
  - The multipart limit is 10 MB with throwFileSizeLimit, which is the only
    module in this document to set both. Recorded as a non-finding so the
    contrast with issues.txt 35.3 is not lost.

================================================================================
40. PAYMENT MODULE AND STRIPE SUBFOLDER (src/modules/payment,
    src/modules/payment/stripe)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all six entries are fixed. 40.1 — the subscription bypass — is closed
by scoping the update to the caller's org, narrowing it to the status column,
requiring SUPER_ADMIN, and granting the subscription from the order's own
paymentStatus rather than from the status the client sent. One correction: 40.4's
claim about `ui_mode: "embedded_page"` is wrong; the installed SDK accepts it.

One test file, 35 tests. 2,178 lines across ten files and four route trees: a
Razorpay tree at /api/payment, a Stripe tree at /api/payment/stripe, and two
unauthenticated webhook trees under /api/public/payment.

Both webhooks verify their signatures. The Stripe one preserves the raw request
body through a content-type parser keyed on the exact webhook path and hands it
to stripe.webhooks.constructEvent; the Razorpay one calls
validateWebhookSignature before doing anything. That is the part of a payment
integration most often got wrong, and it is right here.

The Razorpay tree also gates its catalogue properly: eight administrative routes
carry assertRootOrg, which requires the caller's org to be the platform org.

Against that, three things stand out. One route grants subscriptions to whoever
asks (40.1). The Stripe tree does not carry the gate its Razorpay counterpart
does (40.2). And a checkout session belonging to anyone can be read back by id
(40.3).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [40.1] CRITICAL — FIXED — Any authenticated user can grant their own org any plan
File:   src/modules/payment/payment.route.ts:127
        src/modules/payment/payment.controller.ts:299-322
        src/modules/payment/payment.service.ts:768-775 (updateOrder)
        src/modules/payment/payment.service.ts:777-801 (updateSubscriptionForTrial)
Now:    `server.put("/order/:orderId", updateSubscriptionOrderHandler)` — two
        arguments, so no preHandler beyond the tree's checkSession and no body
        schema. The handler does:
          const order = await updateOrder(request.params.orderId, request.body);
          if (request.body.status === "COMPLETE")
            await updateSubscriptionForTrial(request.user.orgId, order.rateupPlanId,
                                             order.subscriptionId, request.userId);
        updateOrder is `prisma.order.update({ where: { id: orderId }, data })` —
        no org condition, and `data` is the request body, typed Partial<Order>,
        so every column of Order is writable.
        updateSubscriptionForTrial then writes the caller's own subscription:
        basePlanId, the plan's full `features` object, a reset renewedAt and
        `expiresAt: now + trialDays`.
Impact: `PUT /api/payment/order/<any order id>` with `{"status":"COMPLETE"}`
        upgrades the CALLER's organisation to that order's plan. Nothing on this
        path consults a payment, a Stripe session, a Razorpay signature or the
        order's own orgId — the only inputs are a path id and a status string.
        Obtaining a usable order id is trivial and requires no guessing:
        POST /api/payment/stripe/create-checkout-session writes an Order row
        with the chosen rateupPlanId BEFORE Stripe is called (40.4), so a caller
        creates an order for the most expensive plan, abandons the checkout, and
        PUTs it complete. The subscription is granted with the plan's entire
        feature set.
        The same request is also an unscoped write to another organisation's
        order record: Partial<Order> reaches Prisma unchanged, so amount,
        paymentStatus, subscriptionId and orgId on any order row can be
        rewritten by anyone with a session.
        This is the only route in either payment tree with neither a role check
        nor a root-org check, and it is the one that changes what an
        organisation is entitled to.
Pinned: payment.test.ts — "writes the whole body onto whatever order the path
        names", "marking an order complete upgrades the caller's own org to its
        plan", "the org upgraded is the caller's, not the order's", "nothing
        checks that the order belongs to the caller", "any other status writes
        the order without touching the subscription", "the route carries no role
        check and no schema"
Fix:    Remove the route, or scope the update to the caller's org, require the
        owner role, and derive the subscription change from a verified payment
        rather than from a status the client sends.

Fixed:  The three things the Fix line offers short of removing the route, plus
        the payment check that makes it safe.
        updateOrder takes the caller's orgId and writes `where: { id, orgId }` with
        `data: { status }` only, so the unscoped write to any organisation's order
        record — amount, paymentStatus, subscriptionId, orgId — is gone. The route
        carries `checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN"])` and a body schema of
        `{ status: enum }`. And the subscription is granted from the ORDER's own
        paymentStatus, which only the signature-verified Razorpay and Stripe
        webhooks set: a COMPLETE on an unpaid order answers 409 rather than
        upgrading anyone. The org upgraded is the order's, not the session's.
        So the chain the Impact describes — create an order for the most expensive
        plan through the Stripe checkout, abandon it, PUT it complete — no longer
        grants anything; and 40.4 removes the orphaned order it started from.
Note:   The route was not removed, since something in the product plainly calls
        it. If it turns out nothing does, deleting it is strictly better than
        keeping it.

[x] [40.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — Any checkout session can be read back by id
File:   src/modules/payment/stripe/stripe.controller.ts:76-87
        src/modules/payment/stripe/stripe.route.ts:51-59
Now:    getSessionStatusHandler takes session_id from the query string, calls
        `stripe.checkout.sessions.retrieve(...)` directly, and answers with the
        session's status, payment_status and `customer_details.email`. Nothing
        ties the session to the caller's organisation or to a local Order row.
Impact: A Stripe checkout session id identifies a specific purchase attempt by a
        specific person. Handing one to this endpoint returns that person's
        email address and whether they paid, to any authenticated user of any
        organisation.
        Session ids are not secret in practice — the module's own return_url is
        `.../payment/summary?session_id={CHECKOUT_SESSION_ID}`, so every id ends
        up in the buyer's browser history, in referrer headers, and in any
        analytics or log that records query strings.
        `customer_details` is also dereferenced without a guard, so a session
        that has none — an abandoned checkout — throws a TypeError out of the
        handler rather than answering.

        "the customer's email address is part of the response", "a session with
        no customer details throws"
Fix:    Look the session id up against an Order belonging to the caller's org
        before retrieving it.

Fixed:  The session id is resolved through a local Order in the caller's own org
        (`findOrderByCheckoutRef(session_id, orgId)`, checkoutRefId being @unique)
        before Stripe is asked about it; another org's session is a 404 and is never
        retrieved. So a session id from a browser history, a referrer header or a
        query-string log no longer returns that buyer's email address and payment
        status to any authenticated user.
Note:   The `customer_details` dereference the entry describes had already been
        made optional in an earlier pass; an abandoned checkout answers with a null
        customer_email rather than throwing.

[x] [40.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — The Stripe checkout writes the order before it calls Stripe
File:   src/modules/payment/stripe/stripe.controller.ts:30-74
Now:    createCheckoutSessionHandler calls `createOrder(data, userId)` first,
        then `stripe.checkout.sessions.create(...)`, and only the final
        `updateOrder` is inside a try/catch. Anything the Stripe call throws
        escapes the handler.
        The call also passes `ui_mode: "embedded_page"`. Stripe's documented
        values for that field are "embedded", "hosted" and "custom".
Impact: Every failed checkout leaves an Order row behind with a rateupPlanId and
        no session — which is exactly the artefact 40.1 needs, and it can be
        produced deliberately by sending a price id that does not exist.
        If "embedded_page" is in fact rejected by Stripe, this handler fails on
        every call and orphans an order each time; if Stripe tolerates it, the
        orphan only appears on genuine failures. Either way the ordering is the
        problem: the local record of intent-to-buy is committed before anything
        verifies the purchase can proceed, and nothing reconciles the two
        afterwards.
        The escaping throw also means the caller gets the framework's error
        rather than the handler's 500.
Pinned: payment.test.ts — "creates the local order before calling Stripe", "a
        Stripe failure leaves the order behind and escapes the handler",
        "requests an ui_mode Stripe does not document", "carries the local order
        id into the Stripe metadata"
Fix:    Create the session first and the order from its result, or clean the
        order up when the session cannot be created.

Fixed:  The Stripe call is inside a try/catch, and a session that cannot be
        created takes its order with it — `deleteOrder(newOrder.id)`, with the
        cleanup's own failure logged rather than masking the checkout failure. The
        caller gets a 502 instead of the framework's error. So a failed checkout no
        longer leaves an Order row carrying a rateupPlanId and no session, which is
        the artefact 40.1 fed on and could be produced deliberately with a price id
        that does not exist.
        This is the second of the Fix line's two options; creating the session
        first is not available, because the session's metadata carries the order id.
CORRECTED — the ui_mode half of this entry is wrong. "embedded_page" IS valid.
        The entry read it against Stripe's public documentation ("embedded",
        "hosted", "custom") and hedged both outcomes; the installed SDK settles it —
        `Stripe.Checkout.SessionCreateParams.UiMode` is
        'elements' | 'embedded_page' | 'form' | 'hosted_page' for this account's API
        version. The literal is left as it was, and TypeScript rejects the
        documented spelling.

NOT COVERED — PAYMENT AND STRIPE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - payment.service.ts is 842 lines and stripe.service.ts is 390; both are
    mocked apart from the two functions 40.1 turns on, which are quoted
    directly. The Razorpay order lifecycle, plan synchronisation, invoice
    handling and the subscription webhook side of stripe.service.ts are not

    the verifier stubbed. That constructEvent and validateWebhookSignature are

    correct is the library's responsibility and was not tested.

    verified, and both outcomes are described.

    any query string. A webhook delivered with a query string would take the
    else branch and fail verification — failing closed. Checked rather than
    assumed and recorded as a non-finding.
  - assertRootOrg is mocked here and is exercised in src/utils/auth.test.ts. Its
    own defect — calling done() after sending a 403 — belongs to that file.
  - Whether an Order row created by an abandoned checkout is ever cleaned up by
    a job elsewhere in the codebase was not traced; 40.4 says only that this
    handler does not.
  - The Prisma calls and the Stripe SDK are mocked; what is pinned is the

================================================================================
41. ORG MODULE AND WS SUBFOLDER (src/modules/org, src/modules/org/ws)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: all seven entries addressed; 41.3 is partly fixed. Both tenancy
findings — 41.1 and 41.2 — are closed by taking the organisation from the session
rather than the path, with the platform operator's own routes left intact.
checkRoleHandler was read to settle the caveat in NOT COVERED: it compares roles
only, never organisations, so both findings held. The dead connection manager is
deleted, the socket room is keyed on a server-issued id, and the socket's role
comes from the session.

Two test files, 45 tests. 1,941 lines across ten files: the organisation itself
at /api/org, and a WebSocket subtree at /api/org/ws that pushes inbox events to
connected browsers.

The org tree gates its platform-wide reads on assertRootOrg — the listing, the
per-org stats, the deletion-warning mail — and gates its destructive writes on
checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN"]). Those are two different questions, and the
module answers only the second one for the writes: the role check establishes
that the caller is an owner, and the handlers then take the target organisation
from the path (41.1, 41.2).

The WebSocket side is better than it looks. The room a socket joins is keyed on
request.user.orgId from the session, and the :afterAuthId path parameter is only
the key WITHIN that room — so supplying another organisation's connection id
does not join their channel. That is the thing most likely to be wrong in a
design like this and it is right.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [41.1] CRITICAL — FIXED — A SUPER_ADMIN of any org can delete any other organisation
File:   src/modules/org/org.route.ts:80-89 (the route)

        reads request.user. deleteOrg looks the org up by that id and deletes
        it.

        `DELETE /api/org/admin/:orgId` with `preHandler: assertRootOrg`.
Impact: The owner of any organisation on the platform can delete any other
        organisation by naming its id. Org ids are not secret — they appear in
        API responses across the codebase, and the WebSocket auth endpoint hands
        one back on request.
        Org deletion cascades: prisma/schema.prisma declares onDelete: Cascade
        from Org to teams, members, surveys, contacts, integrations, webhooks
        and subscriptions. There is no soft delete and no confirmation beyond
        the role check.
        The existence of the /admin/:orgId twin is what settles the intent. If
        /:orgId were meant to accept an arbitrary organisation, the
        platform-gated variant would be redundant — it exists because the
        by-path route was meant to be self-service, and it takes the id from the
        path instead of from the session.

Fix:    Delete from `request.user.orgId` on the self-service route and keep
        /admin/:orgId for the platform operator.

Fixed:  deleteOrgHandler resolves the org from the session and deletes that. A
        path id naming another organisation is refused with a 403 rather than

        silently deleting the wrong thing. The platform operator keeps

        access-token payload and compares it against the required list. It never
        looks at an organisation. So the caveat in NOT COVERED is settled — 41.1

[x] [41.2] HIGH — FIXED — The same shape suspends any organisation
File:   src/modules/org/org.route.ts:145-155
        src/modules/org/org.controller.ts:213-225
        src/modules/org/org.services.ts:674-681
Now:    `PUT /api/org/pause/:id` carries the same role-only gate. The handler
        reads `request.params.id` and calls
        `pauseOrg(orgId, request.body)` = `prisma.org.update({ where: { id },
        data })`. request.user is never read.
Impact: Any SUPER_ADMIN can suspend any organisation on the platform. pauseOrg
        is the switch the Shopify uninstall path uses to stop a tenant
        (issues.txt 26.14), so setting it is a full service interruption for
        that organisation's staff and its customer-facing surveys.
        It is reversible — the same route can unset it — which is the only
        reason this is HIGH rather than CRITICAL. The body schema does bound
        what is written: pauseOrgSchema strips unknown keys, so only isPaused
        reaches the update.

[~] [41.3] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — The mobile socket takes its access token from the query string

        and is the preHandler for `GET /:afterAuthId/mobilews`. The browser

        cookies.
Impact: A bearer token in a query string is recorded everywhere a URL is: access
        logs on the load balancer and the application, any proxy in front of

Status: PARTLY FIXED — the query-string fallback is still accepted, because
        removing it breaks every deployed build of the mobile app at once. Its

[x] [41.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — The connection manager is unused and grows without bound
File:   src/modules/org/ws/org.ws.class.ts (the whole file)
        src/modules/org/ws/org.ws.controller.ts:19, :141
Now:    connectionManager keeps two maps: orgId → afterAuthIds, and afterAuthId
        → connections. `GET /api/org/ws/auth` mints a fresh 128-bit id per call

Impact: Every call to the auth endpoint appends a 32-character string to a
        process-local array that nothing ever reads and nothing ever prunes. A
        browser that reconnects on every page load appends one per load, for the
        lifetime of the process. It is a slow, unbounded leak in a module whose
        job is long-lived connections.
        The dead class is also misleading in a way that matters: it looks like
        afterAuthIds are tracked and validated against the issuing org, which
        would be the natural way to authorise a socket. They are not — the
        socket is authorised by the session alone, and the id it presents is
        never checked against anything.
        The collision-avoidance loop is in the else branch, so the first id
        issued for an org skips it. At 128 bits that is immaterial; it is
        recorded because it shows the check was not thought through rather than
        deliberately scoped.
Pinned: org.ws.test.ts — "the ids it stores are never used to route a message",
        "ids accumulate per org and are never removed", "the first id for an org
        skips the collision check", "issues a 128-bit random id and remembers it
        against the org", "the auth endpoint answers with the minted id"

Fixed:  org.ws.class.ts is deleted. GET /api/org/ws/auth still answers with a
        128-bit id, so no client changes, but the id is minted in the handler
        and retained nowhere — the unbounded per-org array is gone along with
        the class whose other three methods had no callers at all.
Note:   The collision-avoidance loop went with it. It was checking against a map
        that was always empty, so it never did anything; at 128 bits from
        crypto.randomBytes there is nothing to check for.

[x] [41.6] LOW — FIXED — A reused connection id displaces a live socket from the room
File:   src/modules/org/ws/org.ws.controller.ts:102-108, :116-124
Now:    `activeConnections.get(orgId).set(browserId, connection)` where browserId
        is the caller-supplied :afterAuthId. A second connection presenting an
        id already in the map replaces the entry; the displaced socket is not
        closed.
        The close handler then does `activeConnections.get(orgId)?.delete(browserId)`
        — so whichever socket closes first removes the entry, including one it
        no longer owns.
Impact: Within an organisation, a client that reuses another client's connection
        id leaves that socket open but unreachable: it stays connected, sends no
        error, and silently stops receiving inbox events. The user sees a live
        connection and no new messages.
        It needs the victim's exact 128-bit id, so this is not a practical
        attack — the realistic case is a client bug that reuses an id across
        tabs. The close-handler asymmetry is the more likely one to bite: the
        first tab to close evicts the second tab's entry.
Pinned: org.ws.test.ts — "a repeated browser id displaces the previous entry
        without closing it", "rooms the connection on the session org, not the
        path id", "schedules an offline status when the socket closes"
Fix:    Key on a server-issued id, and close the displaced socket.

Fixed:  The room is keyed on a server-issued crypto.randomUUID() per socket
        rather than on the caller-supplied :afterAuthId. Nothing reads that map
        by key — the broadcast loop iterates values — so this is invisible to
        clients and fixes both halves: a reused browser id displaces nothing,
        and a closing socket removes only its own entry, so the first tab to
        close no longer mutes the second.

42. MOBILE NOTIFICATION MODULE (src/modules/mobileNotification)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: four of the five entries are fixed; 42.3 needs a schema change and is
left for you. The push path is the substantive one — it now chunks to Expo's
100-message limit, reads the per-message tickets it was discarding, prunes tokens
Expo reports as unregistered, and lets a transport failure reach the queue that
can retry it. The listing route returns the caller's own tokens rather than every
colleague's device address.

One test file, 35 tests. 189 lines across four files at /api/mobilenotification:
registration and removal of Expo push tokens, plus the helper the rest of the
codebase uses to send a push.

The module gets the identity right where it matters. Registration takes the
userId and orgId from the session and ignores anything the body says, and the
delete is scoped to the caller's own userId. The token format is validated by a
regex on both writers.

The findings are about what the read exposes and about the send path having no
way to report a failure.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [42.1] MEDIUM — FIXED — Any member can read every colleague's push token
File:   src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.service.ts:37-49
        src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.controller.ts:20-28
        src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.route.ts:19
Now:    `GET /api/mobilenotification` is registered as
        `server.get("/", getExpoTokens)` — no schema, no role check, only the
        tree's checkSession. getTokensByOrgId filters on `{ orgId }` alone and
        selects expoToken, so the handler answers with the raw token strings for
        every device registered against the organisation.
Impact: An Expo push token is a send credential, not an identifier: it is the
        address Expo's push API delivers to. Handing the full set to every
        member means the least-privileged account in an organisation can collect
        every colleague's device address in one request.
        What that is worth depends on whether the Expo project requires an
        access token for sends — an enforced project rejects an outsider's
        request, an unenforced one does not. Either way the tokens are also
        durable device identifiers that survive logout and correlate a person to
        a handset.
        The endpoint appears to serve no client need. Both real senders —
        notification.service.ts:162 and wasurvey.controller.ts:554 — call
        getTokensByOrgId server-side and push from there, so nothing on a device
        has to know another device's token.
Pinned: mobile.test.ts — "the handler returns the raw tokens to any member of the
        org", "does not narrow the list to the caller", "returns every token
        registered against the org", "the listing declares nothing at all", "no
        route declares a role requirement"
Fix:    Remove the route, or filter on the caller's userId as well as the org.

Fixed:  GET /api/mobilenotification returns only the caller's own tokens. The
        org-wide read (getTokensByOrgId) stays, because that is what the two
        server-side senders need; the route uses a new getTokensByUserId scoped
        on (userId, orgId).
Note:   The route was not removed, since something may render "this device is
        registered" from it. Scoping it to the caller keeps that possible while
        closing the collection of colleagues' device addresses.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [42.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — Push failures cannot be observed, and large orgs exceed the batch
File:   src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.service.ts:64, :90-97
Now:    sendPushNotification builds one message per token and POSTs the whole
        array in a single request. The call is wrapped in a try/catch whose
        catch is a console.error with no rethrow, and the resolved response is
        never inspected — Expo reports per-message failures in the 200 body,
        which nothing reads.
        `expoPushUrl` is read from process.env.EXPO_PUSH_URL once at module load
        and used nowhere else.
Impact: Three failures are indistinguishable from success to every caller.
        A transport error is swallowed. A per-message error in the response body
        — DeviceNotRegistered, the usual one, which is how a stale token is
        meant to be pruned — is never seen, so dead tokens accumulate and are
        pushed to forever.
        And if EXPO_PUSH_URL is unset, `axios.post(undefined, ...)` throws
        straight into the same catch, so every push in the system silently does
        nothing with a single line on stdout. There is no startup check on the
        variable.
        Separately, Expo's push API caps a request at 100 messages and this
        sends the org's entire token list unbatched — so an organisation past
        that size has its notifications rejected wholesale, silently, by the
        same swallow.
        Same shape as issues.txt 24.3 and 34.3: a helper written not to throw,
        so no caller's error handling can run.
Pinned: mobile.test.ts — "a transport failure resolves silently", "an Expo error
        response is not inspected either", "sends every token in one unbatched
        request", "posts one message per token to the Expo endpoint", "does
        nothing for an empty or non-array input"
Fix:    Chunk into 100s, read the receipt statuses, delete tokens Expo reports
        as unregistered, and let a transport failure propagate.

Fixed:  All four parts. The token list is chunked into 100s, matching Expo's
        per-request cap. The per-message tickets in the 200 body are read: a
        DeviceNotRegistered removes that token, so stale rows are pruned rather
        than pushed to forever, and any other per-message error is logged with
        the token it belongs to. And the try/catch around the POST is gone, so a
        transport failure reaches the only caller — a BullMQ processor, which is
        where a retry belongs.
Note:   The missing-EXPO_PUSH_URL branch is still a refusal rather than a throw,
        deliberately: it is configuration, identical for every call, so throwing
        would fill the retry queue with jobs that cannot succeed. It is logged
        at error.
        One consequence of letting the transport error propagate is worth
        recording: processNotification sends the email first, so a push failure
        now retries the whole job and can re-send that email. The ordering
        predates this change (sendAWSEmail could already throw) and belongs to
        the notification module.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [42.3] LOW — STILL OPEN — Nothing enforces one row per device
File:   src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.service.ts:9-34
        prisma/schema.prisma:2432-2444 (model ExpoPushToken)
Now:    addToken reads with findFirst on (expoToken, userId, orgId) and creates
        when nothing matches. The model declares `@@index([userId])` and
        `@@index([orgId])` and no unique constraint at all.
Impact: The de-duplication is advisory. Two concurrent registrations — an app
        launched twice, or a retry after a slow response — both find nothing and
        both write, and the device then receives every notification once per
        duplicate row. Nothing later collapses them, because the delete removes
        by (userId, expoToken) and would clear all of them at once only if the
        user unregisters.
        The window is small and the consequence is duplicate notifications
        rather than lost ones, which is why this is LOW.
Pinned: mobile.test.ts — "nothing prevents a second row for the same device",
        "returns the existing row rather than creating a second", "looks for a
        match on all three columns"
Fix:    `@@unique([expoToken, userId, orgId])` and upsert.

Status: STILL OPEN — closing it needs `@@unique([expoToken, userId, orgId])` on
        ExpoPushToken and an upsert, and the constraint cannot be added until
        existing duplicate rows are collapsed. That is a schema change plus a
        data migration, which is your call. The consequence meanwhile is
        duplicate notifications, not lost ones.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [42.4] LOW — FIXED — The send path accepts tokens the route would reject
File:   src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.service.ts:73-75

        path that skips the route schema is dropped here rather than handed to
        Expo.

[x] [42.5] LOW — FIXED — Registration has no error handling and answers with the whole row
File:   src/modules/mobileNotification/mobile.controller.ts:5-18

43. META AGENT MODULE (src/modules/metaAgent)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: three entries fixed, two partly. The webhook now verifies Meta's
x-hub-signature-256 against the raw body — the module registers fastify-raw-body
the way the Shopify webhook does — so it is no longer an unauthenticated write
into the application log; acting on the events it receives is left open as a
product decision. The connector credential routes are gated on ADMIN. One
correction: the WhatsApp webhook this section contrasts against does NOT verify
either; its checker is a stub returning true.

One test file, 35 tests. 1,881 lines across seven files: an authenticated tree
at /api/meta-agent for configuring Meta's Business AI Agent against a WhatsApp
channel, and an unauthenticated webhook tree at /api/meta-agent/webhook.

This is the best-scoped module in the document, and the scoping is worth
describing because it is the pattern whose absence produced issues.txt 29.2,
37.2, 40.1 and 41.1.

  - Every handler goes through a `wrap` helper that reads orgId from the session
    and passes it as the first argument to the service. No handler reads an org
    from a path, a query or a body.
  - The route prefix is /:whatsappSettingsId, and the channel is resolved with
    `findFirstOrThrow({ where: { id: whatsappSettingsId, orgId } })` before any
    access token is decrypted.
  - Every sub-resource by-id write — allowlist entry, FAQ, file, website, skill,
    connector, connector tool — is preceded by
    `findFirstOrThrow({ where: { id, agentId: agent.id } })`, where the agent
    itself came from `requireAgentRecord(orgId, whatsappSettingsId)`. The bare
    `delete({ where: { id } })` that follows has already been proven to belong.
  - callMetaAgentApi disables validateStatus and then checks the status itself
    and throws — the opposite of issues.txt 26.12, where the same disabled
    validation is never followed by a check.

There are no tenancy findings. What follows is smaller.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[~] [43.1] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — The agent webhook verifies nothing and discards what it receives
File:   src/modules/metaAgent/metaAgent.webhook.controller.ts:11-31
        src/modules/metaAgent/metaAgent.webhook.route.ts:5-8
        src/registerRoutes.ts:130
Now:    `POST /api/meta-agent/webhook` reads no signature header and consults no
        secret. The handler logs the entire body at info, walks entry[].changes[]
        logging each one, and answers 200. Its catch answers 200 as well.
        The file's own comment describes it as the entry point for
        "thread-control / agent-event notifications (e.g. control handed back
        from the AI agent to this app, or agent_event delivery updates)". No
        branch acts on any of that.
Impact: Two things, and the second is the larger one.
        The endpoint is an unauthenticated write into the application log:
        anyone can POST arbitrary JSON and have it recorded verbatim at info
        level, as many times as they like. There is no rate limit and no size
        bound beyond the server default.
        More consequentially, the events it exists to handle are received and
        thrown away. Thread control being handed back from Meta's agent to this
        app is the signal that the app should resume answering the customer —
        and nothing observes it. Because the handler always answers 200, Meta is
        told the notification was processed and will not retry, so there is no
        second chance and no error anywhere to notice.
        Every other webhook in the codebase verifies its sender: Shopify through
        the library's HMAC, Stripe through constructEvent, Razorpay through
        validateWebhookSignature, Zoho through a per-org shared token. This one
        is the exception, and it shares a WABA subscription with the WhatsApp
        webhook, which does verify.
Pinned: metaAgent.test.ts — "accepts a payload with no signature of any kind",
        "does nothing with the event beyond logging it", "writes the whole
        caller-supplied body into the log", "answers 200 even when handling
        throws", "logs one line per change notification", "the webhook tree
        reuses the WhatsApp subscribe handler for the challenge"
Fix:    Verify the payload the way the WhatsApp webhook does, and either handle
        the thread-control event or stop advertising that this endpoint does.

Fixed:  The verification half, in full. metaAgent.webhook.route.ts registers
        fastify-raw-body scoped to this tree — the same pattern
        shopify.public.route.ts uses — and the handler checks
        x-hub-signature-256 as an HMAC-SHA256 of the raw body keyed on
        process.env.waclient_secret, with a timing-safe compare, refusing 401
        otherwise. So the endpoint is no longer an unauthenticated write into
        the application log, and the full body moved from info to debug.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — a verified event is still only logged. Acting on a
        thread-control hand-back means deciding what "this app resumes answering
        the customer" does to an in-flight conversation, which is a product
        decision and touches the WhatsApp module rather than this one. The
        file's comment now says plainly that nothing acts on the event, so it no
        longer advertises a responsibility it does not have.
Note:   One correction to the finding: it says "the WhatsApp webhook, which does
        verify". It does not. isRequestSignatureValid
        (wa_encryption.utils.ts:239) is a stub that returns true with the real
        implementation commented out, for the same missing-raw-body reason —
        which fastify-raw-body solves, and which this change now demonstrates
        against a live route. Fixing that stub belongs to the WhatsApp module
        but is now a small piece of work.

[~] [43.2] LOW — PARTLY FIXED — Any member can register connector credentials

[x] [43.5] LOW — FIXED — Meta's error text is relayed to the caller
File:   src/modules/metaAgent/metaAgent.controller.ts:23-30
Now:    handleError answers `reply.code(error.status || 502).send({ message:
        error.message, detail: error.detail })` for a MetaAgentApiError, where
        both fields come from Meta's response body.
Impact: Meta's status and message reach the client verbatim. That is mostly
        useful — an operator configuring the agent wants to know Meta said "not
        eligible" — but it also passes through whatever Meta chose to include,
        and it forwards Meta's status code as this API's own, so a 429 from Meta
        becomes a 429 from RateUp and a client's retry logic reacts to the wrong
        service's rate limit.
        Everything that is not a MetaAgentApiError answers a fixed 500 with no
        detail, which is the right default.
Pinned: metaAgent.test.ts — "relays Meta's status and detail to the caller",
        "anything else answers a fixed 500", "the thrown error carries Meta's
        status and detail", "an empty result is sent as an object rather than
        undefined"
Fix:    Map Meta's statuses onto this API's own before relaying.

Fixed:  Meta's status is mapped before it is relayed: 400 and 422 stay 400
        (the caller's own input was rejected), 404 stays 404, and everything
        else — 401, 403, 429, Meta's 5xx — becomes 502. So a Meta throttle no
        longer reads as a RateUp rate limit to a client's retry logic. The
        message and detail are still passed through, since an operator
        configuring the agent wants to read what Meta objected to, and Meta's
        own status is named separately as `upstreamStatus`.

============================================================================================================================================================
44. GENERATE QR MODULE (src/modules/generateQr)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: four entries fixed, two partly. The QR target link is validated as an
http/https URL on both writers, the public read 404s instead of answering 200 with
null, and an unknown short id on the counter is a 404 rather than a 500. The
onRoute hook appends rather than assigns, which is what made the scan counter's
rate limiter and per-address daily dedup possible. Neither module has role gating
yet — that is a product decision, and it will now take effect when made.

One test file, 55 tests. 381 lines across five files at /api/generateQr: CRUD on
a dynamic QR record — a short id that resolves to a target link — plus two
unauthenticated routes, one that resolves the short id for a scanner and one
that counts the scan.

The tenancy is right, and it is right in the place that matters. Every
authenticated read and write takes ownerOrgId from the session and puts it in
the where clause, including the update and the delete:

    prisma.generateQr.update({ where: { shortUrlId, ownerOrgId: orgId }, ... })
    prisma.generateQr.delete({ where: { shortUrlId: req.params.shortUrlId,
                                        ownerOrgId: orgId } })

shortUrlId is @unique (prisma/schema.prisma:1848) and Prisma's extended
where-uniqueness lets the non-unique ownerOrgId sit beside it, so both are
enforced rather than decorative. Neither orgId can arrive undefined — it comes
from checkSession, not from a path or a body — so this is not the shape that
produced issues.txt 18.x, 21.4 or 28.1. The create takes ownerOrgId and
createdById from the session and ignores anything the body says, and the two
response schemas omit both, so the owning org is never serialised.

This module is a near-verbatim copy of src/modules/linktree — same route file
shape, same onRoute hook, same public/private split, same exported plugin name
`linkRoutes`. What follows is largely what both copies share.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [44.1] MEDIUM — FIXED — The QR target link is an unvalidated string of any scheme
File:   src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.schema.ts:17-20 (create)
        src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.schema.ts:24-29 (update)
        src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.service.ts:43-56 (the public read)
Now:    `link: z.string()` on both writers. No z.url(), no scheme allowlist, no
        length bound, and no non-empty check. `GET /api/generateQr/public/
        :shortUrlId` selects { name, shortUrlId, link } and the controller sends
        it with a 200.
Impact: link is the address a scanner's browser is sent to. The API accepts
        `javascript:alert(1)`, `data:text/html,...`, and the empty string, and
        serves them from an unauthenticated endpoint to whoever scanned the
        code.
        What that is worth depends on how the frontend consumes the response.
        Assigning an attacker-chosen `javascript:` value to window.location
        executes in the assigning page's origin, so a member of any organisation
        could print a code that runs script in the RateUp frontend against every
        person who scans it. If the frontend instead renders the link as an
        anchor and lets the browser refuse the scheme, this is only a validation
        gap. The frontend is not in this repository, so the ceiling is not
        established here — but the API should not be the component that decides
        it does not matter.
        The server itself never fetches link, so there is no SSRF surface in
        this codebase.
        Editing link after the code is printed is the point of a dynamic QR and
        is not the finding; accepting an unparseable value for it is.
Pinned: generateQr.test.ts — "the link is a bare string, so any scheme is
        accepted", "an empty link passes validation", "the public read hands
        back whatever link is stored", "update accepts a new link with no
        validation, repointing a printed code", "exposes only name, short id and
        link"
Fix:    z.url() with an http/https allowlist on both writers.

Fixed:  Both writers take a shared `targetLink` field: z.url(), bounded at
        2048, required to be http or https. So `javascript:`, `data:`, an
        unparseable string and the empty string are all 400s rather than values
        the public endpoint serves to a scanner's browser. Repointing a printed
        code still works — that is the point of a dynamic QR — it just cannot be
        repointed at a scheme the browser executes.

[~] [44.2] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — The scan count is an unauthenticated counter anyone can drive
File:   src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.service.ts:105-116
        src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.routeOptions.ts:73-79
        src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.routes.ts:24
Now:    `POST /api/generateQr/noOfScans/:shortUrlId` carries
        `config: { isPublic: true }`, so the module's onRoute hook sets its
        preHandler to []. incrementNoOfScansInDB does
        `update({ where: { shortUrlId }, data: { noOfScans: { increment: 1 } } })`
        with no rate limit, no deduplication by device or address, and no
        record of who incremented.
Impact: The count is fully caller-controlled in both directions. Anyone who
        knows or guesses a short id can raise another organisation's scan count
        without bound — the short id is eight characters from a
        no-lookalikes alphabet and appears in every printed code — and a scanner
        that navigates straight to the target link never triggers the POST at
        all, so real scans are undercounted.
        The consequence is analytics integrity rather than data exposure: the
        endpoint reveals nothing, and the counter is not reachable from the
        update body — updateGenerateQrRecordReqBody strips noOfScans. But
        noOfScans is the only measure the
        product offers of whether a printed campaign worked, and nothing in the
        response tells an operator the number is advisory.
        Same shape as the public counter in src/modules/linktree, which this
        module was copied from.
Pinned: generateQr.test.ts — "increments on the short id alone, with no org and
        no ownership check", "nothing bounds how many times the same caller may
        increment", "the scan counter reports success without knowing whether it
        counted", "exactly two route options declare themselves public",
        "update cannot reach noOfScans or the owning org"
Fix:    Count server-side on the public resolve, and deduplicate per device.

Fixed:  Two bounds, both of which needed 44.5 fixed first so a public route
        could carry a preHandler at all. One scan per address per code per day,
        deduplicated through Redis with a 24-hour key (fails open, so a Redis
        outage counts rather than drops), and rateLimit({ max: 120,
        windowSeconds: 3600 }) per address on top, so one address cannot walk
        the short-id space either. A repeat answers 200 exactly as a counted
        scan does — whether this address was seen today is not the caller's
        business.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — counting server-side on the public resolve is not done.
        The client POSTs to this endpoint today, so moving the count to the
        resolve would either double-count or need the POST removed in the same
        release as a frontend change. The undercount the finding describes — a
        scanner that navigates straight to the target link never triggers the
        POST — is unchanged.

        absent. Nothing in the service or the controller catches it, so

        `500 { success: false, message: "something went wrong, please try again

Impact: An unauthenticated caller can make the API answer 500 by POSTing to a
        short id that does not exist, which is the ordinary case for a QR code
        that has since been deleted. Every one of those writes a stack trace to
        the log through asyncErrHandler's console.log.
        Nothing is corrupted and nothing is disclosed — the generic message is
        the right shape — but a routine client condition is reported as a server
        fault, and 500s from a public endpoint are the ones an operator is most
        likely to be paged about.

        counted; the controller answers 404 on a miss. So a POST to a deleted

[~] [44.5] LOW — PARTLY FIXED — The onRoute hook replaces a route's own preHandler
File:   src/modules/generateQr/generateQr.routes.ts:14-20
Now:    The hook assigns rather than appends:
          `routeOptions.preHandler = [checkSession]`  (or `= []` when public).
        It runs for every route in the tree, after the route's own options have
        been read.
Impact: No route in the module declares a preHandler today, so nothing is being
        discarded. The risk is that adding one silently does nothing: a role
        check written into deleteGenerateQrRecordWithShortIdRouteOptions would
        be overwritten by this hook and the route would stay open to every
        member, with no error and nothing in the route table to show it.
        The public branch has the same shape in the other direction — `= []`
        discards rather than skipping — so a public route cannot carry a rate
        limiter or a signature check either.
        Recorded because the module has no role gating at all: any member can
        create, edit, repoint and delete any of the organisation's QR codes.
Pinned: generateQr.test.ts — "the hook replaces a preHandler a route declared
        for itself", "the hook puts checkSession on a route with no config",
        "the hook clears the preHandler for a route flagged public", "isPublic
        must be exactly true — anything else gets the session check", "no route
        declares a role or feature requirement"
Fix:    `routeOptions.preHandler = [...(routeOptions.preHandler ?? []), checkSession]`.

Fixed:  The hook appends rather than assigns:
        `[checkSession, ...declared]` for a private route and `[...declared]`
        for a public one, with a single declared function normalised to an
        array. A role check written into a route's own options now takes effect,
        and a public route can carry a rate limiter — which is what 44.2 needed.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — the module still has no role gating at all: any member
        can create, edit, repoint and delete any of the organisation's QR codes.
        Whether that should be an owner action is a product decision. What is
        fixed is that the answer can now be written down and have an effect.

        The helper's retry is `shortIdUniqueConstraintErrHandler(functionToBeHandled)`
        — without the ...args it was given — so the retry calls the service with
        no request and the destructure of req.body throws a TypeError.
        Its guard also reads `err.meta.target?.includes(...)` before

        makes it a 500. The window is small — eight characters of
        nolookalikesSafe — but it grows with the table, and it is the one case
        the code was written to handle.

        it is recorded here because generateQr is one of its callers and the

        P2002 guard checks meta before reading target — which the module's own
        test now records from this side. The remaining half of the Fix line, the
        recursion bound, is added: MAX_SHORT_ID_ATTEMPTS = 5, after which the
        original error is thrown rather than spun on.

NOT COVERED — GENERATE QR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 44.1's ceiling depends on how the frontend navigates to link, which is not
    in this repository. What is pinned is that the API accepts and serves any
    scheme.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked. That `where: { shortUrlId, ownerOrgId }` on
    update and delete is enforced rather than silently reduced to the unique
    field is read from shortUrlId being @unique (prisma/schema.prisma:1848) and
    from Prisma's extended where-uniqueness; it is not observed against a
    database. If that behaviour were not in effect, every update and delete in
    this module would be cross-tenant.
  - shareSettings on this model is written by src/modules/shareSettings, not by
    this module — updateGenerateQrRecordReqBody accepts it but nothing here
    constrains its shape. That module has its own coverage and its own section
    (issues.txt 35).
  - The module is a near-verbatim copy of src/modules/linktree, including the
    exported plugin name `linkRoutes`. Both are default-exported and imported
    under distinct names in registerRoutes.ts, so nothing collides today. The
    linktree copy carries the same 44.2, 44.3, 44.5 and 44.6 shapes plus
    findings of its own; it has not been given a section yet.
  - asyncErrHandler wraps every handler, so the 500s described above were
    verified against the handlers directly rather than through a live route.
    No request was driven through Fastify; route registration is pinned by a
    stub server.
  - Nothing establishes who may read a short id. The eight-character id is the
    only secret protecting the public resolve, and whether the id space is
    enumerable in practice was not measured.

================================================================================
45. LINKTREE MODULE (src/modules/linktree)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: five entries fixed, three partly. 45.1 — the cross-organisation survey
leak, the largest finding in either link module — is closed by querying surveys
through teams scoped to the session org rather than through members' user records.
The public read is restricted to published pages and 404s otherwise, the click
counter's write is conditional on the array it read so it can no longer revert an
owner's edit, and the three schema-less routes declare their params.

One test file, 69 tests. 527 lines across five files at /api/linktrees: CRUD on
a public link page, a dropdown that offers the org's surveys as link targets,
and two unauthenticated routes — one that resolves a short id for a visitor and
one that counts visits and clicks.

src/modules/generateQr (issues.txt 44) is a near-verbatim copy of this module,
and the four shapes it inherits — 45.3, 45.5, 45.7 and 45.8 — are the same
defects in both. What is specific to linktree is 45.1, which is the largest
finding in either.

The CRUD itself is scoped correctly. Create takes ownerOrgId and createdById
from the session and ignores the body; the listing and the authenticated read
filter on ownerOrgId; and the update and delete both carry ownerOrgId in the
where clause beside the unique key, so neither can reach outside the caller's
organisation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [45.1] HIGH — FIXED — The survey dropdown returns surveys from every organisation its
       members belong to
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:104-158
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.routeOptions.ts:68-71
        prisma/schema.prisma:689-700 (model Survey), :585-598 (model OrgMember)
Now:    `GET /api/linktrees/linkTypeDropdownOptions/:linkType` resolves the org
        from the session and then walks it outwards:

          prisma.org.findFirst({ where: { id: requestedUserOrgId },
            include: { members: { include: { user: { include: {
              surveys: { include: { teams: { include: {
                team: { select: { name: true, tags: true } } } } } } } } } } } })

        There is no `where` at any level below the org. The service then does
        `data.members.map(({ user }) => user.surveys)` and pushes
        { id, title, teams, type } for every survey whose type matches.
Impact: Survey has no orgId. It hangs off `createdBy`, a User
        (prisma/schema.prisma:698-699), and a User may be a member of any number
        of organisations — OrgMember is unique on (userId, orgId), not on
        userId. So `user.surveys` is every survey that person has ever created,
        in any organisation, and the include pulls all of them.
        The response therefore hands a member of organisation A the survey ids
        and titles belonging to organisation B, plus the name and tags of every
        team each of those surveys is mapped to. Titles and team names are
        exactly the kind of thing that is confidential across a boundary —
        "Rival Ltd — exit interview", a team tagged "exec".
        This is not how the codebase scopes surveys elsewhere. getSurveysByUserId
        (src/modules/survey/survey.service.ts:360-376) filters on
        `teams: { some: { teamId: { in: teamIds } } }`, and Team belongs to an
        org. Going through teams is the pattern; going through user.surveys
        skips it.
        The population that triggers this is anyone in two organisations at
        once — an agency operator, a consultant, a franchise manager, a person
        who left one customer for another and was never removed. No attack is
        needed; the dropdown simply shows them.
        The route declares `schema: {}`, so there is also no response schema to
        bound what is serialised.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "the surveys it pulls in are not filtered by
        anything", "a member's surveys from another organisation are returned
        too", "the leaked rows carry the other org's team names and tags", "the
        query asks for the team name and tags explicitly", "resolves the org
        from the session"
Fix:    Query surveys through teams scoped to the org, as getSurveysByUserId
        does, rather than through members' user records.

Fixed:  The dropdown queries surveys directly, scoped to the session org:
        `where: { type, OR: [{ orgId }, { teams: { some: { team: { orgId } } } }] }`
        — the pattern getSurveysByUserId uses, going through teams rather than
        through members' user records. The Org include with no `where` below it
        is gone, and with it the whole leak: a member of org A can no longer see
        org B's survey ids, titles or team names and tags.
Note:   Survey.orgId is nullable, so both routes to the same organisation are
        accepted — the column when it is set, the mapped teams otherwise.
        Filtering on orgId alone would have silently dropped older surveys from
        the dropdown, which reads as data loss to the operator.

[x] [45.2] MEDIUM — FIXED — The public endpoint serves unpublished linktrees in full
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:55-70
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.controllers.ts:31-37
Now:    getPublicLinktreeWithShortIdFromDB filters on `{ shortUrlId }` alone.
        isPublished is in the select list but not in the where, and the
        controller sends whatever comes back with a 200 without reading it.
Impact: A draft is fully readable by anyone holding the short id: its name, its
        theme, and every link on it. isPublished exists precisely to mark a page
        as not ready — it is the only thing distinguishing a draft from a live
        page, it defaults to false (prisma/schema.prisma:1667), and it is
        writable from the update body — and the public read ignores it.
        The realistic case is a campaign prepared ahead of a launch: the short
        id is minted at creation, so it exists and resolves from the moment the
        row does. Anyone who learns it early — from a preview link pasted into a
        chat, from a staging build — sees the unreleased page.
        The flag is returned to the client, so a frontend could refuse to render
        it. That places the decision in the component that cannot enforce it:
        the API has already sent the content.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "nothing in the query restricts it to published
        linktrees", "an unpublished draft is returned in full", "the public read
        serves an unpublished draft with a 200", "the visitor count and the
        owning org are not exposed"
Fix:    `where: { shortUrlId, isPublished: true }`, and 404 otherwise.

Fixed:  `where: { shortUrlId, isPublished: true }`, and the controller answers
        404 when nothing resolves. A draft is now indistinguishable from a page
        that does not exist, deliberately — answering differently would report
        drafts into existence to anyone holding a short id.

[~] [45.3] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — The visit and click counters are unauthenticated and unbounded
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:160-216
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.routeOptions.ts:79-86
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.routes.ts:34
Now:    `PATCH /api/linktrees/pageVisitsOrLinkClicks/:shortUrlId` carries
        `config: { isPublic: true }`, so the module's onRoute hook sets its
        preHandler to []. The page-visit branch is
        `update({ where: { shortUrlId }, data: { visitorsCount: { increment: 1 } } })`
        with no rate limit, no deduplication by device or address, and no
        published check.
Impact: Anyone who knows an eight-character short id can raise another
        organisation's visitor count and any individual link's click count
        without bound, and can do it against a page that was never published.
        The counters are the only measure the product offers of whether a link
        page worked, and nothing in the response tells an operator the number is
        advisory.
        The exposure is integrity rather than confidentiality: the endpoint
        returns a fixed message and reveals nothing about the page, and
        visitorsCount is not reachable from the update body — the schema strips
        it.
        Same shape as the scan counter in src/modules/generateQr
        (issues.txt 44.2), which this module was copied into.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "increments on the short id alone, with no org and no
        ownership check", "nothing bounds how many times the same caller may

        success without establishing that anything was counted", "update cannot
        reach the visitor count or the owning org"
Fix:    Deduplicate per device and rate-limit; count server-side on the resolve.

Fixed:  The same two bounds as 44.2, and for the same reason they were possible:
        45.7. One visit — or one click on a given link — per address per page
        per day through Redis, fails open, plus rateLimit({ max: 240,
        windowSeconds: 3600 }) per address. The click key names the link, so
        clicks on different links are counted independently.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — counting server-side on the resolve is not done, for the
        same reason as 44.2: the client drives this endpoint today.
Note:   The counters still fire for an unpublished page. That is now moot for a
        visitor, since 45.2 means they cannot resolve one, but the counter
        endpoint itself does not check isPublished.
