[~] [45.4] MEDIUM — PARTLY FIXED — The click counter is a read-modify-write that can revert an
       owner's edit
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:180-211
Now:    The link-click branch reads the whole links JSON array, maps it to
        increment one entry's noOfClicks, and writes the entire array back:

          const { links } = await prisma.linktree.findFirst({ where: { shortUrlId },
                                                              select: { links: true } });
          ...
          await prisma.linktree.update({ where: { shortUrlId }, data: { links: newLinks } });

        There is no transaction, no version column and no conditional write.
Impact: Two consequences, and the second is the one that loses data.
        Concurrent clicks lose counts: two requests both read N and both write
        N+1, so the pair registers as one. That is the ordinary cost of this
        pattern and only affects the metric.
        The larger one is that the write is the whole array as it was a moment
        earlier. A click that overlaps an owner's `PUT /api/linktrees` — which
        also writes links wholesale — reverts that edit. The owner adds a link,
        renames one, or removes a stale one; a visitor's click lands a fraction
        of a second later carrying the pre-edit array; the edit is gone, with no
        error to either party and nothing in the row recording that it happened.
        The click path is unauthenticated (45.3), so the timing is not something
        the organisation controls.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "writes the whole links array back, not just the
        counter", "a click overlapping an owner's edit reverts that edit", "two
        concurrent clicks on the same link both write the same total",
        "increments the matching link's counter", "a link with no counter yet
        starts from zero"
Fix:    Move click counts out of the JSON column, or do the read and write in
        one transaction with a conditional update.

Fixed:  The write is conditional on the array it read —
        `updateMany({ where: { shortUrlId, links: { equals: links } } })` — and
        retried up to three times against whatever won. So the revert the Impact
        describes cannot happen: a click that overlaps an owner's edit finds the
        array changed, re-reads, and increments the owner's new array instead of
        writing the pre-edit one back over it. Concurrent clicks are no longer
        lost either, for the same reason. A click that loses three times is
        dropped rather than overwriting.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — the counts still live in the JSON column, so this is
        optimistic concurrency rather than the real fix. Moving noOfClicks to
        its own table is a schema change and a data migration.

[x] [45.5] LOW — FIXED — An unknown short id on the public counter answers 500
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:181-188 (link-click)
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.service.ts:166-175 (page-visit)
        src/utils/commonUtils.ts:28-42 (asyncErrHandler)
Now:    The link-click branch destructures the lookup result immediately:
        `const { links }: { links: any } = await prisma.linktree.findFirst(...)`.
        findFirst returns null when the short id does not exist, so the
        destructure throws a TypeError. The page-visit branch has the same
        condition in a different form — a bare update raising P2025.
        Neither is caught, so asyncErrHandler turns both into
        `500 { success: false, message: "something went wrong, please try again
        later" }`.
Impact: An unauthenticated caller can make the API answer 500 by naming a short
        id that does not exist, which is the ordinary case for a page that has
        since been deleted. Every one of those writes a stack trace through
        asyncErrHandler's console.log.
        The same TypeError is reachable a second way: links is `z.any()` on the
        update body, so a non-array value can be stored, and the click path then
        calls `.find` on it.
        Nothing is corrupted and nothing is disclosed — the generic message is
        the right shape — but a routine client condition is reported as a server
        fault from a public endpoint.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "an unknown short id throws a TypeError from
        destructuring null", "a links value that is a string throws rather than
        being rejected", "an unknown short id rejects rather than being
        ignored", "an unknown short id escapes the controller rather than
        answering", "theme, links and shareSettings are unvalidated JSON"
Fix:    Resolve the row first and 404, and validate the links array.

Fixed:  Both branches. Page-visit uses updateMany and returns null on a miss,
        so a deleted page is a client-side 400 rather than a P2025 escaping as a
        500. The link-click branch already guarded the null lookup; it now also
        checks Array.isArray before calling .find, so a links value that is not
        an array is refused rather than throwing a TypeError.

[x] [45.6] LOW — FIXED — Three routes declare no schema, and the module's 404s disagree
File:   src/modules/linktree/linktree.routeOptions.ts:63-77
        src/modules/linktree/linktree.controllers.ts:27, :36
Now:    deleteLinktreeByIdRouteOptions, getLinkTypeDropdownOptionsRouteOptions
        and getPublicLinktreeWithShortIdRouteOptions all declare `schema: {}` —
        no params, no response.
        The two reads also disagree about failure: the authenticated one sends
        `rep.code(404).send("Linktree with requested shortId not found.")`, a
        bare string, while the public one sends 200 with a null body for the
        same condition.
Impact: Three separate consequences, all small.
        `:id` on the delete reaches `prisma.linktree.delete` unvalidated, and
        the column is @db.Uuid — so a non-uuid produces a Prisma validation
        error and a 500 rather than a 400 naming the field.
        `:linkType` is unvalidated, and the service maps only "web-survey" and
        "whatsapp-survey"; anything else leaves linkTypeInDB undefined, nothing
        matches, and the endpoint answers 200 with an empty array. A typo is
        indistinguishable from "this organisation has no surveys".
        And a client cannot rely on the status alone: the public read reports a
        missing page as success, while its authenticated twin reports it as a
        404 whose body is a string where every other error in the module is an
        object.
Pinned: linktree.test.ts — "three routes declare no schema at all", "the delete
        id goes straight into the where clause unvalidated", "an unrecognised
        link type silently returns an empty array", "the authenticated read
        answers 404 with a bare string", "the public read answers 200 with null
        for an unknown short id"
Fix:    Declare params and response schemas; 404 the public read; make the two
        error bodies the same shape.

Fixed:  All three parts. The delete declares `params: { id: z.guid() }`, so a
        non-uuid is a 400 rather than a Prisma validation error; the dropdown
        declares `params: { linkType: z.enum([...]) }` and the service refuses an
        unmapped value with a 400 rather than answering an empty array that
        reads as "no surveys"; and the public read declares its params. The two
        reads now send the same 404 body — the authenticated one was a bare
        string where every other error in the module is an object.
Note:   z.guid() rather than z.uuid(): the latter also enforces the RFC version
        and variant nibbles, which Postgres's uuid type does not, so a legacy id
        the database accepts would have been refused at the edge.

NOT COVERED — LINKTREE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 45.1 is pinned by asserting that the query carries no filter below the org
    and that everything in a member's user.surveys reaches the result. That a
    real User row carries surveys from more than one organisation is read from
    the schema — Survey.createdBy is a User and OrgMember is unique on
    (userId, orgId) — not observed against a database. How many users in
    practice belong to more than one organisation was not measured, and that is
    what sets the real-world blast radius.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked. That `where: { shortUrlId, ownerOrgId }` on
    update and `where: { id, ownerOrgId }` on delete are enforced rather than
    reduced to the unique field is read from shortUrlId being @unique
    (prisma/schema.prisma:1661) and from Prisma's extended where-uniqueness. If
    that were not in effect, both writes would be cross-tenant.
  - 45.4's revert is demonstrated by showing that the click path writes the
    array it read; the interleaving with a concurrent PUT is reasoned about, not
    executed. No concurrent database access was performed.
  - shareSettings on this model is written by src/modules/shareSettings
    (issues.txt 35), not by this module.
  - The `theme` and `links` payloads are z.any(). What a stored link's url may
    contain, and what the frontend does with it, is not established here — the
    same open question as issues.txt 44.1, and the frontend is not in this
    repository.
  - No request was driven through Fastify. Route registration is pinned by a
    stub server, and the 500s described above were verified against the handlers
    directly.
  - additionalData exists on the model and is written by nothing in this module.

================================================================================
46. API ACCESS TOKEN MODULE (src/modules/apiAccessToken)
================================================================================

FIX PASS: seven entries fixed, one partly, one open. The credential is now 32
bytes of crypto.randomBytes base64url-encoded, which closes 46.1 and 46.3 together
— it no longer depends on Math.random's recoverable state, and it fits inside
bcrypt's 72-byte window so every character is checked. generateRandomAlphaId is
fixed at source, so the Zoho webhook secret is covered too. Minting, deleting and
regenerating are SUPER_ADMIN-only; regenerate is one upsert; the hook returns
after refusing and no longer logs the request headers.

One test file, 51 tests. 192 lines across four files: the credential that
authenticates RateUp's external API. Four management routes at
/api/api-access-token behind checkSession, and a preHandler
(verifyApiAccessTokenHook) that guards every route under /api/external/v1
(src/external/v1/index.ts:46) and the three support-user webhook routes
(src/modules/whatsapp/support-user/suport-user.webhook.route.ts:13, :18, :28).

The storage is right: only a bcrypt hash is persisted, the plaintext is returned
once at mint time and never read back, and the org's key is looked up by the
orgId the caller named — so a key for one organisation does not authenticate
against another. That last point is the one most likely to be wrong here and it
is correct.

The findings are about how the credential is generated, who may mint one, and
what it grants.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [46.1] HIGH — FIXED — The API key is generated with Math.random
File:   src/utils/misc/random.utils.ts:1-9 (generateRandomAlphaId)
        src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.service.ts:6
Now:    generateApiKey calls `generateRandomAlphaId(128)`, which builds the key
        one character at a time:

          let ascii = Math.floor(Math.random() * 52);
          ascii += ascii < 26 ? 65 : 71;

        Math.random is not a cryptographically secure generator. Node's crypto
        module is available and is used elsewhere in this codebase.
Impact: The key is 128 characters drawn from a 52-letter alphabet, which reads
        like roughly 730 bits of entropy. It is not. V8's Math.random is
        xorshift128+, seeded once per context and carrying 128 bits of state, so
        the key cannot contain more than 128 bits however long it is made — and
        the real question is whether that state can be recovered from output an
        attacker can see.
        It can be approached from two directions. An attacker who legitimately
        holds one key holds 128 consecutive draws from the generator; each
        character reveals about 5.7 bits of a 52-bit double, which is a heavily
        truncated view, but 128 of them is far more information than the 128-bit
        state needs, so solving for it is a constraint problem rather than a
        search. Recovering it would let them derive keys minted before and after
        their own — including other organisations' keys.
        The second direction is worse and is what raises this above the
        theoretical: the same process exposes the same generator at much higher
        precision elsewhere. src/modules/referrals/referrals.service.ts:56 and
        :71 return `Math.random().toString(36).substring(2, 9)` as a referral
        code — seven base-36 digits, about 36 bits, taken from the top of the
        mantissa, which is the part state recovery wants. Referral codes are
        handed to customers by design. redemptionValidator.ts:393 and
        earningRulesEngine.ts:313 leak the same stream more narrowly.
        Two things bound this in practice and neither is a defence. V8 draws
        Math.random from a 64-value cache and returns it in reverse order of
        generation, so an attacker must account for the batching; and a
        multi-instance deployment means the key and the observed referral codes
        may not come from the same generator state. Both make the work harder.
        Neither makes the key unpredictable, and neither is something the
        application controls or checks.
        A credential should not depend on this analysis being wrong.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "the key is drawn from Math.random, one call
        per character", "a repeated Math.random sequence reproduces the key
        exactly", "the alphabet is the 52 ascii letters and nothing else",
        "generateApiKey returns the plaintext beside its hash"
Fix:    crypto.randomBytes, base64url-encoded. 32 bytes is more than enough.

Fixed:  The key is `randomBytes(32).toString("base64url")` — 43 characters
        carrying a full 256 bits from the CSPRNG, with no dependence on
        Math.random's recoverable state.
        generateRandomAlphaId itself is fixed rather than merely bypassed, since
        its other caller mints the Zoho webhook secret
        (zoho.controller.ts:116): it draws each character with crypto.randomInt,
        which uses the CSPRNG and rejects the biased tail of the range rather
        than taking a modulo. The alphabet and the output shape are unchanged.
Note:   Keys minted before this change keep working — bcrypt.compare truncates a
        presented key the same way it truncated the stored one — so no
        integration breaks and no forced rotation is required. Rotating them is
        still worth doing, since the old keys came from the weak source; that is
        an operational call.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[~] [46.2] HIGH — PARTLY FIXED — Any member can mint a key, and the key authenticates as SUPER_ADMIN
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.route.ts:10-16
        src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.auth.ts:70-72
Now:    apiAccessTokenRoutes adds `checkSession` and nothing else. No route
        declares a role, and all four are registered two-arg — there is no
        options object in which a role check could sit.
        The hook that consumes the resulting key ends:

          // since most of the *.service.ts functions fetches user from request object
          const user = { orgId, role: Role.SUPER_ADMIN };
          (request as any).user! = user;

        The role is a constant. Nothing reads the caller's actual membership,
        and there is no way to mint a key with less than this.
Impact: The lowest-privileged member of an organisation can call
        `POST /api/api-access-token`, receive a key, and present it to
        /api/external/v1/:orgId/... as a SUPER_ADMIN. Any role check inside
        those services — the pattern used throughout this codebase, e.g.
        checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN"]) on org deletion and role changes —
        passes for them.
        The escalation is bounded to the caller's own organisation, and that
        matters: verifyApiAccessTokenHook verifies the presented key against the
        orgId in the path or query, so a key does not travel to another tenant.
        This is a MEMBER-to-owner escalation inside one org, not a cross-tenant
        one.
        Within that boundary it is complete. The external API reaches surveys,
        contacts, contact groups, teams, campaigns, WhatsApp templates and
        loyalty (src/external/v1/index.ts:49-55), and the same key authenticates
        the support-user webhook routes. A member who should not be able to
        change a colleague's role or export the contact book can do both through
        a credential the product hands them on request.
        The same four routes let any member delete and regenerate the
        organisation's key, so a member can also break every existing
        integration at will.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "no route requires a role, so any member can
        mint an API key", "every route is registered two-arg, with no options
        object at all", "every API-key caller is granted SUPER_ADMIN", "registers
        four routes behind a single session check", "create takes the org from
        the session, never from a parameter", "a key for one org does not
        authenticate against another"
Fix:    Gate the four management routes on SUPER_ADMIN, and record the minting
        member's real role on the token so the hook can restore it.

Fixed:  The three routes that mint, delete and regenerate the key now carry
        checkRoleHandler(["SUPER_ADMIN"]). So a member can no longer hand
        themselves a credential that authenticates as an owner, and can no
        longer break every existing integration by regenerating or deleting the
        organisation's key. The created-at read stays open to any member, since
        it returns only whether a key exists and when it was minted.
Status: PARTLY FIXED — the second half of the Fix line, recording the minting
        member's real role on the token so the hook can restore it, needs a
        column on ApiAccessToken. The hook still sets role: SUPER_ADMIN as a
        constant, which is now at least only reachable by an owner's key.
        See 46.8, which is the same column.

[x] [46.3] MEDIUM — FIXED — bcrypt truncates the key, so 56 of its 128 characters are never checked
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.service.ts:10, :67
Now:    `bcrypt.hash(randomKey, 5)` where randomKey is 128 characters, and
        `bcrypt.compare(accessToken, apiKeyHash.accessToken)` on the way back.
        bcrypt hashes at most 72 bytes of input and silently discards the rest.
Impact: Only the first 72 characters of the key participate in the hash. Two
        keys agreeing on their first 72 characters verify against each other's
        hash, and the last 56 characters an operator sees, copies and stores are
        decoration.
        The practical consequence is small — 72 characters is still far more
        than enough, and the real ceiling on this key is 46.1's 128 bits of
        generator state, not its length — but the code does not do what it
        appears to do, and the mismatch is the kind that survives into a later
        change. Anyone shortening the key to 80 characters would reasonably
        believe they had left a wide margin.
        The cost factor is 5, below the usual floor of 10-12. That is defensible
        here and not raised as a finding on its own: the input is a long random
        string rather than a human password, so an offline attack on a stolen
        hash is not the threat. The source comment beside it asks whether
        saltRounds=8 would bottleneck the request path, and then uses 5, so the
        number appears to have been chosen for latency without the question
        being settled.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "bcrypt truncates the key at 72 bytes, so the
        last 56 characters are never checked", "a difference within the first 72
        characters is still detected", "the hash is bcrypt at five rounds",
        "stores the hash, never the plaintext, and returns the plaintext"
Fix:    Hash a SHA-256 of the key, or generate a key at or under 72 bytes.

Fixed:  The key is 43 characters, comfortably inside bcrypt's 72-byte window,
        so every character of it participates in the hash and the comparison.
        The mismatch the finding is really about — code that does not do what it
        appears to do — is gone with it.
Note:   The cost factor is still 5, which the finding explicitly does not raise
        as a defect: the input is 256 bits of random rather than a human
        password, and verifyApiAccessToken pays this on every external API
        request.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [46.4] MEDIUM — FIXED — The invalid-org branch replies and then carries on authenticating
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.auth.ts:35-41
Now:    The `if (!org)` branch at :27 sends a 404 and returns. The catch beside
        it, which fires when getOrgById itself throws — a malformed uuid is the
        ordinary case — sends the same 404 and does not return:

          } catch {
            log.warn("Invalid orgId\n%o", request.headers);
            reply.code(404).send({ ... });
          }                                    // <- no return

        Execution falls through to the bearer-token check, the key comparison,
        and the assignment of request.user.
Impact: One request produces two replies. The 404 is sent, then the fall-through
        reaches a second reply.code(...).send(...) — a 400, a 401, or nothing at
        all if the key happens to be valid — and Fastify raises
        FST_ERR_REP_ALREADY_SENT on the second.
        When the key is valid, the hook also sets
        `request.user = { orgId, role: SUPER_ADMIN }` using the orgId it just
        declared unresolvable, and the bcrypt comparison runs against an org
        that could not be loaded. Nothing downstream is reached, because the
        reply is already sent — but the hook's own state says the request was
        authenticated.
        Same shape as issues.txt 41.3, where the WebSocket preHandler's 401
        branch also refuses and then continues.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "a lookup failure on the org replies 404 and
        then keeps going", "that fall-through can produce two replies for one
        request", "an org that does not exist answers 404 and stops", "a refused
        request never reaches the token comparison"
Fix:    Return from the catch, as the branch above it does.

Fixed:  The catch returns, as the branch above it does. One refused request now
        produces exactly one reply, the key comparison no longer runs against an
        org that could not be loaded, and request.user is not set on a request
        that was already refused.

[x] [46.5] MEDIUM — FIXED — A rejected request's API key is written to the log
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.auth.ts:45, :54, :65
        also :21, :28, :36
Now:    Every refusal path logs the whole header object:

          log.error("Invalid access token\n%o", request.headers);

        request.headers includes `authorization: "Bearer <the key>"`.
Impact: A key that fails verification is recorded verbatim in the application
        log, at error level, with no redaction. So is one presented with a
        non-bearer scheme, and so are the headers on every no-orgId and
        unknown-org path.
        The keys that reach these branches are the ones that did not work, which
        limits it — but "did not work" includes a key that was just rotated, a
        key typed against the wrong org, and a key that will work once the org
        id is corrected. The log is a lower-trust store than the database: it is
        shipped to whatever aggregator is configured, retained on a different
        schedule, and readable by people who were never granted the credential.
        Every other header goes with it — cookies included, on a request that
        carried a session.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "the whole request header set is written to the
        log on every refusal", "a missing Authorization header answers a generic
        401, never the 400", "a non-bearer Authorization header answers 400"
Fix:    Log the org id and the failure reason; never the header object.

Fixed:  Every refusal path logs `{ orgId }` and a message. No branch passes
        request.headers, so a key that failed verification — a rotated one, one
        typed against the wrong org — is no longer written verbatim into the
        application log, and neither are the cookies of a request that also
        carried a session.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [46.6] MEDIUM — FIXED — Regenerate deletes before it creates, with nothing to roll back
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.service.ts:52-55
Now:    `await deleteApiKey(orgId); return await createApiKey(orgId);` — two
        statements, no prisma.$transaction, no compensating action.
Impact: If the create fails after the delete has committed, the organisation is
        left with no key at all. Every integration authenticating against it
        starts failing, and the caller sees an error that gives no indication
        that their old key is already gone — the natural reading of a failed
        "regenerate" is that nothing changed.
        Recovery needs a different endpoint: `POST /` rather than
        `POST /regenerate`, because regenerate begins with a delete that now
        raises P2025 against the missing row. So the operator's obvious retry is
        also the one that cannot work.
        The window is short and the failure mode needs the create to fail
        specifically, which is why this is MEDIUM rather than higher. The
        delete-then-create ordering is also what makes regenerate unusable for
        an org that has no key yet.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "deletes before creating", "the two writes are
        not in a transaction, so a failed create leaves no key", "an org with no
        existing key cannot regenerate", "the regenerated key is different from
        the one it replaced", "deleting when there is no key rejects"
Fix:    One transaction, or upsert the row with the new hash.

Fixed:  regenerateApiKey is a single upsert on the unique orgId, so there is no
        window in which the organisation has no key: the row either gains the
        new hash or is created with it. This also makes regenerate usable for an
        org that has no key yet, which the delete-then-create ordering made
        impossible.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [46.7] LOW — FIXED — The delete handler never replies
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.controller.ts:16-19
Now:    deleteApiAccessTokenHandler awaits deleteApiKey and returns. It never
        calls rep.send, never sets a status, and its reply parameter is named
        `_rep`. The other three handlers in the file all send.
Impact: An async Fastify handler that resolves without sending and without
        returning a payload leaves the request open until a timeout closes it.
        The delete itself has already happened, so the org's key is gone while
        the client is still waiting and will eventually see a network error —
        the one outcome that most reliably prompts a retry of an operation that
        already succeeded.
        The retry then hits P2025 on a row that is no longer there, which
        escapes as a 500. So the visible sequence for a successful deletion is a
        hang followed by a server error.
        Same shape as issues.txt 36.3 and 39.3.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "delete never replies at all", "create sets no
        status code, so the plaintext key is a plain 200", "a failed create
        escapes the handler rather than answering"
Fix:    `rep.code(204).send()`.

Fixed:  `rep.code(204).send()`. The request no longer hangs until a timeout with
        the key already deleted, so the client is no longer prompted into a
        retry that hits P2025 and escapes as a 500.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] [46.8] LOW — STILL OPEN — The identity the hook builds has no userId
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.auth.ts:70-72
Now:    `const user = { orgId, role: Role.SUPER_ADMIN };` — two keys. The comment
        above it says the assignment exists "since most of the *.service.ts
        functions fetches user from request object". checkSession, by contrast,
        sets { userId, role, orgId, email }.
Impact: Any service reached through the external API that reads
        request.user.userId gets undefined. Where that lands in a Prisma `where`
        it is dropped rather than rejected — strictUndefinedChecks is not
        enabled — so a filter meant to narrow to one user silently does not,
        which is the mechanism behind issues.txt 18.x, 21.4 and 28.1. Where it
        lands in a `data` for a createdBy column it becomes a foreign-key error.
        Which of those actually happens depends on the external route, and those
        belong to their own modules. What is established here is that the
        identity is incomplete and that the comment acknowledges services expect
        it to be whole.
        A key is also not attributable: nothing records which member minted it,
        so an audit of what an API caller did cannot name a person.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "the identity it builds has no userId", "a
        valid key puts the org on the request and sends nothing", "falls back to
        the query string when there is no orgId path param"
Fix:    Store the minting user on ApiAccessToken and put it on the request.

Status: STILL OPEN — putting a userId on the identity means recording the
        minting member on the ApiAccessToken row, which is a schema change, and
        it is the same column 46.2 needs to restore a real role. Worth doing
        both at once: it also makes an API caller attributable to a person,
        which is what an audit of what a key did currently cannot say.
Note:   Minting is owner-only now (46.2), so the constant SUPER_ADMIN is at
        least no longer an escalation. The missing userId is unchanged.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[x] [46.9] LOW — FIXED — Create is not idempotent and the read cannot say "no key"
File:   src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.service.ts:17-26, :40-50
        src/modules/apiAccessToken/apiAccessToken.controller.ts:21-25
Now:    createApiKey creates without checking for an existing row, and orgId is
        @unique (prisma/schema.prisma:1802), so a second create raises P2002 and
        the handler has no catch — it becomes a 500.
        getApiKeyCreatedAt returns `apiKey?.createdAt`, and the handler sends
        `{ createdAt: date }`, so an org with no key gets `{ createdAt:
        undefined }` — serialised as `{}`.
Impact: Pressing "create" twice answers "something went wrong" rather than
        either returning the existing key's metadata or saying one already
        exists. The correct action in that state is regenerate, and nothing says
        so.
        The read gives a client no way to distinguish "no key has been minted"
        from a serialisation quirk, so the UI that decides whether to offer
        "create" or "regenerate" is deciding on an absent property. None of the
        four routes declares a schema, so nothing pins the response shape
        either.
Pinned: apiAccessToken.test.ts — "does not check whether the org already has a
        key", "a second key for the same org is refused by the unique
        constraint", "no key answers with an undefined timestamp rather than a
        404", "an org with no key reads back undefined, not null", "the
        created-at read selects only the timestamp"
Fix:    Return an explicit exists flag, and answer 409 on a duplicate create.

Fixed:  Both halves. createApiKey turns a P2002 on the unique orgId into a typed
        ApiKeyAlreadyExistsError, which the handler answers as
        `409 { error: "Use POST /regenerate to replace it" }` — so the state is
        named and so is the remedy, instead of "something went wrong". And the
        read answers `{ exists, createdAt }` with createdAt explicitly null when
        there is no key, so the UI choosing between "create" and "regenerate" is
        no longer deciding on an absent property. Create also answers 201.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOT COVERED — API ACCESS TOKEN
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - 46.1 is the important one and it is pinned only as far as this repository
    reaches: that the key is a pure function of the Math.random stream, with one
    draw per character and no other entropy. No state-recovery attack was
    attempted, and none of the estimates in the Impact section were measured.
    Whether the referral codes and a given API key come from the same generator
    state depends on the deployment topology, which is not in this repository.
  - bcrypt is real in these tests, not mocked, so 46.3's truncation is observed
    rather than argued. The cost factor's effect on request latency was not
    measured; verifyApiAccessToken pays a bcrypt comparison on every external
    API request and nothing caches it.
  - 46.2 states what the SUPER_ADMIN role grants by naming the route trees the
    hook guards. Which individual external services actually branch on
    request.user.role was not traced — those belong to src/external/v1 and to
    the support-user module, neither of which has a section yet.
  - The hook is driven directly rather than through Fastify, so 46.4's second
    reply is observed as a second reply.code(...).send(...) call on a stub.
    That Fastify raises FST_ERR_REP_ALREADY_SENT for it is documented behaviour
    of the framework, not observed here.
  - getOrgById is mocked. That it throws rather than returning null for a
    malformed uuid — the condition 46.4's catch exists for — is inferred from
    Prisma's behaviour on a @db.Uuid column, not observed.
  - The support-user webhook routes take their orgId from the query string,
    since they declare no :orgId param. Whether their handlers scope to
    request.user.orgId belongs to the whatsapp module.
  - The Prisma calls are mocked; what is pinned is the argument each function
    builds. There is no test that two organisations' rows stay separate in a
    real database.

====
APPENDIX — TEST-SUITE DEFECTS FIXED WHILE WRITING SECTION 13
================================================================================

`tsconfig.json` includes src/**/*.ts, so test files are part of `npm run build`.
Three test files from earlier passes did not typecheck, which meant the build was
broken. Fixed in place; all three still pass:

  - catalogSettings.service.test.ts referenced CatalogAddressFlowStatus.DRAFT, which
    does not exist (the enum has IDLE). At runtime that was `undefined`, so both
    assertions using it compared undefined to undefined and passed vacuously.
    Changed to IDLE, which is the state the source actually means.
  - wamessage.service.messages.test.ts called sendMediaMessages with 5-6 of its 8
    arguments. Note the source signature places a required `teamId` AFTER two
    defaulted parameters (wamessage.service.ts:1550-1559), so every caller must pass
    all eight; both real callers do.
  - enumMap.test.ts and question.utils.test.ts used test.each over two arrays of
    different enum types, which TS widened to a union that `new Set(...)` could not
    accept. Annotated as readonly string[].

================================================================================
APPENDIX — PRE-EXISTING TEST FAILURES OBSERVED WHILE FIXING SECTIONS 1 AND 2
================================================================================

Both were already failing before those fixes and neither is related to them. Left
as-is because they belong to other modules; recorded here so they are not mistaken
for fallout.

[A.1] flow.service.test.ts:309 — hard-coded Graph API version
File:   src/modules/whatsapp/flows/flow.service.test.ts:309
Now:    Asserts the upload URL is `https://graph.facebook.com/v21.0/...` while the
        code builds v25.0 from configuration. Fails on every run.
Fix:    Assert against the configured version rather than a literal, so the test
        does not have to be edited each time the Graph version moves.

[A.2] inboxautomation.test.ts:274 — flaky by a fraction of a second
File:   src/modules/whatsapp/inbox/automation/inboxautomation.test.ts:274
Now:    `expect(hoursBack).toBeGreaterThanOrEqual(24)` against a cutoff computed
        from the real clock. Observed 23.99999972222222 on one run and passing on
        the next — the elapsed time between the two Date.now() reads decides it.
Impact: Intermittent red builds with no code change behind them.
Fix:    Use fake timers, or widen the assertion to a tolerance around 24 hours
        rather than a floor.
